#5810. Federal appeals court clears Army to detonate volatile Iowa explosives as Dem gov-elect refuses to sign off

The 'Fox & Friends Weekend' panel discusses a legal battle after a court blocks the U.S. Army's plan to detonate tons of hazardous bomb-making chemicals in Iowa, sparking local fears of a toxic lead dust cloud.

A federal appeals court on Monday cleared a key legal hurdle for the U.S. Army to detonate degrading explosives in Iowa, leaving the next step uncertain after President Donald Trump said the blast would not move forward unless Gov.-elect Rob Sand also agrees to it.

Earlier Monday, Trump declared on Truth Social that he ordered the military to hold off on detonating degrading lead azide at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant unless both the Eighth Circuit Court of Appeals and Sand signed off.

"I feel the proper course is to get rid of this longterm problem but, at the same time, have ordered the Military not to do so until such time as the Eighth Circuit agrees that we can go forward, and am further making this decision fully subject to the incoming Governor Elect, Rob Sand, agreeing to the operation," Trump said in a lengthy post.

He warned that if either "do not agree," he would be forced to "hold the incoming Governor responsible for any and all death or destruction caused by this very unfortunate, and longterm, situation."

ARMY DELAYS CONTROLLED DETONATION OF UNSTABLE BOMB-MAKING CHEMICAL IN IOWA

A federal appeals court on Monday cleared a key legal hurdle for the U.S. Army to detonate degrading explosives in Iowa. (Scott Olson/Getty Images)

Hours later, the Eighth Circuit denied an emergency request from the Iowa Environmental Council and the Sierra Club to block the blast, lifting a temporary pause it issued last week.

"Here we are faced with a situation in which the risk is between a controlled detonation and an unplanned explosion, not between the risk of a controlled detonation and no explosion at all," the Eighth Circuit said.

In a Monday evening post on X, Sand declined to approve or reject the blast.

"The Administration should make this decision based on the information they have from the people they brought in to do the job months ago and have been on the ground in Middletown for weeks," Sand wrote. "Everyday Iowans are entitled to know how Middletown came to this situation, and learn why the administration believes this is the best path forward."

Sand added that he asked the federal government to publicly release the same briefing details he received "as soon as possible."

The Army had already delayed its plan to detonate the material until Wednesday, postponing the explosion expected to send "lead-containing particles" into the air.

The detonation was previously scheduled for Friday but was then rescheduled for Monday, with Acting Army Secretary Adam Telle noting in a Thursday statement, "This will delay the detonation until the latest possible moment prior to a forecasted temperature drop, which would risk further destabilization of the material."

But the Army on Sunday announced another postponement until Wednesday in order "to allow for more optimal weather conditions and all mitigation measures to be emplaced."

An environmental assessment prepared by the Army explains the reason for the plan to detonate the material in Iowa rather than relocating it.

"In March 2026, during a proactive facility safety review, IAAAP identified a stockpile of lead azide, a highly sensitive primary explosive used in munitions detonators, that had degraded and crystallized over time," the assessment noted.

IOWA RESIDENTS NEAR AMMUNITION PLANT TOLD TO VACATE BEFORE ARMY DETONATES 5 TONS OF BOMB-MAKING CHEMICALS

The Army had already delayed its plan to detonate the material until Wednesday, once more postponing the explosion expected to send "lead-containing particles" into the air. (Scott Olson/Getty Images)

"Due to its chemical degradation, the material’s legal status transitioned from a viable production component to an unserviceable hazardous waste. Lead azide in a crystallized state becomes extremely shock-and-friction sensitive presenting a serious safety hazard to plant personnel and preventing safe physical transport of the material to standard off-site demilitarization facilities," the document explains.

But the detonation will impact local communities, an advisory issued last week explained.

"Extensive modeling efforts predict that the potential level of lead particles in the air may be elevated but well below the level at which EPA would require protective actions be taken. The detonation will result in an air plume with some lead-containing particles which will disperse in the air and deposit on the ground," according to the advisory.

"While safety measures will be fully enforced, the controlled detonation will cause temporary noise, ground vibrations, and a dust plume that will contain some lead. Residents in Middletown will hear a loud noise; feel vibrations from two consecutive, rapid detonations; and see a plume of dust which is expected to linger for no more than one hour after the detonation, depending on wind speed," the notice stated.

A legal challenge was lodged last week and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit order on Thursday declared, "An administrative stay of the emergency permit issued by the Environmental Protection Agency is granted until the emergency motion for stay is ruled upon by this court."

But the U.S. government in a document on Saturday alerted the court that the Army planned to proceed with the detonation anyway.

TRUMP'S EPA REFORMS ARE A HUGE BOOST TO MANUFACTURERS, BUT THERE'S MORE RED TAPE TO CUT: EXPERT

The detonation will impact local communities, an advisory issued last week explained. (Scott Olson/Getty Images)

"The Government notifies the Court that the Department of the Army ("Army") intends to invoke the emergency munitions and explosives exclusion to EPA’s regulatory and permitting Resource Conservation and Recovery Act ("RCRA") requirements, under which the Army may perform treatment by controlled detonation of the lead azide waste without the Emergency Permit that is challenged in this petition," the filing noted. "The Army must perform a controlled detonation to protect the public from the more significant threat from an uncontrolled explosion, and the Army intends to act by November 24, 2026."

While the Army had announced on Sunday that the detonation would be delayed until Wednesday, Nov. 25, Trump, issued his Truth Social post on Monday declaring that the project would not move forward without the approval of the court and the governor-elect.

"Iowa is a very special place to me, a place that I won, by substantial margins, in all three Elections but, whether I won or lost, would never deter me from making a correct decision. As everyone knows, there is a Military ‘clean up’ operation, without which, a very dangerous situation exists. In 2009, the Obama Administration improperly stored a vast number of explosives, and the Experts, who know it well, and have been studying it for many years, feel there is a substantial chance that this material will explode of its own volition, perhaps sometime in the near future," Trump wrote in the post.

Sand and the White House could not immediately be reached by Fox News Digital for comment.

Alex Nitzberg is a writer for Fox News Digital.

#5808. America’s Hidden AI Advantage

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JEFFREY DING is Assistant Professor of Political Science at George Washington University and the author of Technology and the Rise of Great Powers: How Diffusion Shapes Economic Competition.

The United States—Not China—Dominates Diffusion

In the view of most observers, China is winning the race to diffuse artificial intelligence across the economy. Even if the United States has a small lead in frontier AI models, the consensus is that China is better at applying the technology. As Kevin Rudd, former prime minister of Australia and a prominent observer of Chinese politics, declared at a conference in October, China is “miles ahead” in spreading AI “into the real economy.” This fits a familiar narrative about the technological rivalry between the two great powers: the United States innovates while China turns those innovations into practical applications at scale.

This narrative has become central to discussions of U.S.-Chinese competition in AI. But it doesn’t match reality. After the Chinese startup DeepSeek shocked the world in early 2025 with the announcement of an open-source large language model that rivaled the capabilities of frontier models made by American companies, the international media published breathless reports on how DeepSeek’s model had rapidly spread across state-owned enterprises, hospitals, and provincial and local governments. Months later, however, analysts determined that this adoption was shallow, narrow, and slow; the technology was being used on clunky “all-in-one machines” embedded on isolated hardware units within these institutions, rather than integrated more broadly into the software services they were using. Then, in January 2026, outside observers marveled at OpenClaw, a new open-source AI agent, as a sign of China’s diffusion advantage. Now, the excitement about these open-source agents has dissipated as security vulnerabilities and inefficiency have limited their uptake.

It is the United States, not China, that has a decisive lead in AI diffusion. This is because the United States has comparative advantages in both its overall level of economic development and the nature of its growth model. China’s underdeveloped software market, weak technology transfer mechanisms, and centralized economic system make AI diffusion much more challenging. All told, the United States’ advantage in diffusing the technology through the economy is likely to persist for the foreseeable future.

Despite its reputation as a pioneer of digital technology such as cashless payments and messaging apps, China’s overall digitization levels—the extent to which software and other digital tools have penetrated various sectors of the economy—are far lower than those in the United States. American firms are on average more than twice as likely as their Chinese competitors to adopt cloud computing services. And the U.S. enterprise software market, which includes services to manage customer relationships and provide business intelligence, is approximately ten times larger than China’s. According to the 2022 Global Cloud Ecosystem Index, which ranked 76 countries on their access to and usage of cloud services, China sat in the bottom half; the United States, by contrast, ranked 17th, not far behind the top performers, in part because of its dominance of “software as a service” products in which businesses can gain access to enterprise software without needing to install and maintain the programs themselves.

China’s relatively low penetration of digital services is a barrier to diffusing AI. When small Chinese hospitals tried to adopt DeepSeek, for instance, they lacked information systems that could collect and manage the data necessary to take full advantage of AI models. In these hospitals, which include many small health facilities in rural areas, patient data is still recorded on paper; where digital records do exist, they are often incomplete or vary from hospital to hospital. By contrast, 95 percent of U.S. doctors maintain electronic health record systems in their offices. As a result, U.S. hospitals have more smoothly integrated AI into electronic health records to recommend treatments, predict health risks, and simplify billing.

China’s underdeveloped cloud and enterprise software ecosystem also explains why the OpenClaw hype did not translate to sustainable diffusion. Open-source tools like OpenClaw appeal to hobbyists and individual developers who are keen to experiment and are less sensitive to security issues. But small businesses have been slow to adopt such tools because they prefer reliable, standardized solutions that are easy to implement and update. Small businesses are more likely to rely on cloud-based tools and enterprise software to implement AI upgrades than to try to experiment on their own—two channels that are less robust in China than in the United States.

The United States also has an advantage over China because of linkages within the science and technology ecosystem that help disseminate advances. Collaboration between universities and businesses is critical to diffusing AI breakthroughs across the entire economy because these connections bring together innovators who can develop new AI products and implementers who can effectively commercialize them.

In this dimension, the United States is far ahead of China. Americans have long led the world in the publication of articles about AI co-authored by at least one researcher from academia and one researcher from industry. Between 2015 and 2019, U.S. researchers published more than twice as many such articles as their Chinese counterparts. The gap extends beyond AI: across the life sciences and natural sciences, the share of publications in the United States that involved both university and industry researchers was also more than double the comparable rate in China. Beijing recognizes this gap: China’s official state news agency ranked weak technical exchanges between industry and academia as one of the five crucial shortcomings of the country’s AI ecosystem.

Although Chinese policymakers are aware of this challenge and have promoted policies to try to spur collaboration between universities and businesses, especially in AI, it will be difficult to cultivate the requisite trust needed for these two sectors to work together. China’s weak intellectual property protections, for example, discourage universities from pursuing partnerships with industry actors because they fear losing ownership of their inventions. The United States, by contrast, has a tradition of university-industry collaboration. The 1980 Bayh-Dole Act, which provides incentives and patent protections that encourage universities to commercialize inventions and license them to the private sector, kick-started decades of collaboration. Without a similar connective tissue to link academic institutions and businesses, Chinese universities are not as equipped to form profitable partnerships with companies. Chinese technical and engineering education is also disconnected from commercial enterprises, meaning that schools do not teach students the specific skills that companies need in their workforces.

Weak technology transfer mechanisms also undermine one of China’s main strengths in AI diffusion: its massive talent base. Analysis by the Information Technology and Innovation Foundation showed that in 2022, China awarded around 50,000 doctoral degrees in science, technology, engineering, and math, compared with only about 34,000 in the United States. Although the sheer quantity of Chinese computer science graduates may eventually help the country adopt AI advances at scale, the quality of that education determines how quickly or effectively education can encourage AI diffusion. If training is divorced from practical applications, graduates will struggle to adapt cutting-edge advances from industry labs into the sectors in which they work. Consider one commonly used standard to measure the baseline quality of AI education: whether a university employs at least one researcher who has published at least one paper at a leading AI conference. According to data from 2023–24, only 38 universities in China met this standard; the United States accounted for 150.

China’s centrally planned approach to AI development is also counterproductive when it comes to diffusion. In 2025, China developed an “AI Plus” action plan that outlines targets for AI adoption over the next decade and released guidelines for government agencies and local officials on how to put the plan into effect across the country. For many observers, the mere existence of a plan showed that the highest levels of Chinese leadership were committed to spreading AI widely and that China was better positioned to win the AI race than the United States, which lacks any kind of similar diffusion blueprint. Rudd’s claims about China’s edge in AI adoption, for instance, cite Beijing’s ambitious policies and targets for AI diffusion as supporting evidence.

But centralized approaches such as China’s often impede technology diffusion, especially when the state tries to intervene in domains in which the development trajectory is uncertain. One of the planks of China’s AI Plus plan identifies “global cooperation” as a priority sector for AI adoption. If local governments were to implement the plan as outlined, it would shift resources from areas in which diffusion has been proven to be effective, such as health care, to the undefined space of “global cooperation.” More broadly, Beijing’s favoritism toward state-owned enterprises and its preference for picking national champions often prop up inefficient technologies that would have been weeded out by market-based competition. Other businesses are unlikely to adopt inefficient technologies, making nonmarket forms of AI development less likely to diffuse widely.

Counterintuitively, the lack of a clear U.S. policy on AI diffusion is a boon, not a hindrance, to the United States’ attempt to diffuse the technology across the economy. At the height of U.S.-Japanese competition in the 1980s, for instance, when the two countries were racing to train software engineers who could spread computer technology, Japan’s Ministry of Education, Science, and Culture, as it was then known, exercised centralized control over universities. But emerging trends in computer science developed faster than the government could adapt. By concentrating state funds on centers of excellence and imposing one-size-fits-all rules on the computing curriculum, Japan could not match the adaptability of American universities, and the United States surged ahead. Cross-national studies have shown that decentralized science and technology systems are correlated with faster diffusion of new innovations.

Another historical case with parallels to China, the Soviet Union, further suggests how centralization is antithetical to diffusion. In the twentieth century, the Soviet Union boasted many plans for technology development. Yet its top-down approach, which focused on large-scale engineering projects, undercut market-based instruments that encourage technology diffusion through competition. As a result, although the Soviet Union pioneered new technologies in many areas, including the casting of steel, it fell behind the United States in adopting general-purpose technologies such as computers. A prescient U.S. intelligence report published in 1969 deemed the Soviet Union’s mechanisms for technology diffusion as “much more balky” than those of the United States because of the absence of competitive pressures as well as limited linkages between the R & D system and the broader economy.

The myth of China’s lead in AI diffusion persists despite clear data showing U.S. advantages. In part, this may be because China’s innovation system has facilitated the widespread adoption of certain visible technologies. In less than 20 years, China has built more than 30,000 miles of high-speed rail, for example, blanketing the country with ultra-fast trains that now reach even the most remote provinces.

But infrastructure presents a very different challenge than AI does. The centralized pressure that officials can use to effectively develop large-scale infrastructure projects is much less effective for general-purpose technologies like AI because it is infeasible to mandate that all small- and medium-size firms, spread across every sector of the economy, adopt artificial intelligence. Those companies would do so only if it made sense from a business perspective, and few have the digital foundations or savvy employees able to harness AI in a way that would help them grow. Moreover, the United States has outbuilt China in the infrastructure most essential to AI development, data centers, in which it retains a ten-to-one edge. In fact, in some cases, the data centers that China has built remain idle because of weak AI demand.

The misperception that China leads AI diffusion may continue unabated because analysis of the country’s technology ecosystem is overly focused on frontier firms such as ByteDance and DeepSeek and first-tier cities, such as Beijing and Shanghai. Yet AI diffusion ultimately rests on whether adoption occurs in medium size companies, small towns, and inland provinces, where it has been extremely limited. Because these settings rarely feature in outside media coverage, analysis of China’s AI diffusion landscape underestimates the difficulty of translating cutting-edge advances from frontier labs and universities in Hangzhou or Shenzhen to, say, a small firm in a county seat in northwestern Qinghai Province.

Perhaps the simplest explanation is that such a myth is useful, even if ultimately untrue. In some ways, the United States is trumpeting Beijing’s AI diffusion in the way that Chinese “tiger parents” try to motivate their children to work harder by raising the success of “that other family’s kid.” “Look at how well that other family’s kid performed at the piano recital,” they might say, without listening closely to ensure that the notes were exactly right. In great-power rivalry, too, there is a natural tendency to overhype the other side’s capabilities, in part to spur more effort at home.

But such a “useful” myth can backfire if it pushes the United States to overlook its own advantages or even to mimic China’s more top-down approach to AI development. By any measure, the United States maintains a comfortable lead in AI diffusion, and China, despite its impressive successes, has serious challenges. Although the narrative of Chinese dominance endures, for now all signs point to a U.S. lead that will remain strong.

In the U.S.-Chinese Tech Race, Diffusion Matters More Than Invention

Regulating AI Will Not Set America Back in the Technology Race

Helen Toner, Jenny Xiao, and Jeffrey Ding

#5807. Trump admin announces sweeping measure to cut Iran’s ‘last financial lifeline’

Tensions escalate in the Middle East as President Donald Trump signals his willingness to resume military strikes against Iran next year following recent drone attacks. Fox News chief foreign correspondent Trey Yingst reports live from Tel Aviv on the retaliatory claims, while retired Navy SEAL officer Mike Sarraille discusses the conflict during 'The Faulkner Focus.'

FIRST ON FOX: The Treasury Department on Monday imposed sweeping new sanctions against more than two dozen Iranian exchange houses, overseas front companies and financial operatives accused of moving billions of dollars through a global "shadow banking" network to keep Iran connected to international trade.

The action, carried out by the Treasury’s Office of Foreign Assets Control (OFAC), targeted "financial plumbing" spanning Iran, Dubai, Hong Kong and Turkey that officials say enables Tehran to bypass U. S. restrictions, sell oil and petrochemicals, and settle transactions in Russian rubles and Chinese yuan.

"Iran’s economy is underwater, and its leaders are panicking," Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent wrote in a statement Monday before the news release. "Iran’s shadow banking system is the last financial lifeline of a bankrupt regime. Treasury will continue dismantling these illicit networks until we have buried the head of the snake."

TRUMP’S IRAN CRACKDOWN ‘SUFFOCATING’ REGIME AS OIL WELLS COULD SHUT WITHIN DAYS, BESSENT SAYS

Monday’s designations mark the latest escalation under Operation Economic Outcast, a maximum financial pressure campaign Bessent launched on Aug. 24, to cut off the regime's remaining revenue streams and heighten secondary sanctions risks for foreign banks and brokers that continue doing business with Tehran.

TRUMP'S FIRST IRAN WAR ESCALATION POST-MIDTERM LOSSES SEEKS TO CHOKE OFF TEHRAN’S TRADE

According to the Treasury Department, Iran's "shadow banking" apparatus is overseen by the Central Bank of Iran and relies on domestic currency exchange houses paired with bank-affiliated "rahbar companies" that manage sanctions-evasion networks through thousands of foreign shell firms. As declining oil revenues and existing sanctions squeeze the Iranian economy, Treasury said shadow banking facilitators have increasingly failed to repatriate export earnings while enriching themselves with state funds.

The Treasury also revealed Monday that its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) "identified approximately $14.7 billion of financial activity from January 2025 to August 2026 potentially linked to Iranian shadow banking."

MILLIONS OF BARRELS OF IRANIAN OIL SIT BEYOND TRUMP BLOCKADE AS HE VOWS ‘ZERO LEAKAGE’

Among the highest-profile targets in Monday’s package is the Iran-based Iran Center for Exchange of Currency and Gold, which matches Iranian exporters holding foreign currency with importers seeking to buy goods abroad across tens of billions of dollars in annual trade. Treasury noted that the Central Bank of Iran also uses bonds issued by the exchange center to facilitate cross-border trade in Russian rubles and Chinese yuan without relying on the U.S. dollar.

BESSENT WARNS IRAN'S OUTSIDE BACKERS TO 'STAY AWAY' AS TRUMP SEEKS TO 'ASPHYXIATE' TEHRAN'S ECONOMY

The OFAC package sanctions Rahbar Bazargani Nesim Baharan Qeshm Company — the rahbar affiliate for Iran’s Bank Sarmayeh — along with three of its senior officials, Sayyed Mohammad Jalali, Valiollah Hemmati and Ali Kalhor, for facilitating transactions on behalf of major Iranian oil companies. Two Iran-based currency dealers, Mina Fashki and Partners Exchange Company and Mellat Exchange Company, were blacklisted alongside five of their owners and officials.

US TARGETS CHINESE REFINERY IN SWEEPING IRAN OIL CRACKDOWN, SANCTIONS ‘SHADOW FLEET’ TANKERS

To choke off Tehran's overseas payment channels, Treasury targeted 10 exchange house front companies across the United Arab Emirates, Turkey and Hong Kong accused of moving billions of dollars for Iranian clients. Those include Dubai-based Recaz Star General Trading LLC, which sent and received hundreds of payments totaling more than $170 million tied to Sadaf Exchange, and OGA Global General Trading L. L. C, which oversaw millions of dollars in payments for the state-owned National Iranian Tanker Company.

Other newly sanctioned front companies include Hong Kong-based Grandez Trading Limited, Turkey-based Bogazici Gumrukleme Ithalat Ihracat Turizm Petrol Urunleri Sanayi Ve Ticaret Limited Sirketi and several Dubai-based trading firms — including Gespl International General Trading LLC, which Treasury alleged was used by the National Iranian Oil Company to launder oil and petrochemical proceeds, and Robust Trading LLC for processing payments for the sanctioned National Iranian Copper Industries Company.

IRANIAN ‘GHOST FLEET’ FILMED EVADING US BLOCKADE EN ROUTE TO CHINA, UANI SAYS

Treasury also invoked counterterrorism authorities to sanction Dubai-based H N B Trading Co LLC, alleging it received hundreds of thousands of dollars from a front company tied to Iran’s Armed Forces General Staff to help pay for the charter of a vessel alongside sanctioned shipping firm Dragon Road Ltd.

Under Monday's order, any U.S.-based assets or property interests belonging to the designated individuals and entities — as well as any businesses 50% or more owned by them — will be frozen, and Americans are barred from transacting with them.

Treasury also warned that foreign financial institutions risk losing access to U.S. correspondent accounts if they knowingly facilitate significant transactions for the sanctioned networks.

Whistleblowers who report sanctions violations resulting in penalties exceeding $1 million might ultimately qualify for federal awards.

Eric Mack is a breaking news reporter and writer Sunday through Thursday, 6 a.m. ET to 2 p.m. ET, with a particular interest in stories that lead the news cycle on politics, elections, foreign affairs and government.

#5805. The Democratic Party’s Three Conflicting Camps on AI

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: The Democratic Party’s Three Conflicting Camps on AI

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

One way to read the tea leaves on the recent U.S. midterms is that, at long last, Washington gets serious about regulating artificial intelligence; the unchecked build-out of those wildly unpopular data centers is reconsidered; Big Tech is held to account; and the United States becomes not just the most advanced AI power but also the more trustworthy AI power. But if you were expecting that the electoral “techlash” would lead to coherent AI governance, policy, and statecraft, then prepare to be disappointed. The more likely outcome is that we shall live with two more years of muddied U.S. technology policy at home and abroad, with one clear winner: Beijing.

For while Democrats did benefit from the promise of checking AI growth, and they now own the legislative machinery, the party must balance three competing impulses: to rein in AI, to ride the AI-led economic momentum and avoid economy-wide slowdowns before the 2028 elections, and to avoid ceding the AI leadership to China.

One way to read the tea leaves on the recent U.S. midterms is that, at long last, Washington gets serious about regulating artificial intelligence; the unchecked build-out of those wildly unpopular data centers is reconsidered; Big Tech is held to account; and the United States becomes not just the most advanced AI power but also the more trustworthy AI power. But if you were expecting that the electoral “techlash” would lead to coherent AI governance, policy, and statecraft, then prepare to be disappointed. The more likely outcome is that we shall live with two more years of muddied U.S. technology policy at home and abroad, with one clear winner: Beijing.

For while Democrats did benefit from the promise of checking AI growth, and they now own the legislative machinery, the party must balance three competing impulses: to rein in AI, to ride the AI-led economic momentum and avoid economy-wide slowdowns before the 2028 elections, and to avoid ceding the AI leadership to China.

To make gnarly matters more tangled, U.S. AI’s central player, Nvidia, has lost its co-founder and CEO over an alleged Chinese intelligence operation, scrambling all three impulses. And we should also remember that over at the White House, a bruised but belligerent Trump retains the executive levers and the veto—as well as an impulse to stymie whatever the Democrats wish to demonstrate as a “win” to the American public.

Before we get to Trump, though, let’s first consider what drives each of the camps in the Democratic coalition.

The first camp can be thought of as the Restrainers. They want to check the proliferation of data centers while holding AI executives to account for independent testing of advanced models—and holding the companies liable for harm.

Midterm voters rewarded candidates in this camp, who pushed back on a sense that AI development was proceeding unchecked; Sherrod Brown won back an Ohio Senate seat with ads calling his opponent “the face of AI data centers,” while Jon Ossoff in Georgia and Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan also won races with opposition to data centers as a key element of their campaign platforms. In nine states, governors and governors-elect, too, have promised or imposed a pause on new data centers, stalling an estimated 41 percent of the country’s data center pipeline. Meanwhile, right after the elections, veteran Sen. Bernie Sanders called for a national data center moratorium.

While slowing down AI would be the most readily available and popular move by lawmakers, the Restrainers still have to get their house in order. They will need to prioritize the many risks and concerns around AI and focus their limited time on the most significant ones. Weighing these will not be easy. A household worried about its power bill, for instance, needs a different intervention compared with a parent who doesn’t want their child left alone with a chatbot.

A second camp is made up of the Riders. They have their eye on the economy and want to ensure that AI investments keep carrying U.S. growth, the stock market, and household retirement savings. AI has propped up the American economy in recent years; letting the air out of that balloon could produce a bust, a recession, and shrinking retirement accounts, which could be bad news come 2028 if voters lay the blame on Congress.

The sharp Nov. 5 reminder about the connection between AI growth and stock market growth is hard to ignore—right after the midterms results had sunk in, the S&P 500 stock index fell 4.4 percent, its worst day since the April 2025 tariff shock, led by stocks tied to the data center build-out, while the Nasdaq also had its deepest one-day drop in 19 months, with investors worried about tougher AI rules from a Democratic Congress.

The Riders will urge caution: hold the hearings, pass disclosure rules, but don’t touch the capital spending contributing to the economy and the stock market. Several moderate Democrats, such as Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro or Kentucky Gov. Andy Beshear, who are also presumed to be business-friendly, are considering presidential runs. They are likely to be the influential voices in the Riders camp.

As for the third camp, let’s call them the Racers. These are Democrats who prioritize national security concerns and want to keep sensitive technology and advanced chips away from adversaries. Specifically, they are eager to ensure that China not win the AI race. The Racers have support from the likes of influential senators such as Elizabeth Warren and Democratic leader Chuck Schumer as well as post-midterms in-the-spotlight senators including James Talarico.

While the AI race against China was not the issue that galvanized voters in the midterms and swept Democrats into Congress, a startling new development has the potential to make this a more pivotal political lever: the scandal involving Jensen Huang, who admitted to an affair with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy. This puts a spotlight on the extent to which China is keen to compete and centers the geopolitical stakes of the AI race in voters’ minds.

Huang’s abrupt exit from the company that he co-founded puts wind at the backs of the Racers, especially with curbing chip sales to China becoming a rare cause with bipartisan support. But it also pushes the other camps to revise their agendas.

The Restrainers’ scrutiny of Big Tech’s leadership has national security implications. They will by now have realized that Nvidia did not have so much as a board chair until the day that Huang left—hardly a case for letting the industry police itself. For the Riders, with Nvidia as the load-bearer for the AI edifice, instability at the top could produce instability across the economy. They can point to half a trillion dollars of Nvidia’s market value being lost within a few days of the scandal breaking and the board investigation beginning.

A Democratic coalition made up of these camps necessarily has a mix of divergent priorities. But while the legislative agenda will be set by political calculation, Congress can only guess at what a lame-duck president who controls the agencies, the veto, the export licenses, and his own stake in the AI trade will do in response.

We have just had a preview: Even as a bipartisan chip bill is in process on Capitol Hill, the White House moved first, issuing its own rule to choke off China’s access to U.S. chips through data centers abroad.

The public will vote again in two years, too short for politicians and too long for technologists. With all these conflicting forces in motion, I would argue that Congress needs to allocate its limited bandwidth to a prioritized short list of AI risks, such as those recorded in my own register of 103 documented cases.

In the absence of a reconciliation across the divergent camps, the result could be a U.S. AI build-out that advances in fits and starts as deals get negotiated, an export regime that can swing with each hearing, a standoff between legislators on Capitol Hill and the White House, and allies who hedge because they cannot predict U.S. policy.

The Chinese AI industry would be handed the perfect case for promoting itself to the world as the comparable, trustworthy, and cheaper alternative. And it has managed to get to this point even without access to the most advanced chips that Huang himself had wanted to sell to them.

Meanwhile, to win, Beijing will not need a newly ascendant Democratic Party to pick the wrong camp. It only needs Washington to pick none.

Bhaskar Chakravorti is the dean of global business at Tufts University’s Fletcher School of Law and Diplomacy. He is the founding executive director of Fletcher’s Institute for Business in the Global Context, where he established and chairs the Digital Planet research program. His next book Defeating Disinformation (co-edited with Joel Trachtman) will be published by Cambridge University Press in January 2025.

#5804. The Model Middle Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW YEO is a Senior Fellow and SK-Korea Foundation Chair at the Brookings Institution and Professor of Politics at the Catholic University of America. This essay is adapted from his forthcoming book South Korea’s Grand Strategy: Narratives and the Rise of a Global Nation.

What South Korea Gets Right About Geopolitics

In the ten months since Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney gave his widely discussed speech at the World Economic Forum in Davos, the question of whether, and how, the world’s middle powers can help rebuild a broken international order has become increasingly urgent. In his remarks, Carney stressed that although the old U.S.-led alliance system was collapsing, middle powers—states that do not have the ability to reshape global politics but that still possess substantial might—were well positioned to pick up the slack. But to do so, Carney argued, they would need to build up their “strength at home” and “act together” to harness their collective power, even in the absence of backing from the world’s hegemon.

Although the Canadian prime minister’s bold vision was widely applauded, some experts thought his agenda was overly ambitious. They noted, correctly, that it would be very difficult for middle powers to build a grand alliance composed of countries as diverse as Brazil, Canada, South Africa, and Turkey. “The path to unity among middle powers is fraught because this group includes rich and poor countries, as well as small and large ones,” wrote the economist Eswar Prasad in February. Carney, however, is right that middle powers have an opportunity to thrive going forward. Even when aligning with great powers, these countries can still exercise greater autonomy by developing national capacity and diversifying external partnerships.

South Korea offers a prime example of this flexible approach. For decades, Seoul has pursued a middle-power strategy centered on building domestic strength and forging partnerships with democracies everywhere while deepening its alliance with Washington. South Korea has been adaptable, recalibrating its security alignments, economic strategies, and diplomatic roles as regional and global conditions require. This has enabled a country smaller in size than most of the world’s states to punch far above its weight, exercising substantial influence in Asia and beyond.

Not every government can follow Seoul’s path, and South Korea’s strategy has its limits. As Seoul itself acknowledges, middle powers cannot escape great-power competition. But the country has begun to show what middle powers can accomplish when they leverage their partnerships and their own strength to protect their interests and deter larger threats. And by maintaining and even building its ties to the United States while asserting greater autonomy, South Korea has proven that middle powers can strengthen themselves without building whole new networks—contrary to Carney’s suggestion.

According to classic international relations theory, grand strategy is the exclusive provenance of great powers. The United States’s effort to contain the Soviet Union during the Cold War is a paradigmatic example; China’s pursuit of global economic primacy is another. Middle powers, by contrast, have little say in such strategies. Instead, these countries are seen as primarily reactive in their foreign policies, navigating around the decisions of the great powers rather than setting the agenda themselves.

But recent history suggests that the division between great and middle powers is less clear-cut than has been assumed. In practice, middle powers very much have their own affirmative visions that they pursue in the world. They have the leeway to decide which threats matter most to them, which partnerships to prioritize, and where to invest scarce resources. Japan, concerned with the threat posed by China, has been quietly exercising leadership in the Indo-Pacific by improving its ties with neighbors, increasing investment and aid to the region, and strengthening its own defense capabilities—all while remaining closely allied with the United States. India, worried about China, Pakistan, and growing economic headwinds, has successfully bolstered partnerships with the United States and other democracies but maintained its relationship with Russia.

Much like Japan and India, South Korea is worried about its neighbors. But for Seoul, this is nothing new. South Korean leaders have been concerned with the very thing Carney fretted about in his Davos speech—becoming collateral damage in conflicts between great powers— since at least the late nineteenth century, when it was caught between China, Japan, and Russia. The country has a well-known proverb, “When whales fight, the shrimp's back is broken,” that captures the sentiment that great-power feuds can harm countries caught in the middle.

To safeguard against this threat, Seoul has long sought to increase its self-reliance through industrial policy. The country transited from light to heavy export industries in the 1970s, turning it into a major player in the chemical, steel, and shipbuilding sectors. State investments helped it become a major global producer of batteries and semiconductors in the present era. South Korea takes great pride in what historians call “the miracle on the Han”—or the country's rapid rise from a war-torn and impoverished state to a rich one during the late twentieth century. They now use the state’s wealth to promote public goods globally, contributing to international development, global health, climate policy, and multilateral bodies.

Seoul has also built up a formidable military, one that experts consistently rank as among the strongest in the world. Today, many South Korean analysts argue that Seoul needs a nuclear arsenal for self-defense, even though the country officially remains under Washington’s nuclear umbrella. The state has simultaneously used its armed prowess to deepen relations with U.S. allies. Seoul, for example, has inked multibillion-dollar arms deals with European buyers, making it a key partner in rebuilding NATO’s industrial capacity. In 2025, it was the second largest supplier of weapons to European NATO members (after the United States). South Korea has also forged defense partnerships in the Middle East and Southeast Asia, widening Seoul's options and reducing its dependence on any single market. South Korea, in other words, has been able to convert its economic resources and bureaucratic capabilities into greater influence by expanding its partnerships, strengthening its voice in international bodies, and helping shape global rules and norms.

But even as it charts its own path, Seoul has no plans to abandon the United States, at least in the near future. In fact, the sanctity of the U.S.-South Korean relationship is a significant historical Korean narrative. Many South Koreans see the alliance as a bond inked in blood, thanks to the sacrifice both countries made during the Korean War, when they worked to stop North Korea, backed by China and the Soviet Union, from overrunning the south. Most South Koreans correctly believe that the continued U.S. military presence in the country—currently, 28,500 American troops are stationed on the peninsula—has played a major part in deterring North Korean aggression over the past seven decades.

At first glance, South Korea’s strategy may seem contradictory. On the one hand, the country wants to deepen its cooperation with the United States, on which it still depends for security. But on the other, it seeks greater autonomy.

Yet these twin goals are not actually at odds. By cultivating ties with Washington, Seoul has gained access to advanced military capabilities and technologies as well as a larger diplomatic platform to expand its regional and global interests. It is in part because of this relationship, for example, that South Korea has had such success at selling weapons to multiple NATO members, including Croatia, Estonia, Finland, Norway, Poland, and Romania. (Seoul has also built up a formidable diplomatic apparatus in its own right.) At the same time, South Korea helps make this compact worthwhile for the United States by providing it with valuable technology, including semiconductor chips. Seoul has also partnered with the Washington to help revive the American shipbuilding industry, agreeing to invest $150 billion in the U.S. sector.

Still, South Korea’s growing push for more independence in its foreign policy does result in some tensions with the United States that cannot easily be resolved. For instance, the current government’s push to give Seoul, not Washington, operational control over both states’ forces on the peninsula in the event of war has been rebuffed by the U.S. military. American commanders instead favor a conditions-based transfer after South Korea has proven it has the capabilities to lead a joint defense, rather than setting a fixed date. The two sides also have not reached a consensus on what role South Korea would play if Taiwan is attacked or blockaded by China.

Yet so far, Seoul has done an effective job at managing these differences. For example, in his second term, U.S. President Donald Trump has repeatedly accused Washington’s allies, including South Korea, of leeching off the United States and not paying their fair share. To extract more from Washington’s partners, Trump has implemented punishing tariffs. In response, South Korea could have been defiant and slapped on retaliatory restrictions, as other U.S. allies did. Instead, South Korean President Lee Jae-myung avoided rebuking the president and worked to meet American demands in ways that heightened the two states’ mutual dependence. For instance, Lee pledged to invest $350 billion in the United States to advance American economic and national security interests, including by purchasing more U.S. military equipment, and to increase South Korean economic support for locally stationed American forces.

Seoul’s strategy has not been a uniform success. The South Korean economy remains exposed to American and Chinese pressure despite its growth and diversification. North Korea is expanding its nuclear and missile forces and deepening military ties with Russia, creating new security headaches for which Seoul still needs U.S. assistance. The country is politically polarized, resulting in sharp policy shifts that can undermine long-term strategic planning. It also suffers from the lowest birthrate of any country in the world, threatening its tax base and the size of its armed forces. Beyond that, South Korea may not provide the ideal template for every middle power. Not every country in this category has similar economic resources, networks, or ties to the United States.

But on the whole, South Korea’s grand strategy has been effective, and it thus carries broader lessons. For one, it shows how adaptive middle powers can draw on their respective national narratives to find their strengths and to chart a path forward. Perhaps more important, it is proof positive that middle powers can exercise influence and achieve a measure of autonomy even without working together to build a neutral third pole, as Carney urged. It also illustrates that middle powers can remain firmly allied with the United States while developing their own independent capabilities. South Korea, then, demonstrates that states can reduce their vulnerability and widen their political options.

An earlier version of this article incorrectly stated that the South Korean government was known to be considering developing nuclear weapons.

With Its Allies, Seoul Has Enough Leverage to Push Back

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5801. Is It Time for Kyiv to Compromise?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Q&A: Is It Time for Kyiv to Compromise?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Understanding the conflict four years in.

Ukrainian parliamentarian Oleksiy Goncharenko is deeply divisive in his country.

In October, he was expelled from his parliamentary faction over a statement advocating for peace negotiations, in which he wrote that Ukraine “cannot defeat Russia.” He proposed several possible compromises with Russia that are viewed as politically toxic in Ukraine, including legal protections for the Russian language. Critics slammed the plan as being based on a false premise that Russia is interested in anything less than Ukraine’s surrender.

Ukrainian parliamentarian Oleksiy Goncharenko is deeply divisive in his country.

In October, he was expelled from his parliamentary faction over a statement advocating for peace negotiations, in which he wrote that Ukraine “cannot defeat Russia.” He proposed several possible compromises with Russia that are viewed as politically toxic in Ukraine, including legal protections for the Russian language. Critics slammed the plan as being based on a false premise that Russia is interested in anything less than Ukraine’s surrender.

Goncharenko’s political message may also be increasingly attractive to some Ukrainians as they face yet another hard winter under Russian attack, an increasingly unstable economy, and social tension over the military draft, a topic on which he is also vocal. And the success of a recent cease-fire in the Ukrainian town of Oleshky looks a lot like what Goncharenko proposed as a potential model for negotiations.

Foreign Policy spoke to Goncharenko for a sit-down interview in Kyiv on Nov. 10—right as U.S.-mediated talks between Russia and Ukraine were taking place in Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates. He discussed his peace plan, the pushback that it received, and whether he thinks Ukrainians are ready to accept some compromises if it means ending the war.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Foreign Policy: What do you think about U.S. President Donald Trump’s approach to peace negotiations?

Oleksiy Goncharenko: I think it’s the only one which exists. It’s difficult to criticize this or that because it is the only one. For the moment, there is no result. Nearly a month ago, I presented my peace plan. I think there are two problems. First is that we are trying to achieve a big deal, and now an even bigger deal. Now, they’re saying we are not speaking just about Ukraine; we’re speaking about the security architecture of Europe. So with time, it’s bigger and bigger and bigger.

But when you have such big things on the table, it’s quite hard to find any compromise solution. So my idea is, OK, if we can’t make a big deal now, a big and beautiful deal, let’s make a small, maybe not so beautiful, humble but realistic deal, like Oleshky, like the grain corridor.

And the second thing which is problematic is to have only one negotiator, which is the United States. With all respect, I want to see the United States at the table. But why can’t we have smaller negotiations on the Black Sea with Turkey and Egypt or India? Why is Brazil not involved? Why is the Vatican not involved?

FP: Why did you decide to come up with that proposal at this specific time?

AG: Every day of war gives us nothing except more suffering, and our position unfortunately is weakening every day. And what we could achieve in 2022, we couldn’t achieve in 2023, and the same [in] ’24, ’25, now ’26, and soon it will be ’27. So for a long time, I was pushing this agenda.

But when I was saying this, often I was asked, “OK, but what is your plan? How do you see this?” So I decided to answer this question. I’m not saying this is a perfect plan, but at least I try to propose some concrete steps, which I think can be done.

Because also what I see is that we have this escalation spiral. So it’s constant escalation, more and more. Half a year ago, vessels were moving in the Black Sea. The bridges were not attacked. Warehouses were OK, and so on and so on. Now every month, it’s worse and worse and worse.

So the idea was, “OK, let us try to have some de-escalation.” Our president [Volodymyr Zelensky], because he was an actor, understands the feelings of his audience very well. I think that it’s important for him to feel that there is a demand for a peace solution, and when he feels it, I hope maybe that will push him to be more active in this.

Definitely, there is no peace today because Russia doesn’t want it. But at the same time, I think Ukraine could do more too.

FP: What do you think about the reaction to the peace plan that you put forward?

AG: There were a lot of attacks against me, saying that I am a Russian agent, I am a MAGA agent, I am Trump’s agent. So it showed that part of society—a small part of society, but with active and loud voices—they don’t want any negotiations.

And that’s also [a problem]. Let us imagine that today or tomorrow in Abu Dhabi, they come out and say, “We have a deal.” [Russian President Vladimir] Putin is a dictator, so if he will agree, Russia will agree. But Ukraine is not a dictatorship. So they can come [out] with this decision, and part of society can say, “Fuck you.”

I think it’s a big problem that before my plan, we didn’t even have a discussion in the country about what is acceptable, what is not. The old idea was that there will be a complete surrender of Russia, which is unfortunately not realistic today. We are living like in this film, Don’t Look Up.

FP: What percent of Ukrainians do you think would be ready for negotiation?

AG: I think in society, it’s a big majority who are completely exhausted with the war, and they’re ready. But again, ready for what? So not for anything, not for leaving [the Donbas city of] Kramatorsk, for example. I think this is very unacceptable for the majority. But for some compromises about NATO, something else about freezing of the current line and so on, about softening of sanctions, if it’s the price of peace.

FP: Do you think there’s a difference between the maximalist aims Russia has stated publicly and what Moscow might be willing to agree to privately?

AG: It’s negotiation tactics. I’m absolutely sure that Russians are ready. One of the counterarguments against my plan was that all this is bullshit because Russians don’t want anything. It’s quite a strange argument. Because, OK, if they don’t want anything, and all I proposed will lead us to nothing, what will we lose?

Because I do not propose to stop the war, to stop fighting. I am saying that the only reason why we can have some negotiations with Russia is because the Ukrainian army is still successful. Because if it is not successful, there will be no negotiations at all.

The Ukrainian army is winning time—but for what? So this is our job, to at least try. And Oleshky and the grain corridor in 2022 and the Black Sea agreement showed that there is a possibility to have some deals with Russia. The idea that you can’t have any deal with Russia means the war will be eternal. But there were no eternal wars.

FP: Do you see yourself as having any other political allies?

AG: There are some others. But all of them are just speaking a little bit about what I was speaking about for more than a year. They don’t show any way to do this. They don’t discuss any compromises because it’s considered that to discuss any compromises is very toxic, and nobody wants to pay this price.

It’s interesting, because in the parliament, so many of my colleagues made statements that I am heavily mistaken. But in the parliament, in the hall, not one of them came to me saying, “OK, you’re not right.” Many of my colleagues came to me saying, “You are absolutely right,” or “you are mostly right.” But it’s very toxic. It’s dangerous.

AG: I don’t think in the physical sense, but in the political sense.

Listen, I can understand. From a psychological point of view, Ukrainian society is very traumatized. It’s clear why. The problem is that all this hate and negative energy, which is in society, you can’t channel it to Russians, unfortunately.

Soldiers at least can kill Russians, but people here can’t do anything. If you can’t channel your hatred to some enemies, you will start to look for these enemies [at home]. So, definitely, it is a very sensitive topic. Some people lost their children. Some people lost their homes. Some people lost their towns and cities.

FP: Would you create your own political party?

AG: It’s very possible that I will start my own party, but not right now, because there are no elections, unfortunately.

FP: Who do you think your electorate would be?

AG: I think the majority of Ukrainians who are patriots, but who are who are realistic.

FP: I’ve talked to people who say any open discussion of negotiations provides room for Russian propaganda. What do you think about that?

AG: I think it’s crazy. Unfortunately, I mean, if the Ukrainian army would advance now to Moscow, maybe we will be in a different situation from where we are. [But] this war will end with negotiations with Russia. And these negotiations will happen either with Ukraine or without. Without, it’s a much worse option.

Like, look at sanctions [on Russia]. Sanctions are easing. And it’s not just American diesel, but [also] British kerosene at the beginning of the year, [French] President [Emmanuel] Macron removing sanctions on [oligarchs] Alisher Usmanov and [Mikhail] Fridman, or Estonia [lifting sanctions] on some Russian firms, and so on. So sanctions are easing, but without anything for Ukraine.

Ukraine is absent there. It’s always like this: Either you are at the table, or you are on the menu. So that’s it. We don’t have any other option. So it’s crazy just to say, “Oh, we can’t have negotiations with Russia because Russians are bastards.” Yes, they are bastards. So what?

Sam Skove is a staff writer at Foreign Policy. X: @samuelskove

#5799. The Only Way to End the Netanyahu Era

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

AYMAN ODEH served in the Knesset from 2015 to 2026. He is the former leader of the Joint List and the Hadash party.

Arab Citizens of Israel Can Bring Change, but They Can’t Do It Alone

As the Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot struggles to assemble a governing coalition following last month’s elections, Israel finds itself in a familiar political predicament. The parties opposed to Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his far-right coalition command a clear majority in the Knesset, with 67 of its 120 seats. Yet the four Jewish-led parties in Eisenkot’s camp hold just 48 seats, leaving them unable to form a government on their own. The impasse echoes the prolonged political deadlock between 2019 and 2021, when Netanyahu’s supporters and his opponents remained locked in competing parliamentary blocs, and four consecutive elections failed to produce a stable governing majority.

But one thing is different this time. Israel’s Arab-led parties, the Joint List and Raam, are stronger than ever. Powered by extraordinary turnout among Arab citizens of Israel, they won a record 19 seats in the Knesset: 11 for the Joint List and eight for Raam. This political mobilization is important in itself, demonstrating Arab voters’ overwhelming desire for change and their recognition of the importance of participating in elections. But its significance goes far beyond the number of seats they have won. Together, the Arab parties now have the potential to be a decisive force in an anti-Netanyahu coalition, providing the parliamentary backing needed to form a government. The proof of this power has already been shown: on November 17, Arab support was crucial in the vote to replace the Likud speaker of the Knesset with a speaker from Eisenkot’s party. Now they have a historic opportunity to put the country on a different path.

As the first and former leader of the Joint List, I know how much is at stake. In 2019, I helped lead a groundbreaking effort to give Arab-led parties a direct voice in the Israeli political system. We took an enormous political risk by endorsing Benny Gantz, then a former chief of staff of the Israeli military and Netanyahu’s principal rival, as a candidate to form a government. We did so despite our profound political and ideological differences with him. Although that effort ultimately fell short, with Gantz choosing to pursue a “unity government” with Netanyahu instead, it established an important precedent: by showing a willingness to work with Jewish political forces with whom they had deep disagreements, parties representing the Arab public had a real chance to prevent a Netanyahu-led far-right government from holding power.

Today, after nearly four years of disastrous leadership by Netanyahu and his far-right allies, that possibility has become far more urgent. It will take political courage on both sides. Eisenkot must be able to offer something to the Arab-led parties, and the Arab-led parties will need to be comfortable offering support to a government that they may not agree with on many issues. But the remarkable Arab turnout and the sizable Israeli majority that is now against Netanyahu have pointed to a way forward. Not only Israel’s future but also its place in the region and the world may depend on the willingness of its leaders to engage with the country’s Palestinian minority. Ultimately, such a partnership will be essential to building the trust between Jews and Arabs that is a prerequisite to achieving any real progress toward a viable Israeli-Palestinian peace. This is the real choice now facing Israel: whether Israelis are prepared to replace the politics of supremacy and exclusion with a politics of equality.

The factors that drove record numbers of Palestinian citizens of Israel to the polls in October have been years in the making. Although the country’s Arab citizens make up more than 20 percent of the Israeli electorate and have long made vital contributions to Israeli society as doctors, lawyers, engineers, high-tech professionals, and in many other fields, the Israeli right has for decades demonized them as enemies: a fifth column and a threat to the state. In recent years, this hostility has increasingly manifested itself in harassment and open racism at universities and workplaces, in the media, in the parliament, and in everyday life.

Yet it would be a mistake to assume that Arab exclusion has been driven only by Netanyahu and the radical right. Zionist parties of the center and the left have also contributed, by keeping a distance from Arab voters or simply ignoring them. The belief that Jewish citizens should ultimately determine the country’s political direction while Arab citizens must remain within whatever limits have been set for them runs deep in Israeli politics. Netanyahu understood early on how to turn this contradiction into a political strategy. Since the 1990s, he has repeatedly used the same formula: inciting his supporters against Arab citizens, delegitimizing Arab representatives in parliament, and convincing Jewish Israelis that a government that relies on Arab political support would somehow be illegitimate.

These tactics proved to be among Netanyahu’s most effective political tools, especially given that Arab-led parties have long been some of his biggest foes. He didn’t need to convince everyone to support his Likud party or its various coalition partners. He only needed to convince enough people, including Jewish Israelis who opposed him, that working with Arab-led parties was dangerous and unacceptable. Netanyahu’s strategy also helped suppress Arab turnout. Since the Arab-led parties had little chance of changing the status quo, Arab voters had little incentive to vote: for many years, a significantly lower proportion of Arab citizens went to the polls than did their Jewish counterparts.

That began to change in 2015 with the creation of the Joint List, which for the first time brought together four parties representing the Arab public on a single electoral ticket and which became the third-largest faction in the Knesset. By embracing a broader vision for our society, grounded in Jewish-Arab solidarity and opposition to the racism of the right, the Joint List demonstrated that Palestinian citizens of Israel could play a new and more influential role in the country’s political system. Then, under my leadership in 2019, the Joint List decided to back Gantz’s bid to unseat Netanyahu. The decision was highly controversial, both within the Arab electorate and across Israeli society. Many Arab voters were deeply ambivalent about supporting a potential government led by a former army chief whose political positions, particularly on the status of Palestinian citizens of Israel and the occupation, differed sharply from their own. Nevertheless, we were prepared to take that political risk because we understood that defeating Netanyahu was more important.

In practice, our willingness to cooperate with the Zionist opposition was not, in fact, reciprocated. The Jewish opposition parties wanted our votes but could not bring themselves to accept us as equal political partners. And, ultimately, Gantz decided to form a government with Netanyahu instead. Nevertheless, the experience showed that Jewish-Arab political cooperation could open up new horizons for a large segment of the public. Since then, every time that Arab-led parties have recommended or supported the effort of a Jewish candidate to form a government, or whenever the Israeli center-left has depended on their support, additional segments of Israeli society have become more open to this kind of cooperation.

In 2021–22, an Arab party, Raam, joined the short-lived government led by Naftali Bennett and Yair Lapid. I had serious reservations about the model of partnership that emerged, which seemed to rest on the expectation that Arab political representatives would limit their involvement to civilian and domestic matters, while refraining from addressing political and national questions, particularly the occupation and the Palestinian issue. I reject this distinction. We are part of the Palestinian people, and our identity and political concerns cannot be separated from our citizenship in Israel.

But these kinds of slow and painful steps are how political change often happens in national struggles. It does not always begin with a moral or democratic awakening. Sometimes, it begins with pragmatic necessity. When there is no other option, people begin to reconsider what had once been seen as impossible. In the current election, that dynamic has become even more important. After the unending crisis of the past four years, many Israeli voters, including both Arabs and Jews as well as the leaders of most of the Zionist opposition parties, have concluded that getting rid of the Netanyahu government is more necessary than ever.

In recent past elections, voter turnout among Palestinian citizens of Israel has generally remained between 50 and 60 percent. What explains the unprecedented 20-percentage-point surge in turnout in the October election? Three developments stand out: the war of annihilation in Gaza and the killing of tens of thousands of Palestinians; the daily racism and hostility that Arab citizens now face in the streets, in public spaces, and in virtually every aspect of their lives; and the crime that has spiraled out of control in Arab communities under the national security minister, Itamar Ben-Gvir, whose policies have allowed it to flourish, and the relentless settler violence against Palestinians in the West Bank enabled by Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich. All the grief and frustration accumulated over these years were brought to the ballot box.

But there was another crucial factor as well. In the run-up to the election, Arab citizens, following the nearly even polls between Eisenkot and Netanyahu, could see in black and white just how much the outcome would depend on them. Voting this time would be more than a political choice. It was for many a personal responsibility and a way of taking control of their own future. That is why hundreds of thousands of Palestinian citizens of Israel made their way to the polls, defying years of efforts to silence them and undermine their political agency. Many understood that the future direction of the country might be quite literally in their hands.

I saw elderly people, like my friend Jumah from Jisr al-Zarqa, taking to the streets and campaigning for the Joint List. Many of them had lived through the 1948 nakba, or “catastrophe,” which accompanied Israel’s establishment and involved terrible massacres, the destruction of hundreds of Palestinian villages and towns, and the displacement of around 750,000 Palestinians. I saw young people debating politics in the cafes and restaurants of my beloved Haifa, persuading their friends to vote. I saw women and men from Nazareth, Umm al-Fahm, Sakhnin, Rahat, Akko, Kafr Yasif, Majd al-Krum, and countless other towns and villages united by a shared sense of purpose. I saw political consciousness become a living, collective force. For a moment, the boundaries between generations and communities seemed to disappear.

The result marks a remarkable advance of the process of Arab participation in the Israeli political system that began in 2019. In this election, Arab citizens of Israel turned out in record numbers, dramatically narrowing the historical turnout gap with Jewish voters. And that has made another political reality increasingly clear: Israel’s liberal and centrist camp cannot form a government without the parties that represent the Arab public. If it excludes them, as well as the ultra-Orthodox and the far right, it will not reach the 61 seats needed. This is the demographic reality. Israeli Jews who oppose Netanyahu must decide whom they are willing to build a political future with.

The debate about what Eisenkot should do and whether he should ask for Arab support is now unfolding beyond the Knesset, in people’s homes, cafés, in universities, and on the street. It is a deeply controversial question, and many Israelis disagree strenuously about it. Yet for the Arab-led parties, gaining sufficient importance to be able to provoke these conversations is an achievement. The fact that this debate is even taking place shows how the political process has caused Israelis to consider possibilities that until recently seemed unimaginable.

In the end, Eisenkot may have few other options if he wants to replace Netanyahu, as he has promised. In most cases, political parties support coalitions to advance positive changes and achieve concrete goals. But there are moments when political cooperation is necessary for a different reason: to prevent something far worse. The most immediate and overriding objective today is to bring an end to the far-right Netanyahu government. That is a priority on which both the Zionist opposition and the Arab-led parties can agree.

Beyond this shared basic goal, the Arab-led parties should use their political influence to work on two levels. First, they must seek tangible improvements in their constituents’ lives: combating organized crime in Arab communities, securing budgets, addressing the housing crisis, ending the brutal treatment of Palestinians living under occupation in the West Bank, and pushing for a permanent cease-fire and the reconstruction of Gaza. These are fundamental issues of human dignity and civilian life. On a broader level, they should continue to push for real progress on Israeli-Palestinian peace that is ultimately based on two states coexisting, side by side.

I do not expect the current Israeli political establishment to embrace these positions, but that does not mean that the Arab parties should not raise them at every opportunity. At the same time, the Arab parties can use their newfound political clout to build alliances between Jews and Arabs around shared political and strategic goals. The resulting partnerships must be about more than parliamentary arrangements; it must also create the foundations for a different future. That will require Jewish and Arab citizens of Israel to engage with one another on the basis of equality, rather than mere political convenience.

Thus far, Eisenkot has given few indications that he is prepared to go down this path. Since the election, it has been encouraging to see him condemn Netanyahu for fueling what he described as a “racist and violent” campaign against Israel’s Arab citizens. Yet many of the mainstream Zionist parties in Eisenkot’s camp have vowed not to work with the Arab parties. Still, Eisenkot must consider what that would mean. Thirty years from now, people will ask how Jewish supremacy became so normalized that even politicians who called themselves democrats accepted the exclusion of one-fifth of their fellow citizens. They will ask why Israel’s opposition could recognize the danger posed by Netanyahu’s government but not confront the assumptions that made his politics possible.

There is an irony in the fact that, in a country that seeks to keep political hegemony exclusively in Jewish hands, the Palestinian minority, after decades of struggle for political recognition and equal rights, has emerged as one of the most significant forces shaping the country’s political future. We have come to understand that we must be courageous and cooperate with the Zionist parties of the center and left in order to oust Netanyahu, Ben-Gvir, and Smotrich. The question now is: Do these parties, which call themselves democratic and liberal, have the same courage?

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

A Failing Peace Process Leads to Only One Outcome

Conflicts in the Middle East and the Horn of Africa Have Become Inseparable

Why a New Majority—Even in Gaza—Supports Compromise With Israel

Michael Robbins, Amaney A. Jamal, and Mark Tessler

The Netanyahu Doctrine Could Outlast Netanyahu

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America
