#5778. Nvidia Is Now Too Big to Hold to Account

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Nvidia Is Now Too Big to Hold to Account

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Put out of mind the scenes of cheering you may have seen of Jensen Huang’s return to the headquarters of perhaps the world’s most important company, Nvidia, marking his personally triumphant comeback from being unanimously ousted by his board.

Get out of your thoughts any happy notions that you have reason to celebrate just because your money, like the money of so many millions of Americans and people well beyond the country’s borders, is tied up in the U.S. stock market, which soared at this surprising turn in the news after slumping badly in response to the scandals that had swirled around Huang.

Put out of mind the scenes of cheering you may have seen of Jensen Huang’s return to the headquarters of perhaps the world’s most important company, Nvidia, marking his personally triumphant comeback from being unanimously ousted by his board.

Get out of your thoughts any happy notions that you have reason to celebrate just because your money, like the money of so many millions of Americans and people well beyond the country’s borders, is tied up in the U.S. stock market, which soared at this surprising turn in the news after slumping badly in response to the scandals that had swirled around Huang.

What has happened in recent days, culminating in Huang’s return in the face of allegations he sustained a secret affair with a woman who was in touch with China’s spy agencies, is bad news for an economy that has grown increasingly imbalanced with the enormous growth of artificial intelligence. Far better for the U.S. economy and for everyone who has a stake in it would have been a reckoning for Nvidia that forced it to undertake the hard work of corporate reform and rebuilding trust.

Nvidia, for those who have not been paying attention, makes the most indispensable product in the world of AI: graphics processing units, a specialized type of chip, so capable and advanced that it has few real competitors, including, crucially, among well-funded Chinese companies. Nvidia, however, is much more than even this. As I have written before, it sits at the center of a highly incestuous economy of AI companies, including most of the big service providers, which it funds to keep purchasing its chips. The circular funding arrangements that Nvidia sustains with its largest customers—companies with extraordinary financial leverage and seemingly bottomless capital needs—is what keeps the AI economy’s party going. Without this, the merry-go-round of this new sector would grind to a halt and make what many thought looked like a bursting bubble in recent days seem like a minor blip.

Add to this the fact that the U.S. government and President Donald Trump have seemingly become convinced that everything and anything must be permitted with Nvidia, and with AI in general, or else China will overtake the United States in the most important technology of the near future. What this gives you is something more frightening than too big to fail: a company declared too strategically important to question or to hold to account.

The reality is that almost everything about the present picture is messed up and deeply worrisome. Let’s start with the fact that Trump, who already leads a government largely unconstrained by Congress and with wildly inflated executive powers, has taken so freely to intervening in the running of major companies. In Huang’s case, he played a central role in his reinstatement, as Monday’s news revealed.

There is nothing reassuring about this, but it gets worse. There is no reason to believe that Trump has an even remotely sophisticated grasp of the technologies at the heart of Nvidia’s business or its race against competitors. What we do know is that Trump has a weakness for very powerful and wealthy men, and Huang fits this billing. In the president’s universe, he is a winner, and Trump loves to associate himself with anyone invested with such an aura. Trump even posted an AI-generated photo of Huang intended to mirror a famous image of himself after surviving an assassination attempt, with the big difference that Huang was shown hoisting a red bra.

Trump, furthermore, is not known for a deep concern about ethics, and that is putting things gently. Where do ethics enter into this? Everywhere, from Huang’s honesty and the extent to which he was forthcoming about his affair with the suspicious Chinese national and shadowy customer, Alice Huang, as well as with at least one other woman who was his direct subordinate at Nvidia, in violation of corporate rules. Ethics are also involved in the way Huang ran his company, with a historically supine board, minimal internal controls on his behavior and oversight, and the way he promoted his family members. What matters is that nothing, inside the company or outside it, checked him. In fact, it seems likely that Trump, with his own record of serial marriages, rumored affairs, and children on the make, can relate.

But even these are mere starters. If competition with China deserves inclusion in any serious conversation about the future of AI, Trump’s treatment of Huang’s situation at Nvidia has been a running chronicle of flagrant disregard for national security. Let’s count the ways. First, when the U.S. government began to look into Alice Huang’s behavior on the suspicion that she might be a spy, it never informed Jensen Huang or gave him what would have been typical: a so-called defensive briefing so that he would know what to look out for and avoid giving away valuable trade secrets or selling his company’s most advanced chips without adequate controls preventing them from being shipped onward to China.

More broadly, historians may come to see Trump’s casual disregard for normal considerations of national security to be one of the most important and unusual hallmarks of his time in power. The examples are numerous. When shown evidence that Russia or China or some other powerful adversary is conducting secret operations against U.S. interests, Trump has often waved away concerns—in effect saying, well, we do that to them, too.

Last week offered the spectacle of Trump mugging in China with that country’s leader and Russia’s Vladimir Putin, with no hint that he had complained to them about offering targeting information to Iran that has enabled that country to attack U.S. bases in the Middle East, killing U.S. troops, as is the case with Russia, or supplying drone components to Iran, as with China. Trump went yet further, though, looking like a kid in a candy shop as he entered a room to greet an awaiting Putin and exclaiming, “He’s my friend.” Lest the message be lost on anyone, he later posted a picture of the meeting with this same message on social media.

Second, there is further reason to distrust the judgment of the U.S. government in the unfolding Nvidia saga. It lies in the vague and unforthcoming way its officials have spoken about Huang and his erstwhile Chinese mistress since their affair became public knowledge, always carefully avoiding clarity about whether or not she might have been a spy or whether his behavior damaged U.S. national security interests.

In fairness, the question of whether she was a spy involves some nuance. Alice Huang could very well have compromised U.S. national security without being a member or agent of any Chinese intelligence service in a formal sense. The lack of openness and straight language about the other question leaves little wiggle room, though. Through the Singapore-based company she worked for, Alice Huang was purchasing Nvidia’s most advanced chips, which fall under explicit U.S. export controls to China. This puts the public in a bewildering place. On the one hand, people are asked to believe that the United States must beat China in a zero-sum race toward the AI future. On the other, given the lack of candor about these Nvidia sales and the damage they may have caused to U.S. interests, we are being told, in effect, one of Trump’s favorite messages: Smile, and be happy. Lots of money was being made.

Taken together, Nvidia and Jensen Huang seem to concentrate enormous and growing risk for the entire public, as does the broader AI sector. Remember that Anthropic, the leading AI provider that went public recently, has warned of the potential power of its systems becoming so awesome as to having the potential to lead us all to an extinction-level event. And when the world’s top AI power is led by someone with such a casual disregard for safeguards, for strong governance, for ethics and for transparency, a company too important to question invites a peril that risks falling on us all.

Howard W. French is a columnist at Foreign Policy, a professor at the Columbia University Graduate School of Journalism, and a longtime foreign correspondent. His latest book is The Second Emancipation: Nkrumah, Pan-Africanism, and Global Blackness at High Tide. Bluesky: @hofrench.bsky.social X: @hofrench

#5776. It’s Time to Settle With Russia

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MICHAEL C. DESCH is Packey J. Dee Professor of International Relations at the University of Notre Dame and founding Brian and Jeannelle Brady Family Director of the O’Brien Notre Dame International Security Center.

What America Should Give to Make Peace in Ukraine

In October, U.S. President Donald Trump sold his eleventh-hour deal to secure diesel from Russian President Vladimir Putin—pausing sanctions that his own administration had imposed—as a boon for the negotiations to end the war in Ukraine. The arrangement, he wrote on Truth Social, was intended to keep Russia at “the bargaining table.” Of course, it was always obvious to observers that the gambit had as much or more to do with bolstering the U.S. economy and stemming a massive victory for the Democrats in this month’s midterm elections, and in that, it failed.

Cynics have grounds for questioning all of Trump’s moves when it comes to Russia and Ukraine. He clearly dislikes Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky and shows a marked preference for illiberal leaders, especially those willing to give him easy wins. Still, a stopped clock can be right twice a day. And concessions such as the fuel deal are exactly what might move Russia and Ukraine closer to peace, even if they are not wholly motivated by public-spiritedness.

Ukraine and its backers in Europe and the United States have long maintained that Russia only responds to sticks. The problem with their argument is that since 2014, and especially since 2022, Moscow has been subjected to nothing but sticks. According to Kaja Kallas, the European Union’s foreign policy chief, the more than 25,000 sanctions imposed on Russia after its full-scale invasion of Ukraine had cost its economy almost $1.5 trillion as of June. And although Trump has backed Ukraine less consistently than U.S. President Joe Biden did, especially in his rhetoric, his administration has still provided significant financial, military, and intelligence support; his CIA has been more aggressive than Biden’s in assisting Ukraine’s offensive and defensive air operations. All of these coercive measures have proved insufficient to compel a change in Putin’s policy or turn the tide in Ukraine’s favor on the battlefield.

European leaders may fret that Trump, now a lame duck and focused on his legacy, will push for a deal on Ukraine that suits the Kremlin’s interests more than their own. But ultimately, this remains a war in which Russia has the advantage. Europe should recognize that its pressure tactics have not worked and welcome the U.S. president’s willingness to throw in a few carrots among the many sticks used to try to beat the Kremlin into submission. Washington, meanwhile, should continue to pursue a concessionary strategy by encouraging Ukraine to surrender territory, freezing NATO’s expansion, and offering sanctions relief to Moscow. Putin has shown a willingness to sign favorable deals in the past, including the Minsk agreements in 2014 and 2015, which promised an end to the civil conflict in Donbas only to collapse under the duress of Kyiv and European capitals. A more enduring peace is desperately needed in Ukraine, however unfair the price may seem.

Ukraine has had some recent success on the battlefield. Early in 2026, Kyiv launched offensives in western Zaporizhzhia that erased a year of Russian gains there. During the summer, the Ukrainian military embarked on a major counteroffensive, Operation Vivaldi, that made significant progress near Liman, delaying Russia’s campaign to take the Donbas fortress cities of Kramatorsk and Sloviansk. And after four years, Ukrainian forces have finally inflicted serious, albeit not irreparable, damage to the bridge connecting Crimea and Russia, disrupting a military transport route and tarnishing a potent symbol of Putin-era expansionism. Kyiv has also brought the war home to segments of the Russian public with its attacks on oil infrastructure and commercial targets such as warehouses for Wildberries, Russia’s largest online retailer. Supporters in Western capitals have trumpeted these achievements as evidence that Ukraine has turned the tide in a war that previously seemed to be moving in Moscow’s favor.

But tactical wins do not necessarily add up to strategic victory. And, as Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby reminded the Ukraine Defense Contact Group at a meeting earlier this month, the war will be decided on the ground. Casualties remain a huge problem for Ukraine. The few times Zelensky has addressed them, he has offered implausibly low figures for his side and cited astronomically high losses on the other. A recent study by Alexander Grable, a King’s College London war studies researcher, dissects these figures and concludes that Ukraine is suffering far higher casualties than Russia in absolute numbers—findings that, if true, refute the canard that Russia is uniquely careless with the lives of its soldiers. These days, while Ukraine pursues costly offensives to temporarily recover territory, Russia is taking cities and densely fortified areas by encirclement rather than frontal assault, reducing the risk to its fighters.

Although Ukraine’s strikes on Russian oil infrastructure have disrupted refining and strained domestic fuel supplies, Moscow has managed to cushion the blow through emergency measures and rapid repairs. In May, the U.S. Defense Intelligence Agency concluded that Kyiv’s long-range drone attacks did not “substantially affect Russian energy production,” which may explain why the Russian civilian population has not felt the full bite of them. The average Russian seems to be weathering this pain with something between resigned stoicism and increased determination to see the war through to victory. If Ukraine’s deep strikes were designed to encourage the United States to support Zelensky’s proposal for an energy truce, its effects have been counterproductive: higher oil prices have refilled the Kremlin’s war chest and greater energy costs are undermining allied support for Ukraine, especially in the United States.

Many Western battlefield analyses suggest Russia has recently lost a bit of ground. In fact, the battle lines in Ukraine remain ambiguous. According to the widely followed Military Summary Telegram channel, which applies a nuanced metric of territorial control to geolocated videos from Russian and Ukrainian sources, Russia has continued to make inroads through October and November. In the north, its troops have carved out a buffer zone on the border, making gains around Sumy and Velykyi Burluk. The see-saw battle for Kupyiansk—a fiercely contested gateway to Kharkiv and northeastern Ukraine—once again favors Moscow. In Donetsk, Russian forces have largely taken the city of Dobropillia, which could enable them to creep north behind the Donbas fortress cities and encircle them. And Russia continues to gain ground in eastern Zaporizhzhia, where its units are now edging closer to the region’s administrative capital. Operation Vivaldi may have slowed the Russian advance north of Kramatorsk, but only at a significant cost to some of Ukraine’s best units. Much like the Kursk campaign in 2024, the counteroffensive seems more like a temporary media victory than a real change in the ground war.

In the air, Russia has commenced a deep strike campaign across Ukraine, using drones and ballistic missiles to turn the tables in key regions. Russian forces have severely disrupted Kyiv’s Black Sea operations, intensified strikes against data centers and electricity infrastructure, attacked locomotives, and hit targets ever closer to Ukraine’s western borders, and are now going after major bridges across the Dnieper, which could literally cut the country in two. Russia’s quantitative and qualitative advantage in jet-powered drones allows these weapons to function as effectively as cruise missiles, and recent air operations have been more sustained and systematic as a result. Ukraine’s dearth of American-made PAC-3 interceptor missiles—a result of the United States’ own dwindling defense stockpiles—means that Russian ballistic missiles are mostly unstoppable.

In October, Trump angrily wrote on social media that Zelensky “doesn’t want to end this war, and probably never did.” This was unfair: undoubtedly, Zelensky does want to end it, just on his own terms. The aid and support Europe and the United States have lavished on Ukraine—initially necessary for preventing a catastrophic defeat—now gives its president little incentive to make peace any other way. Early in the current war, when Ukraine was unexpectedly prevailing on the battlefield, it passed on two promising peace tracks that might have ended the fighting soon after it began.

But negotiations are now the only way forward for Ukraine, and they will not progress by targeting Russia exclusively with sticks. To overcome the current impasse, Ukraine and its supporters have to put their chips on the table. That means forgoing any hope of NATO accession—or of a sustained NATO military presence in Ukraine—and relinquishing territory to Russia. If Kyiv remains unwilling to make the concessions demanded of losing parties in war, then Washington can take steps to encourage better judgement. The United States could taper down or cut off certain services and munitions it provides the Ukrainian armed forces, including Starlink and Starshield satellite connectivity, Patriot missile defense systems, and intelligence support for air defense and deep strikes on Russian targets. Any of these deprivations would be felt immediately on the battlefield.

Washington should offer carrots of its own accord, too. President Trump can lift some sanctions, such as those banning Russian commercial flights from U.S. airspace, without much controversy. Lifting others, such as those restricting fossil fuel exports and financial transactions, will be less palatable but more attractive to Russia and thus more effective. Russia’s readmission to the SWIFT interbank messaging service and other global commercial networks must be pursued accordingly.

Pro-Ukrainian hardliners argue that no amount of carrots will be enough to satisfy Putin’s voracious appetite for territory. They warn that if Russia is not stopped in Ukraine, it will continue its campaign of conquest farther west through Poland, Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia, and beyond, with no end in sight. As Zelensky warned European leaders at the Berlin Security Conference in early November, the Russian military buildup is not just directed at his country, “it is directed against you.” But although Russia is making grinding progress on the fronts in eastern Ukraine, at the rate of its advances in 2025 and this year, it would take three decades just to capture the rest of Ukraine east of the Dnieper. And the idea that Russia, with a military of under 1.6 million men and women in uniform, could defeat a NATO alliance boasting 3.65 million soldiers is implausible, especially considering NATO’s overwhelming advantages in major weapons and military spending.

Even if Russia could take on NATO directly, it is far from clear that Putin wants to. The Kremlin’s war aims in Ukraine may not seem just, but they are narrower than many analysts allege. Since his 2007 speech in Munich excoriating the United States’ global hegemony—and after the 2008 NATO summit declaration that welcomed eventual membership for Georgia and Ukraine—NATO expansion has been Putin’s idée fixe. He has been similarly preoccupied with the status of the pro-Russia citizens of Eastern Ukraine and devoted substantial effort to ensuring protection of their linguistic rights and political influence through the Minsk agreements. Only when that failed did Moscow recognize the two regions of the Donbas, Luhansk and Donetsk as independent states eligible for membership in the Russian Federation, paving the way for direct Russian intervention in the region. Nonetheless, Putin continued to pursue a negotiated solution to the conflict through Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan and Israel’s then prime minister Naftali Bennett. As with the Minsk process, it was Ukraine and its backers who walked away from these negotiations when their aims—the complete liberation of all Russian-occupied territory—still appeared achievable on the battlefield.

In a round of U.S.-led peace talks earlier this month in Abu Dhabi, Russia made maximalist demands, asking for the pullback of NATO forces from Poland and the Baltic states, limits on European nuclear deterrence, and restrictions on the size and capabilities of Ukraine’s military. But read in the broader context of Putin’s singular and consistent fixations, these demands look more like negotiating ploys than dealbreakers. It has long been clear what the Kremlin will settle for: control over Ukraine’s eastern border regions and a largely demilitarized Kyiv. Whatever the ultimate terms of this deal, some basic facts are clear. There is no going back to a Ukrainian Donbas, nor a Ukrainian Crimea or Novorossiya. And whatever security guarantees Europe and the United States give Ukraine in the future, they cannot include NATO membership.

The status quo in Ukraine is unsustainable. Four years of war have lulled many of Kyiv’s supporters into complacency about the risk that continued pressure on Russia could create an escalatory spiral. There is increasingly cavalier talk in some newer NATO capitals, for example, about undertaking aggressive NATO exercises near Kaliningrad and overcoming the city’s air and missile defenses. But those steps may only further widen and deepen the war. The clear and present danger of vertical and horizontal escalation must not be dismissed.

Cracks are already showing in the West’s united front against Russia. France and other western European states are experiencing heightened social and political unrest, fueled in part by the costs of the war. In late October, Finnish President Alexander Stubb and Norwegian Prime Minister Jonas Gahr Store made a rare phone call to Putin to “convey messages directly regarding future negotiations” toward peace, according to a statement released by the two leaders. In the United States, a once bipartisan hard-line toward Russia is starting to fall out of favor, even among Democrats. In mid-October, Democratic Representative Jim Himes told the Warsaw Security Forum that a future Democratic president will have to acknowledge the “tectonic fact” that the United States can no longer afford to play its historic leading role in confronting Russia.

There is reason to think that the Trump administration’s more conciliatory approach has already opened the door to diplomacy. The Oleshky truce brokered by First Lady Melania Trump and the Vatican successfully evacuated civilians trapped in a Russian-occupied city south of Kherson. And the president’s comments in his 60 Minutes interview this month suggested that he is ready to ply Russia with the incentives necessary to get a deal over the line. These are steps in the right direction, but in addition to altering the balance of carrots offered to Russia, the Trump administration must consider providing Ukraine security guarantees outside of the NATO framework.

Now in the twilight of his political career, Trump has made clear his desire to leave a legacy in the form of Nobel Peace Prizes and triumphal arches. Ending a major war in Ukraine would be a crowning achievement, which is why he has waxed lyrical about a “big, beautiful agreement” since he entered his second term in office. And if Trump still believes that China is the pacing threat of the twenty-first century, reintegrating Russia into the global economy and weakening the axis between Putin and Chinese leader Xi Jinping will be paramount for advancing his long-term political vision.

No one has lost money betting against peace in Ukraine since 2014, and it would be foolhardy to expect Trump to pursue diplomacy solely for altruistic reasons. But perhaps if he sees peace as being in his own interest, as well as that of the United States, he might, despite himself, broker an end to a bloody and increasingly fruitless war. Doing so could be one of the few positive legacies of his second term, even if it does not get him to Oslo.

#5772. Unsealed warrant documents for Ilhan Omar's DC home raid reveal decades-long scope of investigation

Fox News legal analyst Gregg Jarrett joins 'Fox & Friends' to explain what a federal judge's expected release of redacted search warrant records could reveal about the investigation into Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn.

Heavily redacted versions of documents used to justify a search warrant and raid on the Washington, D.C. home of Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., were released to the public Monday evening.

Despite the level of redaction, the documents provide more insight into the scope of the investigation into the Somali-born congresswoman.

In an ex parte brief in support of an affidavit for a search warrant, federal prosecutors say they're searching for all "records and information that constitute fruits, contraband, evidence, and instrumentalities of violations of [redacted], those violations involving Ilhan Nur Said Elmi a/k/a Ilhan Abdullahi Omar, and occurring between 1980 to the present (the 'Target Offenses)."

While accounts vary, even from the congresswoman herself, Omar was born in 1981 or 1982. She does not have a birth certificate. She came to the United States in 1995 with her father. Fox News Digital confirmed just after the Oct. 12 raid of her home, that investigators were looking into alleged immigration fraud, potentially related to how Omar and her father filled out her immigration paperwork.

In another document, arguing that the court mischaracterized the government's arguments in ex parte proceedings, prosecutors make it clear that the investigation into Omar spans decades.

"The United States believes that ILHAN has committed [REDACTED] crimes over decades— beginning long before she was ever in Congress," the document says.

Fox News Digital reached out to a spokesperson for Omar.

In a rare move, Obama-appointed federal Judge James Boasberg ordered the documents' release last week, telling the Department of Justice (DOJ) to submit proposed redactions before the materials were made public on Monday. Judge Boasberg, in order dated October 9, 2026, agreed initially to seal the documents due to "such reasonable grounds to believe the disclosure will result in flight from prosecution, destruction of or tampering with evidence, intimidation of potential witnesses, and serious jeopardy to the investigation."

Search warrants typically remain sealed until there is an arrest in a criminal case.

Fifteen media outlets, led by The New York Times, along with watchdog group Judicial Watch, sued President Donald Trump's DOJ for the release of the documents used to conduct the raid of a sitting congresswoman's residence. The outlets claimed that viewing the documents was in the public interest, while the government attempted to block the release by arguing that it was a violation of investigative protocol.

Rep. lhan Omar, D-Minn., continues to generate headlines related to her finances.  (Tom Williams/CQ-Roll Call, Inc via Getty Images)

OBAMA-APPOINTED JUDGE PAVES WAY TO UNSEAL ILHAN OMAR SEARCH WARRANT IN BLOW TO TRUMP'S DOJ

Omar's Washington, D.C., home was raided on Oct. 12, 2026 — an unprecedented action to take against a sitting lawmaker, especially when there is less than a month until the Election Day where they are seeking reelection. The raid did not impact Omar in winning a fifth term in the House of Representatives.

Homeland Security Investigations (HSI), the chief investigative body of the Department of Homeland Security (DHS), conducted the raid last month. Omar's personal phone, computer and passport were seized as evidence in the search. Authorities are also in possession of her DNA.

Additionally, the lawmaker's sister, Sahra Noor, voluntarily submitted to questions from investigators during the week of the raid. Investigators are probing whether Omar or her late father — or both — lied on her citizenship paperwork, sources told Fox News Digital.

In a motion seeking to prevent the federal search warrant for the raid from being unsealed, the U.S. Attorney's Office for the District of Columbia referred to Omar as "Ilhan Nur Said Elmi, a/k/a Ilhan Abdullahi Omar." Sources later told Fox News Digital that Ilhan Omar is not the congresswoman's "real name."

Homeland Security Investigations police search the home of Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., on Oct. 12, 2026, in Washington, D.C. According to reports, the search is part of an ongoing criminal investigation. (Andrew Leyden/Getty Images)

That news caused speculation that Omar married her brother, Ahmed Nur Said Elmi, to resurface. The pair married in 2009, and divorced in 2017.

Omar has not been charged with a crime and has repeatedly denied wrongdoing. She has characterized the investigation as politically motivated, saying President Donald Trump is targeting her.

"For a decade, Donald Trump has relentlessly targeted Congresswoman Ilhan Omar with inflammatory rhetoric, repeatedly putting a target on her back," her office said in a statement after the raid. "Now, those attacks have escalated to a pointless federal raid. The Congresswoman has done nothing wrong. No president should be able to use the power of the federal government to intimidate his political enemies."

The Somali-born congresswoman came to the United States as a child in 1995, after decades of civil unrest in her home country. Her father, Nur Omar Mohamed, served as a colonel in the Somali National Army under the brutal Siad Barre regime.

Barre's regime systematically killed between 50,000 and 200,000 members of the Isaaq clan in what is known as the Isaaq genocide. He was deposed in 1991.

#5768. Massachusetts sues Trump admin to stop ICE's courthouse arrests: 'Unnecessary fear'

Massachusetts Attorney General Andrea Campbell filed a federal lawsuit Monday seeking to block ICE from making civil immigration arrests inside state courthouses without a judicial warrant.

Massachusetts officials sued the Trump administration on Monday to block Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) agents from carrying out civil arrests in the state's courthouses, arguing that such arrests without judicial warrants are illegal under state law.

The lawsuit filed by Massachusetts Attorney General Andrea Joy Campbell challenges a 2025 ICE directive that authorizes agents to conduct civil arrests in or near state courthouses without judicial warrants, which she says violates the PROTECT Act, a new state law passed ​by the Democratic-led legislature that prohibits civil immigration arrests in state courthouses without judicial warrants. The courthouse protections took effect Nov. 3.

Campbell said the ICE directive interferes with the state's authority to operate its judicial system, disrupts court proceedings, undermines constitutional principles and public safety and spreads fear, chaos and confusion.

"The Massachusetts PROTECT Act makes it illegal for ICE to conduct civil arrests in our courthouses without a judicial warrant. ICE’s intentional violation of state law has created unnecessary fear, made it more difficult to operate our courts and hold defendants accountable," Campbell said in a statement.

DOJ SUES TO STOP OHIO COURT BLOCKING COURTHOUSE ARRESTS

Massachusetts officials sued the Trump administration to block ICE agents from carrying out civil arrests in the state's courthouses. (Photo by Jim WATSON / AFP via Getty Images)

"ICE is misleading the public by suggesting that these arrests make us safer when, in fact, ICE's actions are preventing criminal cases from moving forward, denying a victim’s ability to obtain accountability, violating a defendant’s due process, and leaving open the possibility that someone facing serious criminal charges could ultimately be released from ICE custody without their criminal case ever being resolved," she continued.

The attorney general said more than 1,110 people have been arrested at her state's courthouses since President Donald Trump returned to the White House in January 2025, including more than 600 people in 2025 and more than 500 during the first 10 months of 2026.

Campbell said ICE has ignored the new state law and arrested at least 38 people in Massachusetts courthouses without judicial warrants in the two and a half weeks since the courthouse protections went into effect.

"I, along with our district attorneys, share the goal of holding defendants accountable for crimes, yet ICE’s unlawful tactics to arrest someone for a civil immigration violation is not the same as holding them accountable for a crime," Campbell said.

FEDERAL JUDGE IN NEW YORK TIES TRUMP ADMIN HANDS ON ARRESTS AT IMMIGRATION COURTS

The attorney general said more than 1,110 people have been arrested at her state's courthouses since President Donald Trump returned to the White House in January 2025. (Getty Images)

Campbell said that ICE's courthouse arrests have continued in Massachusetts despite a federal ruling handed down in June by U.S. District Judge P. Casey Pitts in California that vacated the Trump administration's courthouse arrest policy ​in a separate class action lawsuit.

In Monday's lawsuit, Campbell argues the ICE policy violates the Administrative Procedure Act since it was adopted without taking into account the impact it would have on states and because the Immigration and Nationality Act does not explicitly authorize courthouse arrests.

The lawsuit also alleges that the ⁠policy violates the U.S. Constitution's Tenth Amendment, which protects states' rights to maintain authority over their own judicial systems.

"The Trump Administration’s ICE arrests at Massachusetts courthouses are interfering with our justice system and undermining public safety. When people are afraid to go to court, victims are less likely to report crimes, survivors are less likely to seek protection, and our communities are less safe," Massachusetts Gov. Maura Healey said in a statement. "We expect ICE and all federal agents to follow Massachusetts law."

The lawsuit challenges a 2025 ICE directive that instructs agents to conduct civil arrests in or near state courthouses without judicial warrants. (Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Fox News Digital reached out to the Department of Homeland Security for comment.

In a statement to Reuters, a DHS spokesperson argued that making immigration arrests at courthouses "conserves valuable law enforcement resources because they already ​know where a target will ​be," adding that "the ability of law enforcement ⁠to make arrests of criminal illegal aliens in courthouses is common sense."

A similar lawsuit against ICE arrests at state courthouses was filed in October by California Attorney General Rob Bonta.

#5756. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5753. Trump orders federal contractors to check with White House before responding to Congress

President Trump speaks at a news conference in the James Brady Press Briefing Room of the White House, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, in Washington. Jacquelyn Martin/AP hide caption

NEW YORK (AP) — President Donald Trump is directing federal contractors and subcontractors to consult with his administration about inquiries they receive from Congress and to withhold any communications that the White House considers privileged, according to a new executive order.

The companies must also coordinate with the White House and U.S. agencies on "appropriate responses" to any such requests, the order issued late Friday says. If they fail to comply, the White House warns their contracts could be terminated and their future work with the federal government suspended.

The order is the Republican administration's latest effort to resist what are expected to be wide-ranging and aggressive oversight efforts from Democrats when they take over Congress in January. Democrats have made government contracts one of their top investigative targets, noting that federal money has been flowing to companies linked with Trump's family, particularly at the Pentagon.

White House officials have already begun taking steps to exert more control over how agencies and officials respond to congressional requests, anticipating there will be a flood of investigations initiated by Democrats.

Trump's counsel, Will Scharf, told federal agencies on Monday that they must seek approval from his office and the White House's Office of Legislative Affairs before agreeing to appear before Congress. Scharf also suggested in a recent letter to Rep. Robert Garcia of California, the top Democrat on the House Oversight Committee, that some congressional investigations may amount to "blatant criminality."

Garcia sent letters this week directing members of Trump's family and top administration officials to preserve documents. Scharf sought to dismiss the requests, noting in a letter to Democratic lawmakers that they are not yet in the majority and cannot compel compliance.

Responding to the new executive order, Garcia said it was part of the administration's "blatant attack on Congress' constitutional right to conduct oversight." He said that despite efforts to impede investigations, "we're just getting started."

In a fact sheet, the White House framed the new requirements as "protecting executive privileges from congressional overreach," arguing that some congressional leaders have tried to intrude on executive branch privileges by "harassing private companies that contract with the federal government."

Presidents of both parties have long used executive privilege to resist congressional inquiries, but the claims can set up messy, protracted legal fights as parties dispute whether it applies. Executive privilege allows the president to keep information from the courts, Congress and the public to protect the confidentiality of White House deliberations, but the protection is far from absolute.

The latest executive order applies to all contractors and subcontractors, a universe of tens of thousands of businesses.

#5744. Medicare looks a bit different in 2027, so don't miss open enrollment

A counselor, right, helps a client through the Medicare signup process at a resource center for older adults in Sunrise, Florida. Medicare open enrollment season started Oct. 15 and ends Dec. 7. Joe Cavaretta/South Florida Sun Sentinel/Tribune News Service via Getty Images hide caption

Annual open enrollment is underway for Medicare, the federal program that provides health insurance to roughly 70 million people. The coverage choices are complex and often consequential.

Open enrollment runs through Dec. 7. The plan you choose takes effect on Jan. 1, for the full calendar year.

Medicare provides health insurance to people 65 or older, as well as to some younger people with disabilities or certain health conditions. But it isn't one plan. It's more like a build-your-own-insurance menu.

Roughly half of beneficiaries are enrolled in traditional Medicare, which consists of Parts A and B. Administered by the federal government, the plan covers hospital stays, skilled nursing care, doctor visits, outpatient care, and preventive screenings. Enrollees can typically see any healthcare provider who accepts Medicare, and they rarely need approval for diagnostic tests, such as MRIs and CT scans. However, there's no cap on out-of-pocket expenses in traditional Medicare, and it doesn't cover most prescriptions or dental, vision, or hearing services.

Stay up to date with our Up First newsletter, sent every weekday morning.

As a result, most traditional Medicare enrollees buy extra coverage from private insurance companies under Medicare Part D, the prescription drug plan. Some also buy a Medigap policy that covers the costs traditional Medicare leaves behind, such as deductibles, copayments, and coinsurance.

This year, the other half of Medicare beneficiaries opted for privately offered, all-in-one managed-care plans known as Medicare Advantage, usually including prescription drug coverage, dental, and vision. The drawbacks are smaller networks and a greater likelihood that treatments and medications will require prior authorization. Like beneficiaries in traditional Medicare, Medicare Advantage participants also pay Part B premiums.

"People really need to do their research before they enroll in Medicare, because there are trade-offs," said David Lipschutz, a co-director at the Center for Medicare Advocacy.

Every fall, insurers that offer Part D and Advantage plans send enrollees a critical document known as an Annual Notice of Change. It's a cheat sheet outlining what will be different in the following year: premiums, deductibles, covered drugs. Consumer advocates and Medicare experts say people sometimes make the costly mistake of throwing it out or filing it away without reading it. Can't find it? Call the insurer and ask for another copy.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services projects that the average Part D premium will increase less than $1 a month for a standalone prescription drug plan, describing the market as stable. But that average doesn't tell the whole story. An analysis by KFF, the health policy research nonprofit that includes KFF Health News, found that enrollees who stay in their prescription drug plan will see a 50% increase on average, from $34 to $51. About 10.4 million people could pay higher premiums, not including higher deductibles and coinsurance costs.

About 4 million people are in Medicare Advantage plans that won't be offered in 2027. If they do nothing, they'll be automatically enrolled in traditional Medicare. Jenny Kane/AP hide caption

Some beneficiaries who didn't have to pay monthly premiums in recent years will begin paying for prescription drug Part D coverage in 2027. While that change follows the 2025 introduction of federal caps on out-of-pocket drug expenses, it comes as the Trump administration is ending temporary federal subsidies that helped keep premiums down. The cap on out-of-pocket expenses for prescription drugs in 2027 will be $2,400, a total enrollees can opt tospread out over the course of the year.

Juliette Cubanski, who directs the Program on Medicare Policy at KFF, said people who don't shop around could be leaving money on the table.

"It is worth it to avoid any unpleasant surprises in the form of either higher premiums or higher cost sharing for covered services in 2027," she said.

An estimated 4.3 million people now have to look for a new Medicare Advantage or prescription drug plan after private insurers left the market. Those who want to stay in Medicare Advantage must select new coverage, or they'll be moved to traditional Medicare automatically. For those shopping for a new plan, low monthly premiums may be appealing, but it's important to read the fine print and add up the other costs.

"There are going to be, often, increases in cost sharing and copays, increases in out-of-pocket caps, and reductions in the scope of some of the extra benefits that Medicare Advantage plans provide," Lipschutz said.

So ask: Is my prescription covered? Is my pharmacy preferred? Is my doctor in-network? What does the dental benefit actually cover?

And pay attention to the out-of-pocket maximum for the Advantage plans, Cubanski said, so you know how much you might have to shell out in 2027.

"Look at that dollar value specifically," she said.

For those moving to traditional Medicare, it won't come with a drug plan, so enrollees must sign up for a new Part D plan if they want most prescriptions covered. Enrollees who miss that step when they are first eligible may not have drug coverage for the coming year, and they may face a late-enrollment penalty on their monthly premiums for life.

When selecting a drug plan, enrollees must also tell their insurer how they want to pay, either by getting a bill in the mail or by having the cost deducted from their Social Security. If you don't pick a payment plan, you could get dropped from coverage.

If enrollees elect to leave their Medicare Advantage plans for traditional Medicare within their first year of joining Medicare, they have a guaranteed right to buy a Medigap plan, with no health screening. After that, all bets are off: Insurers in most states can turn enrollees down or charge more because of a health condition. Connecticut, Maine, Massachusetts, and New York are exceptions.

Enrollees whose Advantage plans have not been renewed — and haven't been consolidated into another plan — can switch to traditional Medicare and buy a Medigap policy without a health screening, but they need to act before March.

Medicare experts advise enrollees to write down all their prescription drugs, as well as any procedures planned in the next year, such as a joint replacement or dental work, to help them decide which plan will cover their needs. Call a favorite doctor's office to see whether they are in-network for the plans being considered. Remember that brokers might earn commissions, especially on Advantage plans, and might not represent every plan.

For unbiased help, every state has a federally funded State Health Insurance Assistance Program that offers free plan counseling specific to your situation, in person and over the phone. To get help, go to the website at shiphelp.org/open-enrollment, email info@shiphelp.org, or call the SHIP National Technical Assistance Center at 877-839-2675.

A SHIP counselor can also help enrollees apply for benefits that help pay for Medicare premiums and prescription drug costs, such as the Medicare Savings Programs and Medicare Part D Extra Help.

KFF Health News is a national newsroom that produces in-depth journalism about health issues and is one of the core operating programs at KFF — the independent source for health policy research, polling, and journalism.

#5741. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Sponsored Content by Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

#5726. ES Foundry ships more than 1 GW of U.S.-manufactured solar cells

As one of the few U.S. solar cell manufacturers with a fully operational facility producing and shipping cells to customers, ES Foundry is helping move American solar manufacturing to commercial reality.

The party has officially won the largest majority in Congress since the early 1990s. Take a look at NPR's breakdown of the U.S. House races, by the numbers.

While AI stocks have driven markets to record highs and bolstered the economy, concerns are growing about a potential bubble. The incident highlights the stakes for European countries in Russia's war on Ukraine, prompting nations along NATO's eastern border to seek enhanced air defense support. Poland plans to present proposals to increase surveillance capabilities and deploy more F-35s to intercept incoming missiles and drones.

Delegates at COP31 in Antalya, Turkey, couldn't agree on a plan over the weekend to address fossil fuels, the main cause of climate change. The event ran two days longer than expected, making it the longest in the history of these summits. Nearly 200 nations failed to finalize targets for electrification and financial aid for developing countries, or to address the impacts of climate change. The European Union rejected any conclusion that didn't advocate for a transition away from fossil fuels, while oil-producing countries like Saudi Arabia opposed this position. Fossil fuels contribute about 73% of global emissions.

by Maria Godoy, senior science and health editor and correspondent

Clinical services supervisor Dale Weiser poses for a portrait at the Chester County Health Department in West Chester, Pa., on Oct. 28, 2026. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption

People had warned me it might be tough to get Amish people to talk to me as a journalist — and probably impossible to get their permission to record their voice. Not a great omen for compelling radio storytelling.

Yet as I headed off to Lancaster County, Pa., last month to report on the biggest and deadliest measles outbreak in the U.S. in decades, I knew I wanted to hear directly from the community hit hardest.

So I turned to some good old shoe-leather reporting. My producer and I mapped out Amish-owned businesses in and around Lancaster County and spent a day driving around from shop to shop. At every stop, we introduced ourselves and explained the focus of our story. We asked Amish people (easily identifiable by their traditional dress) about their views on the measles outbreak and vaccination. While some declined to talk, others opened up about how measles had affected their families and communities, and why they had or hadn't decided to get vaccinated. To my surprise, one woman even let me record her voice.

Outside a farm supply store in a rural town, I met an Amish woman who was visibly pregnant. Measles can have devastating consequences during pregnancy. She told me her husband and two children had gotten vaccinated to protect her and her unborn child. She declined to give me her name at first, then got in a car driven by her hired non-Amish driver. As they exited the parking lot, the driver stopped to ask us what media outlet we were with. "Oh, NPR!" the driver said when we replied. Then she turned to her Amish passenger and told her, "Don't worry, you can trust them."

Part of gaining trust is about showing up, showing respect and really listening. Word gets around that you're trustworthy. Public health officials in Pennsylvania have also been using a similar approach to win the trust of Amish people as they seek to boost vaccination rates in the current outbreak. You can read more about it here.

Bottles of Roundup weed killer are displayed on a shelf at a Home Depot store on June 25, 2026 in North Hollywood, California. Justin Sullivan/Getty Images North America hide caption

#5707. California Democrat Rep. Gomez resigns following ethics report on sexual misconduct allegations

U.S. Rep. Jimmy Gomez, seen here at a Congressional hearing in March, announced Monday that he would resign following a report from the House Ethics committee on allegations of sexual misconduct. Heather Diehl/Getty Images North America hide caption

Rep. Jimmy Gomez, D-Calif., says he is resigning from Congress after a bipartisan investigation released Monday found "substantial evidence" he engaged in sexual misconduct toward a House staffer.

The House Ethics Committee concluded Gomez violated House rules by "making unwelcome sexual advances and engaging in unwelcome sexual conduct towards a House staffer and disregarding the power imbalance inherent in his relationship with House staffers."

The report also says the behavior could constitute criminal sexual assault.

Gomez has served in Congress since 2017 representing parts of Los Angeles. He lost reelection this fall to fellow Democrat Angela Gonzales-Torres. California uses a system where the top two vote-getters no matter the party advance to the general election. Gomez says his resignation will be effective Dec. 1.

Lisa Banks and Sarah Nesbitt, attorneys for the unnamed House staffer, wrote in a statement that their client testified to the committee truthfully and at personal risk.

In a statement announcing his plan to resign, Gomez wrote that he cooperated with the investigation, but has strong disagreements with the report and its conclusions.

"Among other significant deficiencies and omissions, the report does not include key portions of my testimony that are essential to a full and fair understanding of what occurred," he wrote.

"I also have serious concerns about the process. In my interview, I answered the Committee's questions only to see my statements selectively quoted, disparaged, and disregarded throughout the final report, to which I was given no opportunity to respond."

The House Ethics Committee, which includes five Democrats and five Republicans, opened its investigation into Gomez in August following a press report of Gomez kissing a staffer from another Congressional office at a party. Gomez denied those allegations.

The committee asked Gomez to disclose whether he "ever engaged in a romantic or sexual relationship or made sexual contact with a congressional staffer." Gomez disclosed interactions with two women — telling the committee the encounters were all consensual. Both women spoke to the committee.

One denied "she had ever had a nonconsensual sexual encounter" with Gomez. The other, described as Witness 1 in the report, said she had more than one encounter with Gomez and she did not consider them consensual.

She told investigators that Gomez made sexual contact without her consent at a bar in February 2024 and then was forceful with her and tried to kiss her at his congressional office in July 2024.

Gomez denied the interactions were nonconsensual. The staffer, described as Witness 1 in the committee report, eventually left her job on Capitol Hill.

"Here, the House lost Witness 1 as a competent and able staffer because of Representative Gomez's misconduct and because of a broader culture of disrespect and toxic sexual behavior toward staff that permeates life on the Hill and is long overdue for correction," the committee wrote.

The report comes amid a broader conversation about how Congress deals with the behavior of its members, especially when it comes to sexual misconduct.

In April, Democrat Eric Swalwell of California resigned his House seat amid allegations of sexual assault and misconduct. He also denied the allegations. Rep. Tony Gonzales (R-Tex.) also resigned from the House after admitting to an affair with a former staff member.

Rep. Chuck Edwards (R-N.C.) dropped his reelection bid earlier this year after the House Ethics Committee recommended he face a censure vote for allegedly harassing two young female staffers. Edwards denied the allegations.

Republican Reps. Max Miller of Ohio and Cory Mills of Florida also face House Ethics investigations stemming from allegations of domestic violence. Both deny wrongdoing.

Miller lost his reelection bid this fall and Mills lost his GOP primary.

Last week, the House Ethics Committee adopted new rules with the aim of speeding up investigations into sexual misconduct and improving transparency.

#5705. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5697. Google signs 50-MW superhot geothermal PPA with Quaise Energy

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agreement will support Google’s Oregon data center operations beginning as early as 2030, advancing development of a technology designed to access geothermal resources deeper underground.

Google's agreement with Quaise gives superhot geothermal an important commercial partner as developers seek to move enhanced geothermal systems from demonstration projects to utility-scale deployment.

“Geothermal energy remains largely underutilized. It supplies less than 0.5% of global electricity and is limited to just a handful of states in the U.S.,” Diane Hughes, senior vice president of marketing and communications at Quaise, said in an email.

Unlike conventional geothermal plants, which depend on naturally occurring underground hot-water reservoirs that are geographically constrained and rarely exceed 400 degrees Fahrenheit, enhanced geothermal uses engineered reservoirs to access heat in deeper rock formations.

Quaise is developing millimeter-wave drilling designed to ablate rock without mechanical contact. The company is designing wells to reach depths of up to 65,000 feet, where rock temperatures can exceed 932 degrees Fahrenheit and conventional drill bits cannot withstand the heat, according to Hughes.

“Superhot wells like these can produce up to 10x more power per well than what's currently in play and bring geothermal to places where it isn't viable or economical today," she said.

The agreement comes as technology companies increasingly seek firm power sources to meet data center demand. Google has signed nearly 35 GW of clean energy purchase agreements globally since 2010, including previous investments in next-generation geothermal, according to its most recent environmental report. Google says its three Oregon data center campuses are sourced with 83% carbon-free energy.

Google is also pursuing other emerging technologies, including advanced nuclear power and long-duration energy storage, as part of its efforts to achieve net-zero emissions.

Quaise said in its 2025 year-end update that it had drilled 118 meters through granite at its central Texas test site and is now working toward a depth of 1 kilometer.

#5688. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5684. Pacific Power Begins New Customer Protections Against Data Centers

Regulators approve new agreement to ensure data centers pay for their own energy costs

SALEM, Oregon — The Oregon Public Utility Commission has approved an agreement between advocates and Pacific Power. Now, Pacific Power customers have a strong suite of protections against data centers’ impact on Oregonians’ energy bills.

Starting Jan. 1, 2027, new Pacific Power data centers will be held responsible for their own energy costs. These new rules will only apply to future data centers and existing ones making significant investments, which regulators identified as the most pressing challenge to address. Right now, the utility has very few existing data centers on its system.

“With a long line of data centers waiting at Pacific Power’s door, I’m confident that we have the right protections at the right time,” said Bob Jenks, Executive Director of Oregon Citizens’ Utility Board. “We were able to stop more extreme impacts on power bills just in time.”

Data centers over 100 MW will also have to pay a small surcharge to fund energy efficiency upgrades for low-income households. This includes funding for deep home retrofits, lowering energy use across the grid while saving customers on monthly bills.

“Many of the households we serve are already struggling to afford their energy bills and risk having their utilities shut off. Data centers will help fund weatherization, energy efficiency upgrades, and the critical home repairs that often prevent our clients from accessing these improvements in the first place,” said Charity Fain, Executive Director of Community Energy Project. “Requiring data centers to cover the costs of the energy infrastructure they need, rather than passing those costs on to residential customers, is an important protection for low-income households, seniors, and people with disabilities.”

Under this order, if there is a power emergency, Pacific Power can prioritize critical energy use for other customers, like renters and homeowners, by curtailing service to data centers. Increased electric demand by data centers strains the grid and increases the potential for blackouts during heat waves or hard freezes.

“This is a big win that puts Oregon at the forefront of managing data center growth the right way,” said Joshua Basofin, Oregon Clean Energy Program Director at Climate Solutions. “It protects Oregonians from rising costs and strengthens grid resilience. Data centers should not harm our ability to access essential electricity to power our lives and keep us safe all year.”

Regulators also ruled that new data centers can only be connected to Pacific Power’s grid if there is enough emissions-free electricity available to serve them. Under HB 2021, Pacific Power is required by law to serve all customers with 100% emissions-free electricity by 2040. This new rule will help manage costs for other customers outside of data centers during the transition to clean energy.

“We have long known that clean energy is the most affordable form of energy,” said Ben Brint, Senior Climate Program Director at Oregon Environmental Council. “This ruling is a win for data center accountability, and for Oregonians across the state — especially frontline communities already bearing the brunt of climate impacts and rising energy costs. It will save ratepayers money in the short and long run, allocating costs appropriately while advancing Oregon’s 100% clean energy goals.”

This change also comes at a time when many Oregonians are calling for broader changes to how the state handles data centers. Impacts on data centers are poised to be an issue again at the Oregon State Legislature in 2027.

“The POWER Act demonstrated the people’s power to fight back against the rising energy rates and ecosystem harms that large data centers inflict,” said Emily Bowes, Policy Strategist & Acting Director at Sierra Club Oregon. “In 2027, alongside new and returning climate champions, we will continue to fight for a clean energy transition in Salem and statewide.”

In 2025, the Oregon Legislature passed the POWER Act to help utilities rein in data centers’ added costs to Oregonians’ energy bills. Last fall, Portland General Electric began the work to roll out this new law in practice. In January, Pacific Power started a similar process for its customers. The next step is to address the few existing data centers on Pacific Power’s system, starting as early as summer 2027. It is unclear if the final phase will include rate adjustments.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.
