#5681. Volkswagen ID Buzz Made For Sleeping In

The Volkswagen ID Buzz has not been a big hit in the US (where it’s just too expensive and doesn’t even provide a lot of range), but it’s still one of my favorite vehicles. I would certainly have one in my driveway if I had enough money for one. Part of what makes the ID Buzz so appealing is how it reminds one of the VW Hippie Bus of yesteryear, but that vehicle was super low cost as well as versatile and useful. The ID Buzz is sort of a deceiving imitation of that model, as it’s quite expensive (especially in the US) and doesn’t really provide a lot of extra utility compared to what’s on the market today.

However, the company has just rolled out a new version of the ID Buzz that is designed to allow for easy sleeping within the van. The “camper” version of the ID Buzz has a few special features for this. Also, just to reiterate, it’s not cheap. So it’s more a little luxury camper van for people with money than a way to get around and live freely for young people scraping by and vagabonds. The ID Buzz Beach, as it’s called, will start at around £54,000 in the UK. (Whether it will come to the US and at what price, who knows?) It includes the following:

Interestingly, you can get this camper version of the van in either the 5-seat or 7-seat version of the ID Buzz.

Is this going to attract a lot of buyers? Almost definitely no. Is it super cool, though? I think so.

#5680. Texas finalizes ERCOT CEO contract extension, pay raise tied to performance

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

CEO Pablo Vegas could take home more than $4 million next year. The contract extension and pay raise were put on hold in September following public outcry.

The Public Utility Commission of Texas on Thursday filed an order finalizing a roughly 7% base pay raise and potential performance-based bonuses worth millions more for Pablo Vegas, the CEO of the Electric Reliability Council of Texas.

PUCT officials said the contract is “in line with professional peers nationwide.”

The contract was signed in September but put on hold following public outcry, including objections from Texas Lt. Gov. Dan Patrick, who said on social media, “during a time of increasing utility costs, this is not the time to give the CEO of ERCOT a multi-million-dollar pay raise.”

Neither Patrick nor Texas Gov. Greg Abbott responded to a request for comment on the contract’s finalization. Both lost elections in November and will leave office in January.

ERCOT filings with the IRS show Vegas earned a total of about $3.6 million in 2024 and 2025, with a base salary above $1 million for each year.

His new contract could see him earn $6.47 million this year, according to a September presentation to the ERCOT board, though only about $4 million would be paid in 2027 with some potentially due later based on long-term incentives. The new contract runs from 2027 through 2032 and includes base pay of $1.16 million, plus an annual short-term performance incentive of up to 100% of his base salary and an annual long-term incentive up to 180% of his base pay. There is also a $1.38 million “make-whole” payment due in 2027, related to agreements in his previous contract.

The performance incentives are set by the ERCOT board. The compensation agreement “ensures that pay follows performance, where incentive compensation is earned each year only by meeting key grid reliability and performance targets and unpaid incentives are forfeited if the CEO is terminated for cause or resigns without good reason,” PUCT Chairman Thomas Gleeson said in a statement. “This is not a guaranteed payout.”

Vegas has served as president and CEO of the Texas grid operator since August 2022.

#5679. Leapmotor Produces Its 2 Millionth EV

Leapmotor just produced its 2 millionth vehicle, all EVs of course. The EV startup has seen a meteoric rise in the highly competitive Chinese EV market, and increasingly abroad. Of course, most of us in the US and even in Europe know nothing or next to nothing about Leapmotor, but it has clearly been more effective than hundreds or even thousands of other companies that have tried to launch and scale up EV production.

One interesting thing to note regarding this milestone: it’s peanuts compared to Leapmotor’s plans for the next few years. In 2026 alone, Leapmotor plans to produce one million vehicles, and it’s on track to do so. That means that, this year, the company plans to produce as many vehicles as half of Leapmotor’s cumulative total at the moment. So, it will just be sometime in the middle of next year that the company will reach 3 million cumulative EVs produced.

The company is now producing more than 100,000 vehicles a month — for four months running.

Naturally, the company’s plan is to keep growing. As part of that effort, it just launched a new model, the Lafa 5 compact hatchback, with pre-sales starting earlier today. Also, another new model, the D19 SUV, just had its 50,000th unit produced.

So, yes, keep an eye on Leapmotor. It’s already a wildly successful young EV company, and it looks like it is going to continue growing fast.

#5677. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

#5676. Market reforms bolster grid, may lower costs this winter: FERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Improved coordination in the West will help offset lower hydropower output due to historically low water levels in the Colorado River basin, while New England risks overreliance on oil, FERC said.

Electric market reforms in the West and Northeast may bolster grid reliability and, in some cases, lower costs this winter, according to the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s annual winter market and reliability report, released Thursday.

In the West, the California Independent System Operator launched the Extended Day Ahead Market and the Southwest Power Pool expanded into the Western Interconnection earlier this year.

“EDAM and SPP’s expansion should provide ratepayers in much of the West with production cost savings and improved reliability in winter 2026-2027, particularly during cold weather events,” FERC staff said in the report.

This winter, the markets’ improved coordination of generation and reduced need for reserve power supplies will help offset lower power output from the Glen Canyon and Hoover dams due to historically low water levels in the Colorado River basin, staff said.

“On a day-to-day basis, these conditions can create congestion and affect system operators’ ability to respond to voltage support challenges, event response mitigation needs, or any additional grid stressors,” staff said in the report.

About 45% of the continental United States and 95% of the Intermountain West region is in a drought, according to the report.

In the Northeast, ISO New England lowered its capacity performance pay rate, which should lower electricity prices during emergency conditions or capacity performance events this winter, staff said.

Also, the New York Independent System Operator changed its installed capacity market rules to reflect uncertainty about how much power Hydro-Québec will deliver over the 1,250-MW Champlain Hudson Power Express transmission line in the winter, they noted.

The U.S. subsidiary of the Canadian utility Hydro-Québec — the primary reservation holder — has an obligation to serve native load in its Canadian territory, which is a winterpeaking system.

This obligation, along with ongoing reduced runoff to its hydroelectric resources, “has left little or no surplus capacity to export to NYISO in recent years’ peak winter months,” staff said.

The Northeast — especially New England — also faces risks around its reliance on oil-fired power in the winter, according to the report.

On Jan. 25, for example, oil-fired generation reached 7,864 MW in a single hour, supplying about 40% of ISO-NE’s total net generation in that period, staff noted.

However, U.S. and regional distillate fuel supplies are low, according to the report. New England’s supplies were 46% below the prior five-year average as of early October, staff said.

FERC expects natural gas prices will be lower this winter compared to last winter across most of the U.S., in part because of higher storage inventories in some areas and forecasts for warmer weather caused by El Niño, according to the report.

As of Nov. 10, Henry Hub gas prices — a key U.S. benchmark — were trading at $3.19 per million British thermal units for the upcoming winter, 42% below last winter’s settled price of $5.52/MMBtu, staff said.

FERC staff expects power plant gas demand to increase by 0.8 billion cubic feet per day, or about 2%, to 35.7 Bcf/d on average this winter compared to last winter. Power burn is expected to account for 25% of total gas demand this winter, according to the report.

#5674. Budderfly to invest $500M to finance and operate municipal energy upgrades

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The energy-as-a-service company will recoup its investment from savings over a contract’s life. Such agreements have become more common, especially for institutions like universities, one expert said.

Energy-as-a-service contracts are not new, especially for institutions like universities and hospitals, said Eric Smith, professor of practice and associate director of the Tulane Energy Institute at Tulane University. They can provide resiliency in addition to savings, he said.

He pointed to Tulane’s arrangement with Enfra, announced in 2022, in which the company paid for new energy assets and equipment, including a 1-MW solar generation facility, while the school got guaranteed annual cost savings over the 30-year term of the contract and help achieving its emissions-reduction goals.

The upgrades also make the school less vulnerable to power outages from hurricanes that regularly hit Louisiana, which ultimately helps protect students, he said.

“What you're really operating is a job shop to do that equipment optimization and manage it over some period of time,” Smith said of the services companies like Enfra and Budderfly offer.

“The way you get paid for that is to demonstrate that you're saving the ratepayer money,” he continued. “Now we have enough power on campus. We can island the campus and run the whole campus based on the power that they generate with our existing power plant.”

Budderfly’s Edwards said his company started out offering energy management contracts to small and medium-sized businesses before expanding to local governments.

Although governments have access to lower-cost financing options, municipal budget and staffing constraints are a challenge for long-term investments in energy efficiency, he said.

“There's very little appetite anywhere to increase taxes to pay for energy infrastructure,” Edwards said.

The company has already deployed the financing model in Cadillac, Michigan, where it invested $4.4 million in upgrades. Budderfly will use Schneider Electric’s energy management and automation technologies to integrate and manage the energy infrastructure improvements.

It is offering local governments a 15-year contract at a locked-in rate based on its 12- to 24-month historical use average. Budderfly becomes the utility’s customer of record and pays the customer’s utility bill, taking a cut of the savings achieved from upgraded, more-efficient equipment.

If bill savings extend beyond a 30% goal, those are shared with the municipality 50/50, Edwards said. Some contracts also have opportunities for virtual power plant programs and to share additional revenue from demand-side management programs, he added.

#5673. AGCO Celebrates Construction Milestone at Olmedo Solar Project in Spain

OLMEDO, Spain, Nov. 19, 2026 — AGCO (NYSE: AGCO) today announced that Project Olmedo, the solar installation at the center of its first Virtual Power Purchase Agreement (VPPA), has completed construction and commissioning and is expected to begin delivering renewable electricity to the Spanish power grid in the coming months, pending final regulatory approvals. BRUC, one of the largest renewable energy groups in Spain, built the new solar installation in the northwest province of Valladolid.

The milestone follows AGCO’s September 2025 announcement of its 10-year VPPA with BRUC. The installation’s two solar projects, Los Silos and El Granero, span approximately 300 hectares (741 acres) and hold approximately 150,000 solar panels. AGCO’s portion of the installation’s annual production is expected to be approximately 150 gigawatt-hours (GWh) of renewable electricity a year, equivalent to the annual electricity consumption of approximately 40,000 European households, based on Eurostat data.

“Project Olmedo will give AGCO long-term price certainty for the majority of the electricity certificate cost in Europe,” said Tim Millwood, Senior Vice President, Chief Supply Chain Officer. “In volatile energy markets, securing that stability for 10 years strengthens the resilience of our supply chain while supporting new renewable generation.”

A VPPA lets a company support new renewable energy projects without taking direct delivery of the electricity they generate. Because AGCO’s agreement supported the construction of a brand-new installation, it will add new renewable capacity to the grid rather than drawing on existing sources, a principle known as additionality. The agreement also helps reduce AGCO’s Scope 2 greenhouse gas emissions from purchased electricity in its Europe/Middle East (EME) region and supports its commitment to reduce absolute Scope 1 and 2 emissions by 55% by 2033 from a 2022 baseline.

“Additionality is what makes this agreement truly meaningful,” said Roger Batkin, Senior Vice President, Corporate Affairs, General Counsel & Corporate Secretary. “We did not simply buy renewable electricity that already existed; we supported the construction of a brand-new renewable solar project. Accomplishing this project required over two years of involvement from many teams, functions, and areas of expertise across AGCO, along with technical advisory support from Schneider Electric and a strong partnership with BRUC.”

Reflecting AGCO’s Farmer-First strategy, Project Olmedo supports local farmers to further diversify their land use by leasing land for the project while maintaining the option for livestock to graze among the panels, maintaining the vegetation in place of machinery.

AGCO and BRUC leaders, joined by local community representatives José Emilio Escudero García, Mayor of Bocigas, and Ursino Vara Pérez, Mayor of Almenara de Adaja, marked the milestone with a ribbon-cutting ceremony at the site in November. Additional drone footage from Project Olmedo and the ribbon-cutting event are available on AGCO’s YouTube page.

For more details on AGCO’s sustainability initiatives, visit the company’s sustainability webpage and read the 2025 Sustainability Report. For more information about BRUC, visit the company’s website at brucenergy.com.

AGCO (NYSE: AGCO) is a global leader in agricultural machinery and precision agriculture technologies. Driven by a Farmer-First strategy, AGCO delivers value through its differentiated leading brands, Fendt™, Massey Ferguson™, PTx™ and Valtra™.  AGCO’s high-performance equipment and smart farming solutions, including brand-agnostic retrofit technologies and autonomous offerings, empower farmers to drive productivity while sustainably feeding the world. For more information, visit www.agcocorp.com.

#5671. Permitting reform bill heads to House as Democratic divisions persist

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Some Democrats say the party should not agree to a compromise bill that contains what they consider troubling changes to environmental law and state and tribal authority.

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, which proposes sweeping changes to energy infrastructure permitting, faces an uncertain path in the U.S. House of Representatives after passing the Senate in a 62-33 vote Thursday night.

The bill was supported by 47 Republicans and 15 members of the Democratic caucus. It was negotiated by Democratic and Republican lawmakers, along with the White House — but that negotiation preceded the midterm elections where Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress. Now, some Democrats say the party should not agree to a bill that contains what they consider troubling changes to environmental law and the authorities of courts, states and tribes.

Sen. Raphael Warnock, D-Ga., was among the senators who voted against the bill. He said in a statement that he had “hoped that a robust amendment process could’ve addressed these shortcomings in the bill that Georgians have rightly raised ... but in its absence, I am voting no.”

“I believe we can get a better bill when a new Congress is sworn in this January,” Warnock added.

On the House side, Rep. Scott Peters, D-Calif., rebuked this line of thinking.

“There will be no better deal on the table,” he said in a statement. “With Donald Trump in the White House and 60 required votes in the Senate, bipartisanship is unavoidable, whether it’s this Congress or next. The status quo isn’t working.”

Peters also criticized Sens. Ron Wyden, D-Ore., and Jeff Merkley, D-Ore., for blocking voting on all other amendments after Wyden’s amendment to ban data centers on public lands was left off of the list of amendments to vote on.

“Unfortunately two Senators, at the last minute, opposed bipartisan amendments to make a good bill better for tribal partners,” Peters said. “We should make sure this bill gets across the finish line to lower costs and emissions and then continue our work to make the progress they opposed, and more.”

“It’s unimaginable that after this month’s election, when the entire country demanded lower costs, we would whiff on the opportunity right in front of us,” he added.

National Rural Electric Cooperative Association CEO Jim Matheson celebrated the bill’s passage in a Thursday statement, noting that earlier this month, NRECA sent a letter to the Senate “expressing strong support for the bill provided it was amended to exempt electric co-ops, which are locally governed and controlled, from unnecessary transmission regulation by the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission.”

“The final bill passed by the Senate includes this exemption, securing NRECA’s continuing support,” Matheson said. “This legislation will dramatically improve the federal permitting system, making it more manageable and predictable for electric co-ops.”

The Edison Electric Institute, which represents investor-owned utilities, previously expressed support for the bill on Nov. 9 when it passed its first cloture vote in the Senate.

Some prominent environmental groups continue to oppose the bill.

The Natural Resources Defense Council issued a statement Thursday saying the bill “remains unacceptable.”

“Lawmakers should oppose this bill and try again next year to pass legislation worthy of the American people, not for the fossil fuel industry and data center companies,” said NRDC President and CEO Manish Bapna.

Some of the criticism of the bill, as well as its support, is bipartisan.

Sen. Josh Hawley, R-Mo., who voted against the bill Thursday, said it was “stuffed with giveaways to data centers and industry at the expense of farmers.”

Sen. Catherine Cortez Masto, D-Nev., voted no and criticized the bill for a lack of consideration for tribal nations.

“The version of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act that passed the Senate today ignores Tribal input and makes changes to the National Historic Preservation Act that Tribes across the country do not support,” she said. “I’m disappointed that Republicans wouldn’t allow this section to be struck from the bill.”

Correction: A previous version of this story erred in describing Sen. Catherine Cortez Masto’s vote on the bill. Cortez Masto voted no.

#5669. Nextnova unveils solar cell manufacturing facility

The new state-of-the-art factory in Oklahoma City will create about 300 jobs, strengthen the domestic solar supply chain, and establish a new benchmark for transparency and quality.

This month, Nextnova celebrated the official inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City, marking an important milestone in strengthening America’s domestic solar manufacturing industry.

The facility will manufacture high-efficiency solar cells using advanced automation, Industry 4.0 technologies, and comprehensive digital traceability.

Production is scheduled to begin in March 2027 with an initial annual capacity of 2 GW, designed to expand to 5 GW as market demand grows.

The new factory is expected to create approximately 300 skilled jobs in engineering, manufacturing, quality assurance, and operations, supporting economic growth while reinforcing the U.S. clean energy supply chain.

Nextnova celebrates the inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City. Credit: Nextnova

“Today represents an exciting milestone for Nextnova and for American solar manufacturing,” said Nathan Kii Miller, Nextnova’s chief strategy officer, in a statement. “Our Oklahoma City facility combines advanced manufacturing technology, complete supply chain transparency, and rigorous quality standards to deliver premium solar cells while strengthening a resilient domestic solar industry.”

Designed for maximum quality and reliability, the facility features fully integrated manufacturing execution systems (MES), automated inspection technologies, advanced process control, and a dedicated analytical laboratory supporting continuous quality verification throughout production.

Nextnova notes that its commitment to transparent sourcing, manufacturing excellence, and scalable production provides customers with high-performance solar cells that support the continued growth of a secure and competitive U.S. solar industry.

#5666. Sierra Club Reacts to Senate Passage of BAAJA: House Must Reject the Senate Fast-Tracked Bill

The American People Deserve a Better Deal

Washington, DC — Today, the Senate passed the “Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026” following little debate and an insufficient amendment process. BAAJA will now go to the House of Representatives for consideration.

In response, Sierra Club Executive Director Loren Blackford released the following statement:

“While a rogue president is waging a war on clean energy, the Senate rushed a deeply flawed bill that furthers the buildout of fossil fuels, strips away power from communities and Tribes, threatens our clean water and public lands, and endangers wildlife. The House of Representatives has an equal voice in the legislative process. They must reject the Senate’s fast-tracked bill and demand debate and consideration to deliver for the people, not corporate polluters and Donald Trump. The American people made a clear call merely weeks ago: our elected leaders must address soaring costs and a corrupt system that prioritizes corporate polluter profits over our health, our wallets, and our future.

“The Sierra Club thanks the Senators, Tribes, and advocates across the country who raised their voices in the fight to fix this bill. Americans deserve a better deal.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5662. The EV Takeover Is Taking Shape, Despite Heroic Efforts To Stop It

Whelp, the AI boom has had one positive knock-on effect. It is creating new multi-millionaires by the bushel, any one of whom can afford to splurge on a pricey new electric vehicle. That aside, EV stakeholders in the US are springing back to life after a year-long slumber. Even General Motors is in play, stirring up long lost memories of its 1990’s-era EV1 debacle instead of hiding its head in shame. What’s up with that?

I’m just going to stick my neck out and say that the eponymous Aptera solar-powered autocycle from the US startup Aptera will attract enough AI-enriched drivers to make a go of it. The on-again, off-again, on-again automaker has been on this year, working towards volume production of a plug-in, three-wheeled two-seater EV that can travel up to 40 miles on its own solar-plus-storage system (see more Aptera news here).

Autocycles were once common here in the US and overseas markets, distinguished from motorcycles by their front-facing seats and steering wheels, rather than straddled seats and handlebars. They fell out of favor in the US by the middle of the 20th century, but 21st century hot-rodders have revived the form with a focus on eyebrow-raising, open-air, gas-guzzling showpieces. In particular, interest has spread in states that do not require autocycle drivers to invest in an additional motorcycle license.

The Aptera is showy in its own right, resembling a rocket ship with unique aerodynamic styling. Perhaps it may even show up in the next James Bond adventure with James Pattinson at the wheel. In the meantime, the company has just made good on its pledge to reward early investors in the world’s first solar-powered car with the first production models to role off the assembly line. Aptera #1 goes to the founder and CEO of GDP Venture, Martin Hartono, aka “Accelerator #1.”

Drivers can reserve an Aptera of their own for just $100, and they can expect to pay about $40,000 for the ride. Considering that the solar fuel is free, anyone in the mood for a head-turning ride will get quite a bargain for their $40,000 compared to other competitors in the zero emission two-seater field, such as the recently refreshed Tesla Roadster.

Speaking of refresh, General Motors has been weirdly lavishing attention on the EV1 sedan all this year, even though the automaker has been roundly criticized for the killing the short-lived car in its cradle. The automaker began producing EV1 electric sedans starting in 1996, following the unveiling of its Impact electric concept car in 1990. Unlike the Impact, EV1 was designed for use on the road. However, it was also designed mainly for the California market, and primarily for lease, not for sale. Production halted in 2003 at just about 1,000 vehicles, all to be recalled by GM and sent to the crusher as chronicled in Chris Paine’s 2006 documentary, “Who Killed The Electric Car?”

Or, maybe not so weird after all. GM’s electrification journey long predates the launch of Tesla Motors in 2003. The legacy automaker is now determined to stake out its claim on automotive history, with 2026 marking the 30th anniversary of the start of EV1 production.

GM has been celebrating the anniversary all this year with multiple long form articles about the Impact and EV1 on its website, eager to remind everybody that its industry-leading vehicle electrification efforts of the 1990’s continue to manifest decades later, in the form of heat pumps for climate control, by-wire technology, and regenerative braking among other elements.

GM’s latest love letter to the EV1 popped up on November 17, when the automaker officially celebrated the completed restoration of the EV1 “V212” alongside its a private owner, Billy Caruso. The rare survivor of GM’s the junkyard crusher was evidently hiding out in an impound lot in Georgia all these years, until Caruso acquired it at auction and enlisted Jared Pink of the popular show The Questionable Garage to fix it up. Considering TQG’s large following on YouTube, GM wisely lent its engineers to the effort, along with several donated parts from its own EV1.

US President Donald Trump and the Republican majority in Congress have made a heroic effort to dial down the vehicle electrification movement, but facts on the ground keep getting in their way. For starters, today’s advanced batteries apply across multiple energy resources, fossil and non-fossil alike. So, it’s no surprise to see the US Department of Energy continuing to throw taxpayer dollars at new energy storage projects (here’s one example), even though some of that activity will eventually ripple into the vehicle electrification field.

The US Department of Defense is also getting into the act. DoD’s electrification journey has been a tedious one, but in recent years it has begun creeping stealthily towards a vision of ground mobility enabled by electricity, providing the key tactical advantages of silent running and a low heat signature.

The latest news on that score involves a $1.4 billion loan commitment from DoD’s Office of Strategic Capital (OSC), enabling the silicon-based battery materials startup Sila to vault its signature product into commercial production. DoD’s primary interest in the company consists of applications in the electric drone and autonomous mobility fields, but Sila has long been eyeballing the EV market as well.

Sila secured the loan commitment in August, and it has not let the grass grow under its feet. On November 18 the company announced an off-take agreement with the well-known Tesla EV battery supplier Panasonic Energy Corporation of North America. “The multi-year agreement is part of a long-term commercial relationship with the potential for over $1 billion of offtake over time,” Sila explained in a press statement, further elaborating that Panasonic will deploy its proprietary Titan Silicon silicon-carbon anode material in battery cells.

“The partnership is also expected to support significant manufacturing and construction job creation in the United States. With Sila’s U.S.-based anode production underpinned by proprietary American technology and know-how, Panasonic and its OEM customers gain a clearer path toward a more secure, resilient, and sustainable domestic supply chain,” Sila emphasized.

That’s…interesting! Aside from Tesla as one of Sila’s OEM customers, keep an eye on another Panasonic offtaker, the up-and-comer US startup Harbinger Motors, which also has a foot in the DoD door.

Photo: The vehicle electrification movement is far from over in the US, with the startup Aptera among the stakeholders betting on an EV sales rebound in the coming years (cropped, courtesy of Aptera).

#5658. Is AI A Public Or A Private Asset?

Isabelle Ferreras is a professor and research director at the University of Louvain, an Accelerator fellow at the Institute for Ethics in AI at the University of Oxford, and a senior research associate at the Center for Labor and a Just Economy at Harvard University. It safe to say she knows a thing or two about computer technology and AI.

She told The Guardian this week that she has grave concerns about the way AI and the corporations promoting it are managing this brave new technology. Her concerns are spurred by the number of high profile computer engineers who have quit recently over fears that AI development is out of control.

Last month, a person responsible for overseeing safety reports at OpenAI left the company, claiming its culture was “broken.” He added that the entire AI industry was careless when it comes to developing AI technology. The month before that, a researcher at Anthropic — which went public yesterday at a valuation $1.8 trillion dollars — warned that AI “could kill us all by the end of the decade.”

“When the only way for a conscientious engineer to raise the alarm is to leave,” Ferreras said. “it is a clear sign that corporate governance in the industry is broken. Each departure takes with it the very knowledge needed to steer these systems safely. We cannot regulate AI safely from the outside if the companies building it remain autocracies on the inside.”

Ferreras warned that governments think of AI either as a “consumer product to be tested for defects or as a geopolitical race to be won by relaxing rules on business. Corporate lobbyists encourage this view as a trade-off between safety and innovation by arguing that legislation imposes too many burdens.

“This is a dangerous path. AI is not merely a software product. It is a technology of government. Yet inside frontier labs such as OpenAI, Anthropic, Microsoft, and Google, a handful of executives and capital investors decide which values are coded into models that shape millions of lives,” she said.

Interestingly, Michael Connolly, the award winning author of the Bosch and Lincoln Lawyer novels, published a book last year in which the family of a young woman, who was murdered by her boyfriend after he interacted with an AI agent, sues the company behind the AI agent. The essence of the lawsuit is that AI only does what it is programmed to do, and since the lead programmer of this particular AI agent was a misogynist in real life, it reflected his values when it interacted with humans. The book is called The Proving Ground and it is a fascinating read.

While Ferreras applauds those who warn of the malignancies built into AI, she pointed out that humans have short attention spans. “Time and again, a safety researcher or engineer walks out of the door with a public warning that commercial pressures are overriding safety guardrails. As a society, we applaud the courage of these individuals and debate their warnings for a news cycle — and then we move on. But we miss the deeper institutional failure those resignations reveal. As the great political scientist Albert Hirschman made clear, exit is not voice.”

In a democratic society, the exercise of governing power requires the consent of the governed, Ferreras told The Guardian. “Yet corporate law still treats firms as the property of shareholders, and the labor investors, who put their intellect, health and lives into the firm, as subordinates. To align AI with the public interest, we need a dual strategy: alongside solid public regulations on AI ‘from the outside,’ we need to enter the corporate black box and force corporate governance into alignment with democratic principles.”

An analogy can be made to the Federal Communications Commission, which licenses broadcasters and promotes the free flow of information to the public. The digital world thinks such controls are archaic, but the lack of them is a clear and present danger to a civil society.

Ferreras suggested that “governments must outlaw non-disclosure and non-disparagement clauses that silence staff on safety risks, and enact robust whistle blower protection. Tech workers also need a collective standing over corporate strategy, independent channels to report safety breaches, and formal representation inside frontier labs before models are deployed.”

In addition, she argued that “legislators must establish a binding right to co-decision over workplace AI. No algorithmic system that monitors, evaluates or disciplines workers should be deployed without prior negotiation. At national and EU level, we need to consider collective bargaining on AI deployment and veto rights for works councils or trade unions, backed by independent technical experts and a strict ban on automated firing.”

Lastly, she supported the democratization of boardrooms and rewarding firms that refuse to concentrate power at the top. “Legislators should mandate worker-elected directors on boards, moving towards dual-majority governance in which strategic decisions require the consent not only of capital but of labor.” Some, like Elon Musk and Mark Zuckerberg, would characterize her suggestions as liberal claptrap, and they would be correct. Ferreras would happily unravel the “tech bro” manosphere in order to promote a more open and just society.

She pointed to the research by Daron Acemoglu and Simon Johnson, the 2024 Nobel laureates in economics, which demonstrates that technological revolutions do not automatically produce shared prosperity. Instead, institutions decide who benefits and who pays.

“Extending democracy into the firm is not an old battle over power for its own sake, but a practical necessity for more ethical judgment. Aligning AI with the good of workers and society requires more wisdom than closed executive rooms can offer. It needs what the Yale political theorist Hélène Landemore calls ‘democratic reason’ — the collective intelligence of diverse, deliberating voices, brought to bear on corporate decision-making,” Ferreras wrote.

“Left to self-regulation and the occasional whistleblower, AI governance will entrench an unaccountable algorithmic oligarchy. Accountability and alignment begins with giving those who build these systems and those whose work they govern an enforceable voice, from the code floor to the boardroom.”

Ferreras’ point of view is decidedly liberal, and yet her arguments are clear and concise. How can we give so much power to digital agents and not take reasonable steps to ensure the needs of society as a whole are respected? The tech industry enjoys almost unfettered freedom of action today. It may be time to consider whether that is a good long term strategy.

Steve writes about the interface between technology and sustainability from his home in Florida or anywhere else The Force may lead him. He is proud to be "woke" and believes weak leaders push others down while strong leaders lift others up. You can follow him on Substack at https://stevehanley.substack.com/ but not on Fakebook or any social media platforms controlled by narcissistic yahoos.

#5656. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.

#5654. The Metals Company Has a Seabed Mining Production Gap That Its Pilot Has Not Closed

In 2022, The Metals Company (TMC) and offshore contractor Allseas recovered 3,021 wet tons of nodules from the Pacific seabed. Their integrated test system moved material up a riser stretching more than four kilometres from the ocean floor to the Hidden Gem. During production runs, the collector achieved 86.4 wet tons per hour. Four years later, TMC is discussing a million tons a year from an upgraded system and eventually 12 million tons annually across four vessels.

What matters between those endpoints is the operating record: sustained collection rates, marine maintenance, vessel availability and delivered tons sufficient to carry fixed costs. TMC has yet to demonstrate those economics.

The November 12, 2026 corporate update describes a phased approach: begin with an upgraded pilot collector and limited modifications to Hidden Gem, then invest in larger systems as operations develop. Phasing reduces the initial capital commitment. It also pushes the demonstration of the projected commercial production rates into the future.

The annual arithmetic is straightforward. At 5,000 productive hours, about 57% of a calendar year, a system collecting 200 wet tons per hour delivers one million tons. The second-year target of 1.5 million tons requires 300 tons per hour. Three million tons from one production system requires 600 tons per hour. TMC’s 2022 production-run rate of 86.4 tons per hour would deliver 432,000 tons at that utilization.

The upgraded machines are expected to be different from the 2022 test collector. Their performance must be demonstrated, not assumed from the pilot. The operating gap is central to the project economics, and it grows with production ambitions.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines TMC’s production gap alongside Impossible Metals’ autonomous collection model and the investment consequences of underperforming equipment. The source assessment and fleet sensitivities are linked there.

A collector, a multi-kilometre hydraulic riser and a production vessel have to work together. The crawler encounters uneven ground and varying nodule coverage. The riser and pumps handle abrasive slurry. The vessel must remain on station while crews inspect, maintain and repair equipment. Offshore access and weather affect when those activities can occur.

A system can produce at its test rate and still lose the year on maintenance and interruptions. Fewer productive hours push the required hourly rate higher. Repeating a successful short production run does not establish a year’s availability, let alone multiple years of fleet productivity.

The difference matters because capital and offshore operating costs continue while production is interrupted. Ships, crews, marine maintenance equipment and financing cost money whether or not nodules are arriving on deck. Smaller annual production spreads fixed expenses over fewer tons and raises the cost of every ton recovered.

TMC’s August 2025 prefeasibility presentation estimated offshore collection and transport at $179 per dry ton, or about $129 per wet ton under its 28% moisture assumption. Those are projections for a future system. They are not an audited cost record from continuous industrial mining. The revised first-production configuration needs its own cost data.

The missing commercial evidence is a long enough operating record to estimate productive hours, rate degradation, planned and unplanned maintenance, retrieval time, replacement costs and the delivered cost per ton. Until then, moving from a pilot to a production forecast remains a major assumption.

Impossible Metals proposes to collect individual nodules with autonomous underwater vehicles rather than a tracked crawler and riser. A vehicle would descend, select nodules, fill its cargo compartment, return, undergo surface recovery, unload and prepare for another mission. Every trip must be repeated across an entire fleet.

Its September 2026 scientific trial, reported by Reuters, recovered approximately 450 grams of nodules from more than 4,000 metres depth. It demonstrated selective scientific collection. A commercial robot would have to carry vastly more material and sustain cycle times and availability that the experiment did not measure.

In the 2025 commercial concept examined by metallurgical specialist Lyle Trytten and me, each proposed vehicle carried 12 tons of nodules and had 18 collection arms. At one nodule every 1.5 seconds per arm and an average nodule mass of 100 grams, filling the cargo requires roughly 120,000 successful picks. With all arms continuously working, collection alone occupies approximately 2.8 hours.

The proposed complete mission cycle was four hours. Descent, ascent, searching, repositioning, failed picks, recovery, unloading, inspection and relaunch also consume time. The architecture has too many interdependent activities to fit those requirements into that cycle at the assumed performance.

Autonomous navigation and manipulator tests cannot substitute for evidence of an entire fleet repeatedly executing commercial missions. Increasing payload changes vehicle weight, buoyancy, energy use and recovery equipment. Faster picking leaves the surface operation and deepwater transit to be solved.

Our 2025 independent techno-economic assessment tested the financial effects of weaker-than-assumed robot productivity. At the illustrative $180 per dry ton nodule sales value, the model required approximately $2.52 billion in capital with the originally assumed robot throughput. Halving throughput while maintaining output increased required capital to approximately $4.51 billion.

At a 10% discount rate, the modeled net present value moved from positive $863 million to negative $769 million. Even at two-thirds of original productivity, NPV fell to about $15 million, a negligible margin for a multibillion-dollar first-of-a-kind industrial fleet.

These are scenario calculations, not actual bills or calibrated probabilities. They identify the key failure mode: more robots, recovery capacity, batteries, vessels and maintenance are needed to compensate for each production shortfall. A financial model based on high productivity can look attractive until realistic availability and turnaround times are included.

TMC is further along technologically than Impossible Metals, but its future-scale production economics are also unsupported by a sustained operating record. Neither company has demonstrated the productive hours, throughput and maintenance costs needed to support its advertised commercial proposition.

Policymakers and investors should ask for extended end-to-end offshore operating records and audited unit economics before treating future production targets as investable output. The technology risk exists before accounting for nodule processing, metals markets, permitting or environmental liabilities.

The complete TFIE Briefing and linked assessment show the fleet calculations, technical assumptions and capital sensitivities. The remaining articles in the five-part series address processing, metals markets and American Samoa’s exposure.

#5652. The grid’s hidden bottleneck: engineering capacity

The power system is undergoing an unprecedented transformation. Many of the reasons driving that change are well recognized and frequently discussed. Electricity demand from data centers, manufacturing and the electrification of transportation and building heating and cooling is surging. In fact, earlier this year the North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) warned that grid reliability risks were increasing because demand was outpacing the new generation and transmission needed to serve it.

Another challenge: the grid is old. The most recent American Society of Civil Engineers’ Infrastructure Report Card gave the nation’s energy system a D+, in part because of the age of the grid. Utilities have responded to an aging grid and skyrocketing electricity demand by making significant investments. The Edison Electric Institute (EEI) recently forecast that investor-owned utilities would spend $1.4 trillion through 2030 to meet customer demand for power.

However, increased capital investment does not automatically translate into increased project execution. While funding, equipment availability and construction resources are critical, the engineering processes required to move projects from planning into construction represent another significant constraint. Utilities must not only invest in additional infrastructure but also rethink how that infrastructure is engineered and delivered.

Utilities are under pressure to move more projects through engineering and into construction, but experienced engineering resources are increasingly difficult to secure. While utilities can expand internal teams or engage additional engineering, procurement and construction (EPC) partners, scaling traditional engineering processes often introduces additional challenges. Different design tools, inconsistent engineering standards and repetitive manual work can limit productivity and increase coordination demands. That leaves utilities with a pressing question: how can they execute significantly more capital projects without a proportional increase in engineering resources?

Substation engineering provides a compelling example. While utilities have extensive engineering standards and established design practices, many continue to rely on traditional drawing-based workflows, manual design modifications and disconnected engineering information, particularly in protection and control engineering, where changes can impact multiple interconnected drawings and documents. These processes can become increasingly difficult to scale as project volumes grow, particularly when coordinating across internal engineering teams and external EPC partners.

Engineering doesn’t have to slow down important capital projects. Standardized, data-driven engineering can help utilities move faster while maintaining robust standards at scale. Reusable engineering libraries, automated documentation and connected digital design models streamline engineering work, improve consistency and productivity and reduce repetitive tasks common in traditional workflows, allowing utilities to reuse proven designs rather than repeatedly engineering similar systems from scratch.

Software plays a critical role in navigating the multi-stage, multi-stakeholder complexity of power infrastructure projects, including substations. Eplan, a global provider of electrical engineering software, has spent the past few decades helping utilities and manufacturers standardize their electrical system design and documentation. Its shared digital platform gives utilities, EPCs, panel builders and equipment manufacturers access to the same engineering data, from planning to long-term operations. That shared data supports every phase of a project:

Like many distribution grid operators, naturenergie netze in Germany faces a confluence of challenges. Demand from heat pumps and electric vehicles (EVs) is climbing. At the same time, Germany is working to generate about 80 percent of its electricity from renewables like wind and solar by 2030, which means the grid must handle growing two-way power flows.

For naturenergie netze, that means quickly modernizing existing substations and building new ones. Standardization is central to how the utility plans to keep pace. It began by using Eplan to standardize how it engineers the control systems inside its substations. That groundwork laid the foundation for the utility, Eplan and software supplier entegra to build a digital twin of a highly complex substation, which the utility is using to plan a full replacement of the substation’s control systems while it stays in service.

“It would normally take two to three years to plan and implement the modernization, but the new planning methodology will really speed things up,” said Rainer Beck, grid development coordinator for naturenergie netze. The approach also demonstrates how standardized engineering practices can lay the foundation for greater efficiency and design reuse in future substation projects.

Standardized design has even more value when it’s paired with standardized hardware and highly integrated production tools. Rittal, Eplan’s sister company, manufactures modular power distribution systems and automated fabrication equipment that can work directly from Eplan’s engineering data to extend efficiency directly to the production floor. It’s a combination that supports speed and quality across design, build and delivery.

Building on its experience in Europe, Eplan is now supporting North American utilities and their engineering partners in modernizing protection and control engineering workflows, establishing scalable engineering standards and improving engineering productivity to support expanding capital programs with a growing USA-based manufacturing and warehousing footprint.

The grid is being transformed from how power is generated to how it flows, but the engineering processes behind it must also change. Modernizing them is how utilities can turn unprecedented investment into completed projects without needing an equally unprecedented number of engineers.

Accelerate grid infrastructure: Eplan software and Rittal hardware and automation enable growth, scalability and speed — all with One System.

#5649. Electric Vans — Are They The Final EV Frontier? Kia PV5.

We took a trip to see the Kia PV5 electric van. Our brief takeaway: “Kool like a Kia.” Should keep the bean counters happy.

Inspired by a comment in the Electric Vehicle Council report about electric vans, Majella and I decided to have a short visit to a group of dealerships under the Norris Motors banner. We hoped to check out the LDV eDeliver 5 van, touted as selling for under AU$50,000. However, there was no stock to view and we ended up chatting to the dealer and then moving on to the MG U9 BEV ute. You can read about it here. And then we found the Kia PV5 delivery van.

Julie Delvecchio, the CEO of EVC, calls the lack of affordable electric vans in Australia “the single biggest unclaimed opportunity left.” We think that is about to change. Since its launch in May, 496 PV5 have sold in Australia.

The Kia PV5 reminded me a lot of the Renault Kangoo in both size and shape, and it is able to carry about the same payload. And the front looked like the BMW i3. The one we looked at had been sold and was awaiting a fitout as a camper van. The Norris Group Kia specialist was generous with his time and the information he shared. He has been selling the Kia EV5 for the past 5 months and delivered 5 of the vans. He sold his first one within a week of its arrival on the lot — to Ray, a driver working for Amazon.

Ray appreciated the 390 km range. My friendly sales rep tells me that this will be increased in 2027 models to 420 km. Ray was getting out of diesel and most of his questions were around charging and regen braking. Most of his trips were around the burbs and he was not intending to take the van on the highway. The Kia PV5 is a workhorse, with a passenger car feel and level of comfort. It even has electric lumbar support for the driver — great when you are spending so much time in the van delivering parcels in the lead-up to Christmas. It certainly has a lot more tech than the diesel he was trading in.

In fact, he was “blown away” by the standard features — the 360-degree camera, for example. “Vans are not known for their visibility.” On his return to the dealership for his first service, he commented on the smoothness of the torque: “It has no issues going uphill.” He reported that the van felt “well planted on the road.” Ray has gotten used to trickle charging at home and has lost his range anxiety. Like most new EV drivers, once he started living and working with the vehicle, his fears vanished. The PV5 supports fast charging also for when Ray needs a boost on the road.

But what about that all-important question of cost? Can we make the bean counters happy? The Kia PV5 retails for AU$56,000 (before on-road costs), which is about the same price as comparable diesel vans like the Ford Transit and the Toyota HiAce. Add to that the savings on fuel, the peace of mind from fuel security, and the reduced servicing and it is easy to build a business case for the Kia PV5 — cost per parcel delivery. The PV5 even comes with vehicle to load.

And it isn’t made in China! Can the PV5 replace Toyota’s HiAce? Find the answer in this video. The reviewer points out many features that make the PV5 an attractive option: the charge port is on the front. The van has lots of nooks and crannies for storage. She measures storage by capacity to fit in bottles of wine — for example, two in the glove box. There is a step provided to alight the van, aided by grab handles. More storage space is provided above the steering wheel, including a nifty phone slot. There are customizable buttons on the steering wheel to create shortcuts; for example, to the air conditioning settings. A wireless phone charger is nestled near the dash. The glovebox contains the V2L adaptor supplied with the van.

The fact that the van reverts to the lowest regen setting every time you restart the car is a little niggle. That, and there is no frunk — reducing the reviewer’s all-important need for wine storage. Full specs can be found here. Kia even gives you helpful advice on how to apply for electric vehicle subsidies (last page, bottom right). One of the pictures that caught my eye shows the PV5 as a flatbed truck.

The PV5 was recently named 2026 International Van of the Year. It measures 4695mm long, 2255mm wide, and 1899mm tall on a 2995mm wheelbase. Range is stated as 416 km, but will of course vary with driving style and load. The battery is a 71.2kWh nickel cobalt manganese (NCM) lithium-ion battery. Kia offers a 165,000 km warranty on the battery. The van is front-wheel drive.

The PV5 has over 4 cubic metres of cargo capacity, double both that of the ID Buzz and the Tesla Model Y. Space enough for two euro pallets. There are multiple access points, with sliding doors on both sides and barn doors in the rear. The rear barn doors open to 180 degrees, and a low 419mm load height improves accessibility.

Goods being shipped are protected by padding on the rear doors and insides of the back. The PV5 can carry up to 740 kg. Tiedowns on the floor of the rear of the van help to secure cargo. There is also a power point in the back of the van. Perhaps a tradie can recharge their tools on site. No frunk for wine.

From the spec sheet: “Standard driver assist features include blind-spot collision avoidance, navigation-based adaptive cruise control, lane-keep assist, lane centring with hands-on detection, and parking collision avoidance assist.”

A launch in the USA is financially difficult because of the 25% “chicken tax.” Kia builds the PV5 in South Korea and classifies it as a commercial vehicle, and it faces a 25% US import tariff on light trucks and commercial vans.

Sadly, the Kia PV5 doesn’t come in red, but only in white, grey and black. Most companies may take up the option of wrapping the van in their own livery. Overall, we were impressed with Kia PV5 and look forward to seeing more of them on the road throughout the suburbs as Australian delivery drivers move towards a bright electric future.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5648. FERC approves MISO plan to speed legacy interconnection study process

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Without the rule changes, the Midcontinent Independent System Operator’s queue cycles — totaling about 125 GW of generation and storage — would have been delayed, according to the grid operator.

The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission on Friday approved the Midcontinent Independent System Operator’s plan for clearing out its legacy generation and storage interconnection queue, in part by giving project developers a chance to withdraw nonviable projects without paying a penalty.

The plan is designed to speed up MISO’s review of its “Definitive Planning Phase–2022” interconnection queue, which contains about 35 GW of active projects. Without the approved changes, that queue cycle would have been delayed by roughly 18 months, affecting other pending study cycles, according to MISO.

In total, MISO has almost 600 projects representing nearly 125 GW in its interconnection queue, which consists of four study cycles. The queue contains 44 GW of solar, 25 GW of batteries, about 20 GW of wind, 19 GW of gas and 10 GW of hybrid projects.

“MISO’s proposed tariff revisions accomplish the purposes of the commission’s final rules on generator interconnection … by helping to ensure that interconnection customers are able to interconnect to the transmission system in a reliable, efficient, transparent, and timely manner,” FERC said.

MISO’s rules for its 2022 study cycle — which was not affected by more recent interconnection process reforms — created incentives for nonviable projects to remain in the interconnection queue because by doing so they avoid potential withdrawal penalties, according to the grid operator. This can lead to withdrawals later in the process, which trigger restudies that delay the process for everyone in the queue, MISO said in its Sept. 21 proposal.

To eliminate that problem, the proposal for the 2022 queue cycle would allow projects to exit the interconnection queue penalty-free before “Decision Point II,” which occurred on Oct. 20. MISO’s plan also limits the number of restudies performed in Definitive Planning Phase Phase III to a single restudy for the DPP-2022 cycle and all future DPP cycles.

FERC approved MISO’s plan with an Oct. 19 effective date. Some details remain outstanding pending FERC decisions in two other pending dockets at the agency.

In its decision, FERC approved MISO’s plan to raise the bar for allowing a penalty-free exit later in the review process. Currently, a project can drop out of the queue without penalties if MISO finds it faces a 35% network upgrade cost increase. The proposal increased that threshold to 50%.

FERC rejected arguments from Ranger Power, a renewable energy developer, that MISO’s penalty-free withdrawal proposal rewards unwanted behavior and is discriminatory.

“MISO has demonstrated a need to expeditiously process its queue and reduce the risk of cascading withdrawals and restudies that have delayed previous queue cycles,” FERC said.

FERC also dismissed an assertion from Pathway Power, a clean energy developer, that MISO failed to justify the increased 50% threshold for penalty-free withdrawal. The threshold is consistent with FERC’s approval of a 50% threshold for MISO’s queue process starting with the 2023 study cycle and it doesn’t unfairly burden small project developers, the agency said.

#5644. RISE St. James, Sierra Club Sue LDEQ to Vacate Hyundai Steel Air Permit

LDEQ issued air permit to the facility despite serious flaws in permit.

Donaldsonville, Louisiana — Today, RISE St. James Louisiana and Sierra Club filed a lawsuit challenging Louisiana Department of Environmental Quality’s decision to issue an air permit to Hyundai-POSCO for its proposed steelmaking facility. The groups claim that the air permit must be vacated because LDEQ did not conduct an environmental justice analysis, nor did the agency give full and careful consideration to mitigation measures like electrified components and green hydrogen, or to the environmental costs of Hyundai’s added pollution in an already overburdened area, as is required by the Louisiana state constitution and the Clean Air Act.

Throughout 2026, Sierra Club, working with other experts and local groups, submitted a series of comments to LDEQ in an effort to bring to light the errors, miscalculations, and omissions in the permit application. Despite some improvements to the permit application, made by Hyundai Steel following public pressure, the final air permit issued by LDEQ authorizes the facility to emit substantial quantities of greenhouse gases, nitrogen oxides, particulate matter, carbon monoxide, lead, mercury, ammonia, and other pollutants. Many of these pollutants cause significant and irreversible damage to human health even at low levels.

The area around the planned facility already bears a toxic air pollution burden greater than 96% of the rest of the country, and ranks high across a range of environmental and socioeconomic indicators. The Hyundai Steel Plant is one of multiple polluting facilities planned as part of the 17,000-acre Riverplex MegaPark in an area known as Cancer Alley in Ascension Parish, Louisiana.

In response, the petitioners issued the following statements:

Andrea Issod, Sierra Club Senior Attorney, said: “LDEQ must ensure the Hyundai Steel Plant permit complies with the legal requirements to reduce harmful pollution impacts as much as possible on the surrounding community, which already bears a 96% percentile burden of toxic releases as compared to the national average. By refusing to conduct an environmental justice analysis, consider cleaner technologies like electrifying components and replacing dirty gas with green hydrogen, or consider any environmental costs of Hyundai’s pollution, LDEQ has failed to perform the most basic function of its role and deprived the community of a transparent, thorough, and accurate assessment of the facility’s potential negative impacts. For that reason, we are asking the Court to reverse LDEQ’s final decision and vacate the permit for Hyundai Steel’s proposed facility.”

Mekaylyn Lavigne, Modeste Coordinator for RISE St. James, said: “I was born and raised in the small and peaceful community of Modeste. Although this region is now known as Cancer Alley because many chemical plants have polluted our air and water, my family and neighbors still enjoy sitting on our porches, watching birds and wildlife, and fishing. Now, LDEQ is allowing a steel mill to move in next door to my home, and tells us the air quality is fine and this massive iron and steel making facility will not have any negative impacts. Modeste residents deserve the truth. The court should make LDEQ go back to the drawing board on this permit to disclose all the impacts and use the cleanest technologies to reduce pollution.”

Additional information about the petition:

Rise St. James and Sierra Club note in their petition that Hyundai Steel’s air permit was issued despite LDEQ missing or critically lacking key elements of a public-trust analysis, including:

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5641. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.
