#5639. Is the Energy Crisis Accelerating the Clean Energy Transition? 4 Takeaways from the IEA’s World Energy Outlook 2026

Energy expert Nick Wayth unpacks findings from this year’s report—and what needs to happen next.

The International Energy Agency has published its World Energy Outlook 2026 amid an unprecedented global energy crisis that is reshaping how countries think about energy security. The report finds that the push to reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels is accelerating electrification and the shift toward domestic energy sources, including renewables. Yet electricity demand is surging, grids are struggling to keep pace, and concerns about energy security are also giving coal a longer life in some countries. The IEA warns that the pursuit of energy security alone won’t deliver the emissions reductions needed to meet global climate goals.

What do these findings mean for the energy transition, and where should policymakers focus their efforts? Nick Wayth, global director of the WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition, explains four key takeaways from this year’s report—and what needs to happen next.

Q: How close will current and proposed policies get us to the COP31 pledge to raise electricity’s share of final energy consumption to 35% by 2035? What changes are needed to bridge the gap?

The crisis in the Strait of Hormuz has reinforced the case for electrification as a way for countries to diversify their power sources and reduce dependence on imports, improving energy security and affordability. While the world’s reliance on fossil fuels hasn’t changed substantially, there’s been a real shift in how countries think about the risks of that dependence—and the benefits of electrification. But progress is too slow. The IEA projects that under current and proposed policies, electricity will account for only 29% of final energy consumption by 2035. That’s only half the increase needed to reach the COP31 target of 35%.

Much of the technology is already available. The IEA estimates that nearly half of fossil fuel demand in road transport and more than a third of demand for low- and medium-temperature industrial heat could be electrified at a competitive cost today. The challenge is creating the conditions for businesses and households to make that switch.

To reach the target, countries need to develop detailed plans for shifting transport, heating, and heavy industry toward electricity and invest in transmission and distribution. Governments can help keep their industries competitive by making it more attractive for businesses to switch from gas to affordable electricity. Some countries are doing this successfully already. Germany, for example, has reduced electricity taxes for manufacturers and has subsidized grid charges, helping lower electricity costs for businesses.

Q: The IEA projects rapid growth in electricity demand from both air conditioning and data centers. How do the challenges posed by these two sources of demand differ?

Energy demand from AI and data centers has dominated public attention this year, but by 2035 the global growth in electricity demand from cooling is forecast to be about 70% greater than from data centers. Notably, the IEA finds that global demand for cooling buildings is overtaking demand for heating them for the first time in history.

Energy demand from data centers is concentrated in the U.S., China, and the EU. By contrast, demand for cooling is widespread, especially in emerging and developing economies where much of the population is vulnerable to extreme heat, an increasing threat to human life.

Under current and proposed government policies, cooling is projected to account for 12% of global electricity consumption by 2035. But during periods of peak demand, when millions of air conditioners are running at once, it could reach around 30% of the electricity needed. Electricity grids must be able to cope with these spikes in demand, even if they occur only occasionally. Without better planning and more efficient cooling, countries could face costly grid upgrades—or risk power outages during extreme heat.

This is why policymakers and utilities need to anticipate future electricity demand and plan investments in grid infrastructure accordingly. But building more isn’t the only solution. Governments and utilities should also invest in energy storage and other grid flexibility measures that enable them to adapt to fluctuating supply and demand as needed.

Q: The energy crisis is accelerating clean electrification in some countries but also encouraging greater reliance on fossil fuels in others. What does the IEA’s outlook tell us about the future of oil, coal, and gas—and the risks of investing in new fossil fuel infrastructure?

The report shows that global oil demand is expected to level off by 2030 under current and proposed policies. The slowdown in China’s oil demand, driven largely by electric vehicle adoption, is expected to continue. Other fast-growing economies, such as India, despite electrifying transport, are nonetheless projected to increase their demand for oil.

The current energy crisis is reinforcing these differences in strategy. Some countries are investing in electrification to reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels, while others are turning to domestic fossil fuels.

The energy crisis has given coal demand a short-term boost, and the IEA now expects coal use in 2035 to be higher than it projected just a year ago. Even so, under current and proposed government policies, global coal demand is expected to fall by 14% between 2025 and 2035, driven largely by a decline in coal-fired power generation, particularly in China. But coal use in industry is projected to fall by just 5%, highlighting the challenge of reducing dependence on the fuel.

Countries need to be cautious about investing in infrastructure that may not be needed. The IEA estimates that about 400 billion cubic meters (bcm) of new liquefied natural gas (LNG) export capacity is under construction and could come online by 2035. Yet under current and stated government policies, export capacity could significantly exceed demand in 2035 by70 bcm. That surplus raises the risk of underused infrastructure and investments that fail to pay off.

The IEA estimates that the world will need at least 25 million kilometers of new power lines over the next decade to meet growing electricity demand and connect new sources of power, including renewables. Another 15 million kilometers of existing lines will need to be upgraded or replaced. Both will require substantial investment.

The biggest challenge is delivering these changes fast enough. Major grid projects can take 5 to15 years to plan, secure permits for, and complete—far longer than many renewable energy projects. Governments need to speed up permitting and grid connections, improve coordination between utilities and developers, and encourage investment before demand materializes. One encouraging example is the EU’s recent provisional agreement to streamline permitting for grid projects.

Equally important is to invest in ways to use existing assets more efficiently. Grid-enhancing technologies can speed up connections by allowing existing power lines to carry more electricity, reducing the need to build new infrastructure. And increasingly, governments and utilities are seeing the importance of making grids more flexible—building in capability and strategies to balance supply with demand in real time. Brazil, for example, pays large electricity users to temporarily reduce consumption when the grid is under strain. Businesses that fail to deliver the agreed reductions can lose payments or face suspension from programs. By combining energy storage, demand-response programs, smart grid controls, distributed energy generation, and other flexibility strategies, energy planners can meet growing demands and increase resilience while reducing the need to build more grid infrastructure.

Courtesy of WRI. By Sarah George, Senior Manager, Strategic Communications, WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition; and Francisco Boshell, Global Lead, Decarbonized Energy Consumption, WRI Polsky Center for the Global Energy Transition.

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. #5635. Yozma IN 11 Review — Offroad Dirt Biking with 4800W Peak Power

As a teen, I loved riding — anything, my skateboard, my bike, whatever had wheels and I could get moving on, I was there for it. Then I spent a few years racing mountain bikes in the NORBA circuit when I was in my mid-20’s. I was never very good — always pretty mediocre, in fact, but I’ll be damned if it wasn’t some of the funnest experiences I’ve ever had. The feel of a trail, the woods, cruising through nature … it’s intoxicating. It always came with a lot of work — mountain biking pre e-bikes was a frikkin workout. Pedaling a bike up a combined 3,000 feet worth of climbing during a race would knock me out for days. If only I was in my teen years or 20s now — LOL.

With bikes like the Yozma IN 11, you can cruise mountain trails at speeds up to 45 mph, and do it completely powered by solar energy, to boot. Compared with noisy, smelly gas bikes, the Yozma can cruise on sunshine and offers the quintessential instant torque of the all-electric motor, making racing competitions with gas bikes an absolute joke — the Yozma will crush them.

Check out the lineup of Yozma bikes here.

The Yozma combines some impressive stats into one good looking dirt bike capable of handling a lot of challenging terrain:

It also has a number of other features, including wheelie assist, reverse gear and high visibility brake lights.

I think one of the things I’m most floored by is the price. My full suspension mountain bike I used to pedal up hills in the mountains of southwest Virginia as a kid cost about $1500. So does this all-electric dirt bike capable of going 44 mph and helping me do assisted wheelies to impress the ladies. I mean … wtf.

#5630. How Can Energy Improve Lives? Why Counting Connections Isn’t Enough

Global tracking of energy access should expand beyond connections to include affordability, reliability, adequacy, and development outcomes.

Access to energy is a cornerstone of development—it can improve health, education, incomes, and overall quality of life.

But access is only the first step. A health clinic can’t perform surgery if frequent power cuts make its equipment unreliable. An electric irrigation pump is unlikely to raise a farmer’s income if crops can’t reach buyers. And a small business may gain little from electricity if it lacks financing to buy the equipment it needs to expand.

For energy to improve people’s lives, it must be affordable, sufficient, and reliable—and people and businesses must be able to put it to productive use.

The United Nations Sustainable Development Goal 7 (SDG 7) calls for “universal access to affordable, reliable, and modern energy services” by 2030. But the two indicators used to track its universal-access target focus only on whether people have access to electricity and whether they primarily rely on clean fuels and technologies. They do not address reliability, affordability, or economic impacts.

Other approaches already offer a fuller picture. The World Bank’s Energy Sector Management Assistance Program developed the Multi-Tier Framework to measure electricity access beyond connections, including whether electricity is available when needed, adequate for the equipment people want to use, reliable and affordable. While the framework is used by some countries to assess these broader dimensions of energy access, such measures are not the default, as many countries rely on the U.N.’s official global indicators.

As governments consider what should follow the SDGs after 2030, they should make multidimensional energy-access measures a regular part of national surveys and use the results to track progress globally. Since access alone does not necessarily deliver better livelihoods, improved measures of access could also be paired with indicators of whether energy is delivering the development outcomes it is intended to support. These could include whether clinics and schools can function as intended, farmers can increase yields and incomes and businesses can grow revenues and employment.

Together, these measures could shift energy planning from the largely supply-side question of how many people have access toward the demand-side questions of what people and institutions need energy for and whether the energy system enables them to meet these needs.

The examples below show why looking beyond connections matters in practice.

National electrification rates can mask large differences in where electricity is available within a country. Addressing those gaps requires looking beyond national averages to identify the households, businesses, and public facilities that lack access.

In 2023, WRI, working closely with Uganda’s Ministry of Health, used WRI’s Energy Access Explorer (EAE)—an online mapping tool that combines energy data with information on infrastructure, public services, and economic activity—to assess the electricity needs of health facilities. The analysis found that 675 facilities serving more than 3 million people had no electricity, either grid or off-grid, yet all were in areas with significant solar energy potential. The analysis helped identify places where solar power could fill the energy gap.

In Ethiopia, the Ministry of Water and Energy and the World Bank used EAE to help design a financing program for off-grid electricity. The analysis identified 145 priority woredas, or districts, where investments could reach more than 19 million people. Having this location-based data helped translate national energy-access goals into decisions about where public financing should be directed.

Ethiopia also illustrates why simple access figures can overstate the level of service people receive. A 2025 national energy-access survey found that 65% of households had access to at least one source of electricity. However, just 44% of the population received at least a basic level of electricity service, enough for uses such as lighting and charging a phone for at least four hours a day.

Simply having an electricity connection is not enough if the supply lacks the capacity or voltage stability to operate the equipment people need.

In the Gumla district in Jharkhand, India, villages that had gained household grid connections still lacked enough power for many businesses to function. WRI found that the single-phase electricity supplied was insufficient for productive uses such as milling and oil pressing. Beginning in 2015, Mlinda Charitable Trust, a nonprofit, installed solar mini-grids that supplied three-phase electricity, allowing local enterprises to run equipment, including rice and wheat hullers, oil expellers, flour mills, and pulverizers.

Governments should assess not only whether communities are connected but also whether the electricity supplied can meet the capacity and power-quality requirements of the services and equipment people need—and plan grid upgrades or complementary systems accordingly.

A connection provides limited benefit if households, businesses, or public facilities cannot pay for enough energy to meet their needs. Measuring meaningful access therefore also means asking whether the energy is affordable for the people and institutions expected to use it.

In Ethiopia’s Berbere district, farmers initially used petrol-powered pumps to irrigate their fields, but high fuel and maintenance costs meant these were too expensive for many to keep using. Petrol cost $91 to $203 per hectare a year, while repairs and the cost of transporting fuel added further expenses.

WRI and the Danish nonprofit DanChurchAid later installed two solar-powered pumps serving 48 farming households. The solar systems have no fuel costs, while maintenance costs are less than $10 a year, compared with more than $1,300 for petrol pumps. But the two pumps’ combined $19,200 upfront cost was beyond what most farmers could afford out of pocket. Governments and funders should therefore assess both the ongoing cost of using energy and the upfront cost of the technologies needed to use it, and provide financing or targeted support where high initial costs remain a barrier.

Being connected to the grid does not mean electricity will be available whenever it is needed. WRI researchers documented power cuts lasting three to 12 hours a day at two health facilities in Jharkhand, with outages worsening during the monsoon season. The facilities relied on diesel generators and small battery backups during outages.

In 2017, after solar photovoltaic systems and larger batteries were installed, the facilities were able to keep wards and offices running and power other essential equipment during grid outages, reducing their reliance on grid electricity.

Tracking the frequency and duration of outages would give a clearer picture of whether electricity is dependable enough to support essential services.

Even when energy is available, adequate, affordable, and reliable, it may not translate into better livelihoods. People also need equipment, financing, and access to markets to put energy to productive use.

In Uganda, WRI Africa and partners used EAE to identify $15 million in potential investments in renewable energy technologies, including solar-powered irrigation systems and equipment for cooling and milling, that could benefit more than 28,000 farmers. Some organizations identified through that work have since secured funding for related projects. Masaka District Farmers Association, for example, received an IKI Small Grants award for an irrigation project that equipped 150 hectares of coffee-growing land with drip irrigation and worked with 1,500 smallholder farmers.

In Berbere, solar-powered irrigation has enabled farmers to harvest three to four times a year and grow higher-value crops. In their first season, many farmers earned four to 10 times as much from onion, pepper, and cabbage sales as they had previously earned in a year from staple crops.

Tracking these outcomes alongside measures of energy access can help show whether investments are translating into higher productivity and incomes.

Making greater use of existing multidimensional measures, alongside complementary indicators of development outcomes, could change not just how governments report progress but also how they plan and direct investment.

Rather than starting with how many new connections a project can deliver, governments and funders would ask what people and institutions need energy for, what prevents them from using it effectively, and which investments could remove those barriers. In some places, that may mean extending electricity supply. In others, it could mean improving reliability, lowering costs, or helping farmers and businesses obtain the equipment and financing needed to use energy productively.

Energy is essential to improving human well-being, but it is only one part of what drives development. Like water, health care or education and energy is necessary but not sufficient. Treating access as a starting point rather than an end goal—and measuring whether people can use, afford, and rely on the energy they receive—would give governments a clearer picture of whether energy investments are improving people’s lives.

Courtesy of WRI. Experts: Dimitris Mentis, Harsha Meenawat

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

#5629. New Jersey utilities have less than a year to implement virtual power plants

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Data from the two-year interim version of PowerUp NJ will inform the development of a long-term, open-access virtual power plant tariff, state utility regulators said on Nov. 12.

PowerUp NJ is a “first-of-its-kind statewide virtual power plant program that will give ratepayers more control to drive down their own energy costs,” Sherrill said in a statement that also referred to the plan as a “voluntary community energy program” that allows customers to “lend power from their homes” to support the grid.

The BPU’s order this month cements the program features and goals outlined in a July straw proposal from the agency.

Among other things, the order envisions a market-based framework that compensates distributed energy resources for grid services, rather than subsidizing their deployment; has standard program design elements across all four regulated utility territories; is technology neutral, with eligibility extended to net-metered solar-plus-storage systems; and requires utilities to test localized dispatch with an eye towards developing grid services that could defer or avoid infrastructure upgrades, according to a program factsheet.

The order also establishes technical working groups on measurement and verification, cost-benefit analysis, and program marketing; and aims to coordinate existing utility and state programs and proceedings involving distribution system planning, net metering, energy efficiency and electric vehicles, according to the factsheet.

The BPU expects smart thermostats, stationary home batteries, internet-connected electric water heaters and vehicle chargers and electric vehicles themselves to be eligible for PowerUp NJ at launch. Participating utilities and manufacturers will notify customers of specific eligible devices and models in mid-2027.

PowerUp NJ will be an opt-in program, meaning customers must affirmatively choose to participate. Once enrolled, customers will be able to “choose which devices and events to include” to avoid inconvenient or uncomfortable situations, like ceding control of a thermostat on a hot day, the BPU said.

Like other state-level VPPs, such as California’s Demand Side Grid Support program, PowerUp NJ aims to reduce grid stress and mitigate power price spikes by shifting load away from demand peaks. It will target both “distribution-level grid stress events” and PJM capacity and transmission coincident peaks, according to the program factsheet.

A preliminary staff analysis found that a 3% cut to peak demand could avoid about $60 million in annual system capacity costs while cutting local and greenhouse gas emissions from fossil-fueled peaker plants in New Jersey, the BPU said.

In a statement, BPU President Ben Hertz-Shargel said his agency would apply lessons from the two-year interim version of PowerUp NJ to the development of a long-term framework for the program.

“We will develop this program carefully, learn from real-world results, and expand it over time, growing the earnings opportunities for residential and business customers,” Hertz-Shargel said in a statement.

#5626. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.

#5625. New ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept: This Electric GTI Is Ready For The Racetrack

270 kW (367 PS), top speed of 240 km/h and 4.7 seconds from 0 to 100 km/h: With the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept, Volkswagen is marking the 50th anniversary of the GTI by showing how uncompromising an electric GTI with motorsport ambitions can be. The concept car will be presented to the public next week at the Essen Motor Show (27 November to 6 December 2026). Its performance figures meet a two-door silhouette and flared wings as well as a large front splitter, diffuser and rear wing. The result: a powerful appearance and maximum downforce for fast laps. At the same time, the concept vehicle provides a preview of how the GTI idea can be further developed in the electric age.

Kai Grünitz, Member of the Board of Management for Technical Development, Volkswagen Brand and Brand Group Core: “The GTI has been one of Volkswagen’s great success stories for 50 years. This year, we launched our GTI as an electric vehicle in series production for the first time. With the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept, we are showing how much more potential there is in the electric GTI world. Our platform strategy and MEB+ give us the technological freedom to make even extreme concepts possible.”

Designed for performance. The design of the new ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept is based on the production model, the 166 kW (226 PS) ID. Polo GTI. In both models, the drive power is transmitted to the front axle. In the 270 kW (367 PS) ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept, this power delivery is controlled – thanks in part to an electronically regulated front differential lock (VAQ) – so that the model reaches 100 km/h from a standstill in just 4.7 seconds with Launch Control. Like the drive system, the electrical infrastructure has also been adapted to the high performance of the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept. The 52 kWh high-voltage battery can provide up to 290 kW at short notice, enabling a top speed of 240 km/h. The DCC (adaptive chassis control) has also been tuned for use on the racetrack and ensures precise handling and high traction in conjunction with the front differential lock. The new ceramic brakes of the one-box brake system are also designed for high performance.

Dynamics are visible in the design. On the exterior, the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept follows the powerful clarity of the ID. Polo GTI production model, while at the same time incorporating design elements that underline the motorsport characteristics. Particularly striking features are the two-door instead of four-door silhouette, pronounced flaring of the wings by 50 millimetres in each case, a large motorsport splitter at the front, a wider rear end, a new diffuser and a rear wing in carbon fibre that protrudes above the roof line.

Two-seater. Reduced to racing. The interior is also tailored to the racetrack, and only the basic layout of the overall cockpit has been adopted from the production model. This means there is no infotainment system, for example. The focus is solely on ergonomics and driving. The driver and front passenger sit on Recaro racing bucket seats with red 5-point harnesses. The rear and luggage compartment have been completely cleared. Instead of a rear seat system, a rollover bar painted in the GTI colour Tornado Red is integrated above and behind the front seats. The dash panel, side trims and many other details are covered in dark ArtVelours microfibre fabric with red topstitching – work that has been carried out with great precision by Volkswagen trainees. In many areas – in keeping with Volkswagen tradition – the young employees were directly involved in realisation of the concept vehicle by the Development department and designers.

550 instead of 290 newton-metres. The ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept features the high-torque APP550 motor. The special technical challenge here: this motor is actually intended for the rear of large models such as the ID. Tiguan or ID.7. It therefore had to be fitted into the smaller ID. Polo as front-wheel drive and additionally combined with an electronic front differential lock. Thomas Schmall, Member of the Board of Management Volkswagen AG for Technology, and CEO of Volkswagen Group Components: “The ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept shows just how emotive electric mobility can be: This is made possible by our APP550, one of the most efficient electric drives in its class. Manufactured over one million times at the Group Components plant in Kassel, this drive was actually developed for larger vehicle classes. Integrating it into the Polo required millimetre precision and is a powerful example of engineering skill and teamwork at Volkswagen, from Development and Design through to our trainees who worked on the project.”

GTI from the master of modular platforms. The ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept is based on the modular electric drive (MEB+) platform with front-wheel drive. Volkswagen has used the basic technical layout of the production model and taken it to a new level of performance with a 104 kW (141 PS) more powerful electric drive. The result is the most dynamic interpretation of a front-wheel drive ID. model to date. At the other end of the scale is the Mission Efficiency, which is also based on the ID. Polo and set four world records as a particularly efficient near-production electric vehicle. Both concept vehicles show the large bandwidth offered by electric drive systems from maximum sportiness to outstanding efficiency.

2026 is the year of the GTI. Following the Golf GTI EDITION 50, the new ID.3 GTI (sales start early 2027) and the new ID. Polo GTI (available to order since October), the ID. Polo GTI Clubsport Concept is making its debut this year as the fourth new model under the umbrella of the iconic GTI product brand. No other compact sports car family has been as successful worldwide for half a century now – with more than 2.5 million units sold – as the GTI models.

#5624. DOJ closes NextEra-Dominion antitrust probe as state hearings continue

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The decision followed a promise by the utilities to allow ISO-NE’s internal market monitor to investigate proposed plant retirements.

In a July filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, Dominion said the merger needs approval from FERC, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission, the SCC, the North Carolina Utilities Commission and the Public Service Commission of South Carolina.

The SCC has been holding daily evidentiary hearings since Nov. 17, while South Carolina and North Carolina regulators have scheduled theirs to begin in December and February, respectively. Neither FERC nor the NRC have issued a decision.

Dominion and NextEra announced in a Sept. 14 release that they would double a proposed monthly residential bill credit for Virginia customers from two to four years.

In that release, the companies also said they would accelerate solar, storage and other resources in Virginia to “affordably power the Commonwealth’s growth,” advance the Virginia Clean Economy Act and deliver “long-term affordability benefits by leveraging the combined company’s scale to buy, build, finance and operate more efficiently.”

Last week, SCC regulators and attorneys for the state pressed witnesses, including Baine, NextEra CEO John Ketchum and Dominion Energy CEO Bob Blue, on how customer benefits could be calculated and ensured.

Baine said that when Dominion merged with SCANA in 2019, it saw “benefits of scale” where input cost was lowered, adding, “We believe it will be on a much bigger scale with this merger, but just can't do the math for you today.”

John Farmer, a senior assistant attorney general for Virginia, pointed Ketchum to a written response from Dominion and NextEra in which they said they “have not prepared a year-by-year quantification of benefits to Dominion Energy Virginia's existing customers.”

Ketchum said he stands behind “the fact that we've offered up the bill credits” and broadly outlined the potential operational benefits of a combined company.

“Shame on us if we can't realize the scale benefits that I've spoken about,” Ketchum said in Wednesday’s hearing. “That’s what we do.”

SCC Chair Kelsey Bagot asked Blue how he would ensure that “potential issues do not erode the benefits” that are “possible” as a result of the merger.

“Have you started developing a plan?” Bagot said.

Blue said that if the merger isn’t managed well, “it is possible that the longer-term benefits that we're describing won't materialize as quickly.”

“As I mentioned in my answer to you before, I don't believe that would leave our customers worse off,” he continued. “There may be some missed opportunities.”

Some intervenors also criticized the bill credit proposal.

Bloom Advisory Partners partner Teresa Ringenbach testified on behalf of the Retail Energy Advancement League that the bill credit structure “effectively penalizes customers for choosing a competitive supplier,” which she said “is discriminatory and undermines the statutorily mandated retail choice framework.”

“A large general service customer using 72,000 megawatt hours per year would receive approximately $225,000 if it shops, compared to approximately $642,000 if it does not,” Ringenbach said. “That difference is a price signal, and it’s funded by the utility's new parent, and it rewards remaining on the utility's generation service.”

“Shareholder credits should not be viewed as the price of getting a merger approved,” she added. “They should deliver real value to every single wires customer.”

Bagot asked Richard McCann, an energy expert testifying on behalf of Gov. Abigail Spanberger’s office, “Do we have the ability to provide rate credits to a single rate class? For example … only to residential customers and not to any of the other rate classes.”

“I recognize that's not exactly what you're proposing,” Bagot added, “but it does shift potentially what that rate credit would be, maybe closer to $29 per month.”

Raising the credit to $29 a month was a condition McCann proposed in his written testimony, along with a six-year residential rate freeze.

In response to Bagot, McCann said, “I know that at $10, they won't notice if it phases out or not. $30 a month, they might notice that the credit disappears if it's a sudden shock.”

He said that implementing the higher credit for residential ratepayers alongside other methods he proposed — such as raising the debt share of Dominion's capital structure — could lower bills over time and provide a “smooth glide path” for the end of the credits.

SCC Commissioner Jehmal Hudson pushed back on the rate freeze proposal, asking McCann what authority allows the SCC to “freeze future base rate increases irrespective of the future costs” and noting that rate freezes in Virginia “have primarily stemmed from the General Assembly legislation.”

“You are in an extraordinary position right now with this merger,” McCann said. “There are certain things that you have discretion about going forward — you will have to decide if you have that discretion.”

#5623. energyRe Agreed to Sell 100% of a Portfolio of Utility-Scale Solar Projects in South Carolina to Enel Green Power North America, Totaling 317 MWdc

energyRe Will Continue as Construction Manager Through Commercial Operation; Sale Reflects Strength of the Company’s South Carolina Development Platform; Transaction Expected to Close in First Quarter of 2027

energyRe today announced that it has agreed to sell 100% of a portfolio of utility-scale solar projects in South Carolina to Enel Green Power North America, Inc. (EGPNA). The projects have a combined total capacity of roughly 317 MWdc of new generating capacity across the Palmetto State. The transaction is expected to close in the first quarter of 2027, subject to customary conditions, including required U.S. regulatory approvals.

“This transaction is a strong validation of the projects we have built in South Carolina and of the team that developed them,” said Ryan Brown, Chief Operating Officer at energyRe. “Enel Green Power is a world-class owner and operator, and we are pleased to hand these projects to a partner that shares our commitment to delivering reliable, affordable power to South Carolina homes and businesses. Our team will remain on the ground as construction manager to see these projects through to commercial operation.”

As part of the transaction, energyRe will continue to serve as construction manager through commercial operation.

energyRe has broken ground on approximately 450 MW in the last year and has nearly 700 MW in operation or late-stage development. The company’s 1.8+ GW portfolio spans 20 projects in South Carolina, North Carolina, Nebraska, and Oklahoma.

#5622. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5621. JA Energy Storage Launches an Internal Battery Passport Platform to Facilitate Compliance with EU Standards

JJA ESS has launched its own battery passport data platform to support lifecycle information management and help companies prepare for upcoming EU regulations. The platform is hosted in the company’s own cloud environment, giving the team direct responsibility for data policies, access permissions, and future updates.

According to the EU Battery Regulation, from February 18, 2027, battery passports are mandatory for batteries used in light vehicles (LMTs), electric vehicles, and industrial batteries with a capacity exceeding 2 kWh that are placed on the market or put into service. Further requirements will follow.

A battery passport is a digital data record linked to a single physical battery. A QR code and a unique identifier based on the ISO/IEC 15459 series connect the data record to the battery, enabling access to information throughout its entire lifecycle. This can include product identity, material composition, CO₂ footprint, recycled content, performance and durability, condition, supply chain due diligence, and end-of-life disposal. Taken together, these data points support consistent information management from product identification to the end of the product lifecycle.

For manufacturers, the challenge lies not simply in creating a QR code. The data must be traceable back to its source, up-to-date, and accessible in accordance with regulatory and business requirements. Companies must also manage access to sensitive information and comply with regulations governing cross-border data transfers and trade secrets. This balance is particularly relevant for companies operating in multiple markets. When companies utilize a third-party platform, its functionality, access to data fields, and interoperability can be determined by the provider’s configuration and service model.

JA ESS developed the platform internally and hosts it in its own cloud environment. Data policies, access permissions, and future platform updates are managed by the JA ESS internal team. This team ensures compliance with EU regulations regarding data access, presentation, and updates, while also giving JA ESS greater control over data management. The platform can also evolve as regulatory requirements change and real-world applications provide further insights.

JA ESS is the energy storage business unit under the JA brand. As a central component of JA’s energy storage strategy, JA ESS offers a wide range of FTM and BTM applications with safe, efficient, intelligent, and customized solutions for the large-scale, commercial, and industrial sectors as well as the residential market.

#5618. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5616. Off-grid costs will push data centers back to utilities: Mitsubishi Heavy Industries CEO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

CEO Eisaku Ito expressed confidence in utilities’ long-term expansion plans, arguing that growing demand for electricity does not rely solely on data centers.

Despite the recent attention on new electric demand created by data centers, Mitsubishi Heavy Industries sees greater potential for long-term growth in the utilities sector, MHI President and CEO Eisaku Ito said earlier this month.

Utilities and independent power producers accounted for 94% of Mitsubishi Heavy Industries’ new gas turbine orders in 2024 and 2025, Ito said.

Although many U.S. data center operators say they intend to install their own off-grid generation, Ito argued that these plans rely on smaller gas-fired power plants that cost more to run than the larger plants utilities and IPPs are pursuing.

In the long-term, Ito said, the cost differential will push data centers to connect to the grid, ultimately supporting utilities' expansion plans.

His comments, made during a call with analysts on Nov. 6, according to a translated transcript, come as fallout from the U.S. midterm elections and stock market volatility raise questions about the future of the artificial intelligence buildout in North America. Ito expressed confidence in utilities' long-term expansion plans, arguing that growing demand for electricity does not rely solely on data centers.

“Electricity demand from data centers is expected to grow rapidly in the United States, but data centers still account for only around 10% of the country's total electricity demand,” Ito said. “If some of the anticipated growth in demand does not materialize, there will be some impact, but we believe the effect on our overall GTCC business would be limited.”

MHI booked 8 GW in orders for large frame gas turbines in the first half of its 2026 fiscal year, compared to 16 GW of orders in all of FY 2025. Profit for the company’s Energy Systems segment rose 80% on account of increased orders and shifts in the value of the yen, according to the company's financial statements.

“We are also beginning to see the benefits of improved pricing on new unit projects, lower loss-related costs and greater production efficiency,” MHI CFO Hiroshi Nishio said.

Company executives said they continue to take a conservative approach to forecasting demand and expanding manufacturing capacity. The company expects global demand for gas turbines at all manufacturers and makers to average about 70 GW per year over the next five years — somewhat lower than the 100 GW MHI expects will sell across all manufactuers this year amid what Ito characterized as “exceptionally strong” demand he does not anticipate will continue.

But Ito said he remains confident in MHI’s company backlog, noting that the company vets new orders carefully and hasn’t had any order cancellations in the past two years.

The company plans to double its gas turbine production capacity by 2030, though most of the additional capacity will come from improved processes and automation at existing plants in the U.S. and Japan.

“We are improving productivity and driving other initiatives at a pace that outstrips the growth in orders,” he said.

MHI also signaled that it's exploring opportunities to expand into other infrastructure-related markets.

Ito said MHI will release additional details in May, but suggested the company is interested in expanding its gas turbine service and repair capabilities, as well as investing in unmanned systems that can operate in harsh environments. At the same time, MHI plans to divest some of its aerospace business, he said.

“These examples may seem quite different, but they all share a common theme: resilience,” Ito said. “We aim to identify new business opportunities in infrastructure and industrial machinery through the lens of resilience and turn them into future growth.”

#5615. Jackery & Every Electric Transform Urban Energy Storage With Plug-In VPP

Earlier this week, Jackery announced a strategic partnership with Brooklyn-based consumer energy aggregator Every Electric to offer the first plug-in Virtual Power Plant (VPP). The program starts with New York City apartment residents. I went to the RE+ in Las Vegas to meet with leaders from Jackery and Every Electric to learn more.

The plug-in VPP costs customers nothing but earns them money by turning their household appliances into grid stabilizing assets. For the customer, the process is simple:

That’s it. As Every Electric puts it: “Get paid to plug in.” Alternatively, a customer can buy the batteries for a discounted price through the program of $650 and receive a higher payback per year, up to $250 per battery. Over the 11-year rated lifespan, purchasing the battery could increase the total net payback, although the upfront cost would be a little higher.

It seems almost too simple and convenient for the customer. However, a lot of complex work and coordination is happening behind the scenes. The Jackery battery is in communication with Every Electric, who automates power management to charge when electricity demand is low and discharge to run the air conditioner when electricity demand is high. This kind of active demand response can go far beyond the typical TOU plan to directly mitigate utility demand shifts. Every Electric monitors data for each battery. The demand response of the combined storage across thousands of individual batteries is aggregated and sold to the utility, in this case Con Edison. Then the benefits are divided up and distributed to customers based on their contributions. Over 2000 batteries were in the initial trial phase, with a larger scale rollout planned next year. I am already on the waitlist, along with over 15,000 other NYC households.

Because it does not feed electricity back into the grid, implementation is much simpler. No electrician needed. The system monitors the amount of energy being stored and deployed at the point of use with no need for user intervention. Unlike traditional demand response that turns off appliances, there is no inconvenience to the user. Thermostats do not need to be adjusted. Smart appliances are not needed. The battery simply provides power when grid demand is high, shaving off the peaks.

You may still be asking “what’s the catch?” Currently, the system needs to coordinate a large number of relatively small batteries under the same utility to have enough combined capacity to sell to the utility. To start, it is only being offered in New York City, which sets the blueprint. In the initial application, the batteries will be used with 120V air conditioners, up to around 12,000 BTU. This is a common size for NYC apartments, letting one battery size serve a wide range of customers, simplifying initial rollout. It also offers clear return on investment based on how much electricity is used, when that electricity is used and when demand peaks for the utility. However, I wouldn’t expect it to stop there.

The batteries being used are led by the Jackery Explorer 2000 v2. This battery has 2042 Wh of capacity and up to 2200 W of output with 4400 W of surge power. A total of three outlets are on the front panel, as well as two USB-C and a USB-A port. Due to the UPS function, the portable power station adds protection and would be ideal to plug in a computer or sensitive electronics, in addition to the air conditioner. Although the VPP is based on air conditioner use, using all the outlets can potentially add to the amount of load shifted by the VPP and, as a result, increase the amount of payback for the customer. Plugging in other forms of energy consumption could also extend the benefit beyond the times of year when the air conditioner is being used.

At 40% smaller and 34% lighter than the previous generation model, the Jackery Explorer 2000 v2 weighs 39.5 lb. That’s less than a 5-gallon water cooler bottle but less bulky to carry. Due to the plug and play nature, people changing apartments within the city can easily take it with them at 13.2” x 10.4” x 11.5”. Multiple levels of safety protections and a ruggedized portable design using automotive-grade LFP batteries ensure safe operation even if apartment occupants bump into it.

That portability also contributes to the emergency preparedness capability of the battery. For the months of the year when the air conditioning isn’t running, the battery could provide backup power for a refrigerator in a power outage, for example. Of course, Jackery also offers batteries specifically for refrigerators. Those batteries are not a part of the initial program… yet.

This also gets to a limitation of the current program. To keep the program manageable as it scales up, only one size of battery in one use case is initially being offered. Right now, the biggest challenge is scaling up. Making it too complicated at the start would make scaling up more difficult. However, once the program reaches scale, more options can become available. Jackery also offers batteries with greater aesthetic appeal, like the slim FridgeGuard, which may be a better fit for the homes of some customers. As the program scales up, the applications have the potential to diversify.

Much of RE+ focused on supplying power and storage for data centers. While residential clean energy tax credits (25D) have been cut, data centers can still benefit from the Clean Electricity Investment Tax Credit that can add up with bonuses to cover half the cost of the project. With additional state and federal incentives piled on top, the majority of the cost of data center ESS can be covered by subsidies. With the heavily subsidized storage and large-scale contracts with the utility, data centers can access benefits not typically available to residential customers.

However, the plug-in VPP changes the energy storage dynamic. People get credit for energy storage deployment at the point of use. AI technology, rather than just being a consumer of resources, is being used to automate the VPP system and let the benefits flow to more families. By starting with small window air conditioners, the system also tends to provide relief to people living in typically older apartments, rather than the sleek new high rises with multi-million-dollar apartments.

There is no need for home electrical upgrades. In fact, by taking some of the load off the circuit, the potential for my wife to trip the breaker from ironing in the living room while the TV and air conditioner are running on the same breaker is reduced. As the energy monitoring comes directly from the battery, it also isn’t dependent on smart meters.

For the system to have enough total capacity to sell to the utility, the plug-in VPP needs to connect thousands of batteries to provide multiple MWh of storage. This scale can easily be accomplished in urban areas. In addition, the value of the system will heavily depend on the local price of electricity and the variability in price. In NYC, electricity is significantly more expensive than in most of the US.

New York City apartments are a perfect starting place. While apartments tend to be far more energy efficient than detached houses and have lower net emissions overall, many of us living in apartments have traditionally felt a bit left out on clean tech. We don’t have roofs for rooftop panels. We typically do not have attached garages to place large permanent batteries or access to modify the electrical panel. If we do have the ability to make electrical modifications to the apartment, they can be prohibitively expensive in NYC. As such, a plug-in VPP is an exciting development for those of us living in apartments.

A plug-in VPP also makes the benefits of battery energy storage more accessible to urban areas. Often, there is not room for container-sized batteries. In places like New York City, the fire regulations are so strict, and the permitting is so complicated that large-scale BESS becomes exceedingly difficult to execute. Large-scale BESS, if approved, could take years to deploy.

According to NREL, the national average full installed price of utility scale BESS was $334/kWh of capacity in 2024, with hardware making up roughly two thirds of the total. However, the cost would undoubtedly be higher in NYC. For comparison, the discounted Explorer 2000 v2 in this program (which avoids retail markup) comes in at $318.32/kWh, with no added permitting or installation cost. That is also significantly less than the installed cost of many home batteries used in traditional VPPs, like Tesla Powerwall. Once delivered, it can be online within minutes.

Currently, plug-in VPP is the fastest, easiest and least expensive way to add battery capacity that shaves peak demand on urban grids in the US.

As high draw usage like air conditioning increases, more stress is placed on the grid. Transformer and other electricity transmission and distribution infrastructure upgrades can be expensive. Having storage at the point of use reduces the amount of electricity that needs to flow through the grid during peak periods. As plug-in VPP is deployed more broadly, the benefits will be experienced more broadly. And CO2 emissions can drop in the process.

While the benefits of the program are already attractive, the initial program may just be the tip of the iceberg. In New York City, buildings over 25,000 square feet must reduce their greenhouse gas emissions under Local Law 97 (LL97). This includes many apartment complexes and offices. The deployment of batteries at the point of use has the potential to reduce emissions.

While the current batteries and air conditioners in the program are 120V, Jackery also makes batteries that are 240V. For appliances that have intermittent use but high draw, like heat pumps, hot water heaters, ranges, etc., 240V connections are often needed to provide the necessary power. The electrification of applications that were previously run on gas or fuel oil can greatly reduce building emissions. But many apartment buildings do not have 240V outlets available. However, larger Jackery models can charge on 120V, accumulate charge and discharge at 240V for intermittent use. There is potential for batteries to support the higher voltage electrification without expensive electrical upgrades while also providing VPP benefit. The math would have to work out and it hasn’t happened yet. But the potential is clearly there.

Overall, the potential applications for this approach are massive. Already, I can see the potential to transform energy storage in NYC. In talking with Jackery and Every Electric, I started thinking about where the technology could go from here. Plug-in VPP could apply far beyond just New York City. However, that is for a follow-up article.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5613. Insulin Resistance by Age 30 Quite Telling of Future Heart Health

— Metabolic syndrome by age 50 marks next stage of a bad trajectory

Insulin resistance in young adulthood was a predictor of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD) years down the line in middle age, according to a CARDIA cohort analysis.

There were more than 3,000 CARDIA participants who remained under observation and free of ASCVD through year 20 (when they were 38-50 years old), with one in four having shown insulin resistance at baseline (at age 18-30), reported a group led by Chuyue Wu, MD, PhD, of the UCLA Fielding School of Public Health in Los Angeles.

With repeated clinical assessments and long-term follow-up, these individuals with insulin resistance in early adulthood had a significantly higher risk of ASCVD events through year 35 of the study, when they were 53-65 years old (risk ratio [RR] 1.50, 95% CI 1.13-2.01).

"These findings have potential implications for early cardiovascular prevention by suggesting that IR [insulin resistance] in young adulthood may signal an adverse CKM [cardiovascular-kidney-metabolic] trajectory before conventional cardiometabolic risk factors become fully established," Wu and colleagues wrote in JACC: Advances.

Of note, fasting insulin is not routinely measured in clinical practice. In the study, insulin resistance was indirectly measured as a Homeostatic Model Assessment of Insulin Resistance (HOMA-IR) value at or above the 75th percentile (≥2.67) after a 12-hour fast -- which may not exactly correspond to the gold-standard method of using the hyperinsulinemic-euglycemic clamp.

The researchers also found that 63.4% of the insulin resistance-later ASCVD relationship was mediated by midlife metabolic syndrome at the year-20 mark (indirect RR 1.29, 95% CI 1.13-1.48).

"The substantial mediation through midlife MetS [metabolic syndrome] further identifies the clustering of abnormalities in adiposity, blood pressure, glucose, and lipids as a potentially important intermediate stage linking early IR to later ASCVD," Wu's group suggested. "These findings highlight midlife MetS as an important marker of adverse CKM progression among individuals with IR earlier in adulthood."

Study authors acknowledged that they had not tried to account for changes in metabolic syndrome status and its individual components before or after year 20. Residual confounding is also possible given the study's observational nature.

"Although the findings support closer cardiometabolic surveillance among young adults with IR, further research is needed to determine whether HOMA-IR improves risk prediction beyond routinely measured clinical factors, develop practical approaches for identifying IR when fasting insulin is unavailable, and test whether preventing or delaying progression to MetS reduces subsequent ASCVD risk," Wu and colleagues urged.

One interpretation of the results is that "insulin resistance may represent an early signal of an adverse cardiometabolic trajectory, whereas MetS may mark a subsequent stage of risk-factor clustering," according to an accompanying editorial by Amier Haidar, MD, MPH, of McGovern Medical School, University of Texas Health Science Center at Houston, and Leonardo Roever, MHS, PhD, of the Brazilian Evidence-Based Health Network in Uberlândia, Brazil.

"For current practice, the most important implication is therefore not a new laboratory test. It is a new sense of urgency," the duo stressed. "A young adult with progressively increasing abdominal adiposity, worsening blood pressure, rising triglycerides, and deteriorating glycemic measures should not need to accumulate multiple abnormalities before clinicians recognize the direction of travel."

"Young adulthood represents a particularly important and underused window for prevention," the duo asserted. "Young adulthood is therefore not a cardiovascular waiting room; it is a period when cardiometabolic trajectories are being established."

The study included 3,622 participants in CARDIA, a multicenter, population-based, prospective cohort study. Included in this analysis were those who were enrolled in 1985-1986 as young adults (mean age at baseline 25.0 years, 45% men, 47% Black) who remained free of ASCVD through year 20.

A person was deemed to have metabolic syndrome if they met three of these criteria: waist circumference ≥102 cm in men or ≥88 cm in women; triglycerides ≥150 mg/dL; HDL cholesterol <40 mg/dL in men or <50 mg/dL in women; systolic blood pressure ≥130 mm Hg, diastolic blood pressure ≥85 mm Hg, or the use of antihypertensive medication; and fasting glucose ≥100 mg/dL or the use of glucose-lowering medication.

Over a median follow-up of 14 years after year 20, 3.9% of participants logged ASCVD events.

#5612. Europe’s Modelled €5.70/kg Hydrogen Faces Industrial Buyers Who Can Afford Far Less

Europe’s industrial hydrogen plans have a buyer problem. A factory cannot make its products competitive by paying whatever a new hydrogen plant needs to recover its investment. Its maximum hydrogen price is set by the cost of making the same product, or delivering the same useful heat, another way.

The central 2050 scenario in my European hydrogen assessment puts on-site production at €5.70/kg. That starts with electricity at €60/MWh and 53 kWh of electricity per kilogram of hydrogen, so the electricity alone costs €3.18/kg. Electrolysers, capital recovery, maintenance, stack replacements, water and other plant costs bring the total to €5.70. Getting hydrogen to a customer can cost more.

At a factory using hydrogen for process heat, €5.70/kg is an expensive fuel. With 94% heating efficiency, delivering one MWh of useful heat requires roughly 31.9 kg of hydrogen. The fuel bill reaches €182/MWh before the burner, maintenance and other equipment costs. In the model’s central 2050 comparison, suitable direct-electric heating sets a hydrogen price ceiling of €2.07/kg. At that ceiling the same hydrogen heat costs about €66/MWh. The hydrogen production case overshoots the customer’s competitive input price by €3.63/kg.

The mismatch is not a prediction that hydrogen makers will never lower costs. It is the actual commercial test investors should apply before financing supply. Reducing the cost of production from €7 to €5 a kilogram achieves little if the relevant industrial customer can pay €2. Production-cost targets are meaningless without a corresponding market price ceiling.

The International Energy Agency’s Global Hydrogen Review 2026 has made customer cost acceptability part of its assessment. Across most sector and region comparisons, the IEA finds maximum acceptable hydrogen prices below US$2/kg without policy support. Some incumbent-steel comparisons yield negative acceptable prices. The IEA examines a 2035 horizon and a different set of competitors; the same buyer-side arithmetic exposes the problem.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment calculates the maximum hydrogen input price for industrial heat, ammonia and ironmaking and compares it with distinct European supply cases. It also links to the underlying European hydrogen research and interactive model.

European ammonia illustrates how demand for a product differs from demand for European-made hydrogen. Ammonia remains necessary for fertilisers and industrial chemicals. The buyer’s real choice is where to produce it. In the central 2050 TFIE model, making qualifying green ammonia inside Europe can compete with imported green ammonia only when its hydrogen input costs roughly €2.62–€4.00/kg, depending on the origin and production route. The €5.70/kg onsite supply case misses those thresholds. Europe can keep consuming ammonia without building every electrolyser locally.

Ironmaking has a similar sourcing decision. The model’s hydrogen input-price ceiling versus qualifying imported hydrogen-reduced or hot-briquetted iron ranges from €2.28 to €4.45/kg. Against a European electrochemical iron process, the central ceiling is approximately €1.43/kg. Where enough suitable scrap can deliver the required steel grade, the hydrogen-based route can lose even at a zero price for hydrogen. Steel quality, industrial integration and the commercial maturity of electrochemical iron matter to those comparisons. They do not entitle a hydrogen supplier to ignore the alternatives.

Hydrogen used as a chemical reagent deserves a different assessment from hydrogen burned for heat. Chemical production may need the molecule. Industrial heating needs temperature, pressure, reliability and useful energy, all of which can be delivered directly from electricity for many applications. Manufacturing hydrogen from electricity and then burning it adds conversion losses and fuel-system costs before the plant receives the heat it needs.

The supply cases have defined boundaries. The central onsite cost is €5.70/kg. A generic delivery allowance takes that case to €6.95/kg. The model’s separately configured hub-and-pipeline case is €5.81/kg. Those are different cost structures, not interchangeable price quotes for the same customer. Even the favourable 2050 case, at €3.46/kg with its specified delivery allowance and €28/MWh electricity, clears some industrial thresholds but still misses others.

Policy can create demand that market prices do not support. Carbon pricing, procurement mandates, subsidies and carbon contracts for difference can raise willingness or obligation to pay. That may be a deliberate industrial or climate choice, but governments should disclose the premium, who will finance it and the contract duration. Describing a regulated purchase as competitive demand hides the cost rather than eliminating it.

The EU energy regulators’ market monitoring points in the same direction. ACER reported 4.2 GW of committed electrolyser capacity in November 2026, including operating plants, projects under construction and those with final investment decisions. Roughly 49% was associated with refining, where renewable-fuel mandates provide a strong demand signal. The remaining industrial sectors face a harder test when policy does not compel the same purchases.

For lenders, the consequence is immediate. A proposed hydrogen plant costing €5/kg has no viable commercial case when its intended industrial buyer can afford €3/kg. A memorandum of understanding cannot close that €2/kg gap. Developers need buyers with enforceable contracts at prices that cover supply costs, lower production costs, or identified support durable enough to finance the difference.

Industrial and public procurement should start with the product the customer must deliver, calculate the best eligible competing route, and determine the maximum hydrogen price that route permits. Only then should investors consider electrolysers, pipelines and capacity targets. Suppliers do not set the market by drawing cheaper future cost curves. Industrial customers set the ceiling.

The full TFIE Briefing and open European hydrogen assessment contain the industrial route comparisons, scenario assumptions and research submitted to the European Commission. The next Briefing examines the competing routes for sourcing green ammonia and iron.
